Dynamics of Ginzburg-Landau vortices for vector fields on surfaces
Giacomo Canevari, Antonio Segatti

Abstract
. In this paper we consider the gradient flow of the following Ginzburg-Landau type energy F ε ​ ( u ) := 1 2 ​ ∫ M | D ​ u | g 2 + 1 2 ​ ε 2 ​ ( | u | g 2 − 1 ) 2 ​ vol g . F_{\varepsilon}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|Du\right|_{g}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|u\right|_{g}^{2}-1\right)^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}. This energy is defined on tangent vector fields on a 2 2 -dimensional closed and oriented Riemannian manifold M M (here D D stands for the covariant derivative) and depends on a small parameter ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . If the energy satisfies proper bounds, when ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 the second term forces the vector fields to have unit length. However, due to the incompatibility for vector fields on M M between the Sobolev regularity and the unit norm constraint, critical points of F ε F_{\varepsilon} tend to generate a finite number of singular points (called vortices) having non-zero index (when the Euler characteristic is non-zero). These types of problems have been extensively analyzed in the recent paper by R. Ignat & R. Jerrard [ 19 ] . As in Euclidean case (see, among the others [ 8 ] ), the position of the vortices is ruled by the so-called renormalized energy. In this paper we are interested in the dynamics of vortices. We rigorously prove that the vortices move according to the gradient flow of the renormalized energy, which is the limit behavior when ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 of the gradient flow of the Ginzburg-Landau energy. Keywords: Ginzburg-Landau; Vector fields on surfaces; Gradient flow of the renormalized energy; Γ \Gamma -convergence. MSC: 35Q56 (37E35, 49J45, 58E20).



1.
Introduction and Main Result

A prominent example of the non-trivial interplay between PDEs models, material science,
differential geometry and topology is the the study of the formation and the evolution of the so-called topological defects, that is regions in the material where the order parameter (in this paper, a vector field on a
2
-dimensional Riemannian manifold) experiences a very rapid change.
To put this phenomenon in perspective,
we let
(M,g)
be a closed (i.e. compact, connected and without boundary), oriented,
2
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with Euler characteristic
\chi(M)
.
The classical Poincaré-Hopf Theorem (see
[
12
]
) provides a precise relation between the zeros of continuous vector fields on
M
and the topology of
M
.
More precisely, any continuous vector field
u
with finitely many zeros
\left\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\right\}
must satisfy

(1.1)

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\text{ind}\left(u,\partial B_{\delta}(a_{j})\right)\,=\,\chi(M),

where
\ind(u,\,\partial B_{\delta}(a_{j}))
is a local topological invariant
— the index — computed on the boundary of a small geodesic ball
B_{\delta}(a_{j})
,
with radius
\delta
and center
a_{j}
. The radius
\delta
must be taken small enough,
so that the ball
B_{\delta}(a_{j})
does not contain any zero of
u
other than
a_{j}
.
(For the definition of the index, see Section
4
below.)
An immediate consequence is that a non-vanishing continuous vector field
exists if and only if the Euler characteristic of
M
is equal to zero, namely only if
M
is a torus (up to homeomorphism).
In other words, if
\chi(M)\neq 0
then
M
does not support a globally defined, unit-norm, continuous vector field.
Interestingly, the very same topological obstruction holds for Sobolev vector fields.
More precisely (see
[
12
]
and
[
38
]
), indicating with
D
the Levi-Civita connection, we have that the Sobolev class

(1.2)

\left\{v:M\to TM:v(p)\in T_{p}M,\,\,\left|v\right|=1\,\,\text{a.e. in }M,\,\,\left|Dv\right|_{g}\in L^{2}(M)\right\}\neq\emptyset\Longleftrightarrow\chi(M)=0.

Thus, the Dirichlet-type energy on unit-norm vector fields

(1.3)

F(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|Du\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

is well defined only if the Euler characteristic of
M
is zero.
The energy
F
is interesting both from the geometric and the modellistic point of view.
On the one hand, the energy
F
is usually called
Total Bending
(see
[
44
]
) and (quantitatively) measures how a globally defined unit-norm vector field
u
, if it exists, fails to be a parallel vector field (namely a vector field
u
for which
D_{v}u=0
for any
v
).
On the other hand, the energy
F
is related, for instance, to the modelling of nematic shells, namely a rigid particle coated by a thin film of nematic liquid crystal (see
[
5
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
25
]
,
[
30
]
,
[
41
]
,
[
43
]
) when one neglects the effects of the extrinsic geometry of the substrate on the elastic energy. We refer to
[
27
]
,
[
28
]
and
[
37
]
for models including extrinsic effects.

When
\chi(M)\neq 0
the energy
F\equiv+\infty
, therefore its minimization requires at first a relaxation of at least one of the constraints in the Sobolev class of vector fields in (
1.2
).
Looking back at the definition (
1.2
) we see that there are many ways to relax one of the constraints and obtain
a non-empty class on which the energy
F
is finite.
A common strategy (and the one we will follow in this paper) consists in relaxing the unit norm constraint and studying the asymptotic behavior of the Ginzburg-Landau energy

(1.4)

F_{\varepsilon}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|Du\right|_{g}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|u\right|_{g}^{2}-1\right)^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

This problem has been addressed by Ignat & Jerrard in the recent paper
[
19
]
. Note that this type of approximation is completely intrinsic as it makes no reference to any embedding of the manifold.

There are however other possible strategies to obtain a relaxed version of the energy
F
.
For instance, if our
2
-dimensional manifold is an embedded surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
a possible strategy consists in replacing
M
with a suitable triangulation of
M
and the energy
F
(actually its extrinsic version where the covariant derivative is replaced with the derivative of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) with a discrete energy defined on unit norm vector fields sitting in the vertices of the triangulation of
M
(see
[
13
]
).
This approach, contrary to the Ginzburg-Landau approximation, is extrinsic in the sense that it depends on how the manifold
M
sits in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
However, these two approaches have the common feature of giving information, in a suitable asymptotic regime, about the location, and the charge of the vortices.

Since our work is very much related with the work of R. Jerrard & R. Ignat, we briefly recall some of the main results of
[
19
]
regarding (
1.4
).

Let
i
be an almost
complex structure on
M
, that is,
an operator
TM\to TM
which restricts to a linear map
on each tangent plane
T_{p}M
and satisfies
i^{2}+1=0

(where
1
denotes the identity on
TM
). For any (sufficiently regular)
vector field
u
on
M
, we define the
1
-form

(1.5)

j(u):=(Du,iu)_{g}

and the
2
-form

(1.6)

\omega(u):=\mathrm{d}j(u)+\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
\kappa
is the Gauss curvature of
M
.
The form
\omega(u)
is called the vorticity of
u
.
In the Euclidean setting, when
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
the vorticity
\omega(u)
essentially reduces to the
Jacobian determinant of
u
, i.e.
\omega(u)=2(\det\nabla u)\,\mathrm{d}x
.
As in the Euclidean case, if a sequence
u_{\varepsilon}
satisfies
a logarithmic energy bound such as
F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|

(for some constant
C
that does not depend on
\varepsilon
)
then the sequence of vorticities
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})
is compact.
Controlling the vorticity of
u_{\varepsilon}
is key to
proving a sharp lower bound for the energy of
u_{\varepsilon}

and obtain compactness results for the minimizers of
F_{\varepsilon}
.
However, when the genus
\mathfrak{g}=\frac{2-\chi(M)}{2}\neq 0
, it is
also necessary to control the
L^{2}
-projection of the
1
-form
j(u_{\varepsilon})
on the space of harmonic
1
-forms,
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
.

\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
is a real vector space of dimension
2\mathfrak{g}
.
Let
\mathbb{P}
be the
L^{2}
-projection onto
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
.
Ignat and Jerrard
[
19
]
showed that,
if a sequence of vector field
u_{\varepsilon}
satisfies
suitable energy bounds, then there exist distinct points

a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
in
M
, integers
d_{1}
, …,
d_{n}
,
a form
\xi\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
and a (non-relabelled) subsequence,
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}j(u_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\xi

The integers
d_{j}
represent the charges of
topological singularities which arise at the points
a_{j}
.
They satisfy

\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}=\chi(M).

The harmonic
1
-form
\xi\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
depends non-trivially
on
\mathbf{a}:=\left(a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\right)

and
\mathbf{d}:=(d_{1},\,\ldots,\,d_{n})
, as it must satisfy
the constraint
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

where
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
is a suitable subset of
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

defined by the position and the charges of the singularities.
(See
[
19
, Section 2.1]
and Section
3

for the definition of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
.)
If the
u_{\varepsilon}
’s are minimizers of
F_{\varepsilon}
, then
n=\left|\chi(M)\right|
,

d_{j}=\sign(\chi(M))
for any
j
and

(1.7)

F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)+n\gamma+\mathrm{o}(1)\qquad\textrm{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

Here, the function
W=W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
is the so-called
renormalized energy, which accounts for the interaction between
the singular points
a_{j}
. It can be characterised in terms
of the Green function for the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M

and the Gauss curvature of
M
(see
[
19
, Proposition 2.4]
and section
3
below). When the
u_{\varepsilon}
’s
are minimizers of
F_{\varepsilon}
,
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
minimize the
renormalized energy
W
. The constant
\gamma>0
in (
1.7
)
is the so-called core energy, which accounts for the energy
in a suitably small neighbourhood of each singular point
a_{j}

(we refer to
[
19
, Section 2.3]
for
the definition of
\gamma
— in Ignat and Jerrard’s notation,

\gamma=\iota_{F}
). Moreover, again up to a
to a subsequence, minimizers converge to a unit-norm vector field
u_{*}
,
called a canonical harmonic vector field,
which is smooth in
M\setminus\left\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\right\}

and is uniquely determined by
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
up to a global rotation.
These results represent the extension of the classical two-dimensional results of Bethuel, Brezis and Hélein
[
8
]

to the case of vector fields on a Riemannian manifold (see also
[
2
]
for a

\Gamma
-convergence result).

In this paper, we discuss the dynamics of the vortices on a two dimensional closed and oriented Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
. We show that
the vortices move according to the gradient flow of the renormalized energy.
The gradient flow of the renormalized energy emerges as limit dynamics of the (properly rescaled) gradient flow of the Ginzburg-Landau energy (
1.4
).
More precisely,
we consider the asymptotic behavior when
\varepsilon\to 0
of the Ginzburg Landau dynamics

(1.8)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-1\right)u_{\varepsilon}=0,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ a.e. in }M\times(0,T),\\
\\
\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}(x,0)=u_{0}^{\varepsilon}\,\,\,\,\hbox{ a.e. in }M.\end{cases}

The operator
\Delta_{g}
is the so called rough Laplacian (see (
2.26
) for the definition) while
the factor
\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}
in front of the time derivative of
u_{\varepsilon}
comes from scaling and indicates that the isolated zeros of
u_{\varepsilon}
with nonzero index move with velocities of order
\left|\log\varepsilon\right|^{-1}
.

Our results confirm in the Riemannian setting the classical planar results
of Rubistein & Sternberg
[
32
]
, Jerrard & Soner

[
22
]
, F.H. Lin

[
24
]
,
[
23
]
and Sandier & Serfaty

[
35
]
.
Quite recently Chen & Sternberg
[
15
]
have proved the convergence of the (rescaled) heat flow of the Ginzburg-Landau energies
defined on maps
u:M\to\mathbb{C}
where
M
is a two-dimensional compact simply connected Riemannian manifold without boundary.
Our setting and our results are however different for some crucial points.
As we deal with vector fields, the topology of
M
triggers the formation of the vortices via the Poincaré-Hopf Theorem, while in
[
15
]
the vortices arise thanks to the choice of initial conditions and the scaling of the equation.
Another difference lies in the energy or, more precisely, in the Dirichlet part of the energy.
The energy
\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|\nabla u\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}
defined on maps
u:M\to\mathbb{C}
(in this case
\nabla
stands for the Riemannian gradient) is conformally invariant. This is not the case for the energy
\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}
defined on vector fields (as before
D
is the covariant derivative).
The conformal invariance combined with the Uniformization Theorem permits (as in the “static” situation considered in
[
7
]
) to transfer the analysis from the manifold
M
to a reference domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
As a result, the renormalized energy is modeled on the Laplacian in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with corrections due to the conformal change. In particular, the interaction between the vortices is ruled by the Green function of the Laplacian in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
On the contrary, when dealing with the energy
\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}
, we have to deal with the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M
and with its Green function.
The resulting renormalized energy inherits the complexity of the Green function (see
[
40
]
)
and couples the Gauss curvature of
M
with the position and with the charge of the vortices. Moreover, it explicitly depends on the harmonic
1
-form
\xi
.
Finally,
the interaction term between the positions of the vortices behaves logarithmically only when the geodesic distance between the two vortices is small enough.

We will consider initial conditions
u_{\varepsilon}^{0}
satisfying the following set
of hypothesis.
Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\geq 1
,
we consider a
n
-uple of
distinct

points
\mathbf{a}^{0}=(a^{0}_{1},\,\ldots,\,a^{0}_{n})
in
M

and a
n
-uple of integers
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\,\ldots,\,d_{n})

such that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}=\chi(M),\qquad\left|d_{j}\right|=1\quad\textrm{for any }j.

Moreover, we fix an harmonic
1
-form
\xi^{0}
such that

\xi^{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d})
.
Once we have chosen
\mathbf{a}^{0}
,
\mathbf{d}
,
\xi^{0}
as above,
we consider a sequence of vector fields
u^{0}_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
(see (
2.53
) for the definition of this Sobolev space) such that

(1.9)

\displaystyle\omega(u^{0}_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}^{0}}\,\,\,\,\text{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)

(1.10)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{0})\leq\pi n|\log\varepsilon|+W(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi^{0})+n\gamma+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1),

(1.11)

\displaystyle\left\|u^{0}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\leq 1.

Energetically speaking we are saying that the sequence
u^{0}_{\varepsilon}
is a recovery sequence for
W(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi^{0})
.
A sequence
u^{0}_{\varepsilon}
that satisfies (
1.9
), (
1.10
), (
1.11
)
will be called a sequence of
well-prepared initial conditions
.
If
u^{0}_{\varepsilon}
is a sequence of well-prepared
initial conditions, then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a smooth solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of (
1.8
).
The main result of the paper is the following

Theorem 1
.

Let
(M,g)
be a two dimensional closed and oriented Riemannian manifold.
Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a sequence of solutions of (
1.8
)
with
u_{\varepsilon}^{0}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
a sequence of well-prepared initial conditions.
Then there exists a time
T^{*}\in(0,T)
(the first collision time) such that for any
t\in(0,T^{*})

\displaystyle\omega(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(j(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\right)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\xi(t),

where
\mathbf{a}(t):=(a_{1}(t),\,\dots,\,a_{n}(t))
is an
n
-uple of distinct points such that
\mathbf{a}\in H^{1}(0,\,T^{*};\,M^{n})
,
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0}
.
Moreover,
\xi\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
and

(1.12)

\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}\left(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d}\right)\quad\textrm{for any }t\in(0,\,T^{*}),\qquad\xi(0)=\xi^{0}.

Finally,
\mathbf{a}
is a solution of the gradient flow of the renormalized energy
W
, namely

(1.13)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{a}(t)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d},\xi(t))\qquad\textrm{for any }t\in(0,T^{*}),\\[11.38092pt]
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0}.\end{cases}

Although the function
W
is differentiable,
the definition of
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W

requires some care, because the variables
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

are
not
independent — they are related to each other
by the constraint
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
. Therefore,
the gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W
must be taken in a suitable sense.
We address this issue in Section
3

(see, in particular, Lemma
3.3
).
The maps
\mathbf{a}
,
\xi
are uniquely identified by the
conditions (
1.12
), (
1.13
)
— see Corollary
3.7
below. This justifies why the convergence in Theorem
1
holds for the whole sequence of
\varepsilon
.

The proof of Theorem
1
relies on the abstract scheme developed by Sandier & Serfaty in
[
35
]
.
This scheme essentially consists of three main points.
First of all, we need that the Ginzburg-Landau energy, upon rescaling,
\Gamma
-converges to the renormalized energy
W
. As anticipated, this first point is provided by the recent results of Ignat & Jerrard (
[
19
]
).
Second of all, we need a lower bound relating the properly rescaled
L^{2}
norm of
\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}
with the norm of the limit velocity
\mathbf{a}^{\prime}
.
Finally, we need a further lower bound relating the properly rescaled
L^{2}
norm of
the gradient of the Ginzburg-Landau energy with the norm of the gradient of the renormalized energy.
As in the Euclidean case, the proof of the first lower bound relies on the so-called product estimates (see Sandier & Serfaty in
[
36
]
) which are extended to our Riemannian setting.
The proof of the second lower bound relies on the expression for the gradient of the renormalized energy in terms of the canonical harmonic vector field, which is obtained as the limit of
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
along a suitable subsequence of
\varepsilon
. This approach is in the spirit of known results in
[
8
]
and
[
24
]
,
[
23
]
.
This strategy requires a careful study of the differentiability properties of the renormalized energy
W
and of the structure of its gradient with respect to the position of the vortices.

We conclude by recalling that the dynamics of Ginzburg-Landau type vortices for vector fields on surfaces including the effects of the extrinsic geometry of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
has been recently addressed in the paper
[
14
]
.

2.
Preliminary Material

2.1.
Differential Geometry preliminaries

We let
(M,g)
be a closed (compact and without boundary),
oriented
2
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with
Euler characteristics
\chi(M)
, Gauss curvature
\kappa

and Levi Civita connection
D
.
We let
\mathrm{vol}_{g}
denote the
2
-volume form on
M
induced
by the metric
g
and we set
\left|g\right|:=\sqrt{\left|\det g\right|}
.
We will write
B_{r}(p)
for the open geodesic ball in
M

with center
p\in M
and radius
r>0
.
We will use a similar notation for balls in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

as well. However, in this paper we only consider
balls
B_{r}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
centered at the origin.

We let
\displaystyle TM:=\bigsqcup_{x\in M}T_{x}M
the tangent bundle of
M
.
A vector field on
M
is a map

(2.1)

v:M\to TM,\,\,\,\,\,\,v(x)\in\mathrm{T}_{x}M.

We let
\left|v\right|_{g}:=g(v,v)^{1/2}=(v,v)_{g}^{1/2}
denote length of the vector
v
with respect to the metric
g
.

We recall that for a tensor of type
(1,1)
its Riemannian norm is given by

(2.2)

\left|Dv\right|_{g}^{2}:=g^{ij}(D_{i}v,D_{j}v)_{g},

(with some abuse of notation we indicate with the same symbol
\left|\cdot\right|_{g}
both the norm of a vector and the norm of a tensor)
and thus

(2.3)

\displaystyle\left|Dv(x)\right|_{g}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left|D_{\tau_{1}}v(x)\right|_{g}^{2}+\left|D_{\tau_{2}}v(x)\right|_{g}^{2},

(2.4)

\displaystyle(Dv(x),\,Dw(x))_{g}

\displaystyle=(D_{\tau_{1}}v(x),\,D_{\tau_{1}}w(x))_{g}+(D_{\tau_{2}}v(x),\,D_{\tau_{2}}w(x))_{g}

for any vector fields
v
and
w
and any
x\in T_{x}M
,
where
\left\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right\}
is an orthonormal basis for
T_{x}M
.

As we are interested in an evolution problem, we will need to consider the cylindrical manifold
Q:=[0,\,T]\times M
, with
T>0
.
The tangent space
TQ=T([0,\,T]\times M)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}\oplus TM
and thus, any vector field in
TM
can be identified with a vector field in
TQ
.
The cylindrical metric
g_{\text{cyl}}
is defined as

(2.5)

g_{\mathrm{cyl}}:={\mathrm{d}t}^{2}+g.

Thus, for
u\in TM
we have that
|u|_{g}=|u|_{g_{\mathrm{cyl}}}
.
We let
D^{Q}
be the Riemannian connection
on
(Q,\,g_{\mathrm{cyl}})
.

2.2.
Differential Forms

Given an
n
-dimensional Riemannian manifold
(N,\,g)

(with or without boundary) we let
T^{k}(T^{\prime}N)

denote the bundle of covariant
k
-tensors on
N
.
We let
\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}N)
denote the subset consisting of alternating
tensors, namely the disjoint union over
p\in N
of the
spaces
\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}_{p}N)
of alternating covariant
k
-tensors
on
T^{\prime}_{p}N
:

\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}N)=\bigsqcup_{p\in N}\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}_{p}N).

A differential
k
form (which we will often call
k
-form)
is a section of
\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}N)
.
The space of
k
-differential forms on
N
is denoted
\Omega^{k}(N)
.
At each point
p\in M
,
\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}_{p}N)
has a scalar product
induced by the metric
g
.
With some abuse of notation, we will denote it by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
.
The scalar products on
TN
and
T^{\prime}N
induce an isometric
isomorphism bewteen
TN
and
T^{\prime}N
, denoted

\flat\colon TN\to T^{\prime}N,\qquad\#=\flat^{-1}\colon T^{\prime}N\to TN

Adopting the standard convention for the indices, we can write

\flat
,
\#
in a local coordinate system
\{\mathrm{d}x^{1},\,\ldots,\,\mathrm{d}x^{n}\}

as

X^{\flat}=g_{jk}\,X^{j}\,\mathrm{d}x^{k},\qquad\omega^{\#}=g^{jk}\omega_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}

for any vector field
X
and
1
-form
\omega
.
Here
g_{jk}
are the component of the metric tensor
g

and
g^{jk}
are the components of
g^{-1}
.
These isomorphisms allow us to define the Riemannian
gradient of a scalar function
\varphi\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
,
as
\nabla\varphi:=(\mathrm{d}\varphi)^{\#}
.

We let
p\in N
. For any
v\in T_{p}N
,
we define a linear map
{\bf i}_{v}:\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}_{p}(N))\to\Lambda^{k-1}(T^{\prime}_{p}(N))
, called
interior multiplication, as

(2.6)

({\bf i}_{v}\omega)\left(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\right):=\omega\left(v,w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\forall\omega\in\Lambda^{k}(T^{\prime}_{p}(N)).

This definition extends to differential forms and vector fields by working pointwise. More precisely, if
v
is a vector field and
\omega
is a
k
-differential form, we define the a
(k-1)
-differential form by

(2.7)

({\bf i}_{v}\omega)_{p}:={\bf i}_{v(p)}\omega_{p}.

We let

(2.8)

\star:\Omega^{k}(N)\to\Omega^{n-k}(N)

denote the Hodge dual operator. The Hodge dual operator is linear and it is uniquely defined by requiring that

(2.9)

\omega\wedge\star\eta=\left(\omega,\,\eta\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

for any
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(N)
,
\eta\in\Omega^{k}(N)
.
The Hodge dual is an isometric isomorphism

\Omega^{k}(N)\to\Omega^{n-k}(N)
and it satisfies

(2.10)

\star\star\omega=(-1)^{k(n-k)}\omega\qquad\textrm{for any }\omega\in\Omega^{k}(N).

Using the Hodge
\star
operator, we may now define
the codifferential
\mathrm{d}^{*}\colon\Omega^{k}(N)\to\Omega^{k-1}(N)
as

(2.11)

\mathrm{d}^{*}\omega:=(-1)^{n(k-1)+1}\star\mathrm{d}\star\omega\qquad\textrm{for any }\omega\in\Omega^{k}(N).

The differential
\mathrm{d}
and the codifferential
\mathrm{d}^{*}

satisfy an “integration by parts”: if

U
is an open bounded subset in
M
, the boundary
\partial U

is smooth (and carries the orientation induced by
U
),
and if
\eta\in\Omega^{k-1}(N)
,
\zeta\in\Omega^{k}(N)
, then

(2.12)

\int_{U}\left(\mathrm{d}\eta,\,\zeta\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{U}\left(\eta,\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\zeta\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\int_{\partial U}\eta\wedge\star\zeta.

Equation (
2.12
) follows by writing

\mathrm{d}(\eta\wedge\star\zeta)=\mathrm{d}\eta\wedge\star\zeta+(-1)^{k-1}\eta\wedge\mathrm{d}(\star\zeta)
and applying Stokes’ theorem.
If
N
is closed and
U=N
, then (
2.12
) reduces to

\int_{N}\left(\mathrm{d}\eta,\,\zeta\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{N}\left(\eta,\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\zeta\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

In particular,
\mathrm{d}^{*}
is the
L^{2}
-adjoint of
\mathrm{d}
.

We will specialize these definitions when
N=M
(where
M
, as above,
is a closed, oriented, two-dimensional Riemannian manifold)
and when
N
is the “space-time” cylinder
Q=[0,T]\times M
,
equipped with the cylindrical metric (
2.5
).
As we will work in the ambient space
N=M
most of the time,
we will denote the Hodge dual, differential and codifferential
on the manifold
M
simply as
\star
,
\mathrm{d}
,
\mathrm{d}^{*}
.
On the other hand, the Hodge dual, differential and codifferential
on the product manifold
Q
are denoted as
\star_{Q}
,
\mathrm{d}_{Q}
,
\mathrm{d}_{Q}^{*}
,
respectively.

Since
M
is
2
-dimensional, the Hodge dual operator
maps
0
-forms (i.e., scalar functions) into
2
-forms and vice-versa,
as
\star 1=\mathrm{vol}_{g}
,
\star\mathrm{vol}_{g}=1
. The action of
\star
on

1
-forms may be expressed in local coordinates
\{x^{1},\,x^{2}\}
as

(2.13)

\displaystyle\star\mathrm{d}x^{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}(g_{12}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+g_{22}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2})

(2.14)

\displaystyle\star\mathrm{d}x^{2}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}(g_{11}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+g_{12}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2})

The Hodge dual of a
1
-form may also be used to define
an almost complex structure on
M
, that is,
a map
i:TM\to TM
that restricts to an isometry on each
tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}M
and satisfies

(2.15)

i^{2}v+v=0

for any
v\in T_{p}M
. We construct such a map by imposing

(2.16)

(iv,\,w)_{g}=-(v,\,iw)_{g}:=\mathrm{vol}_{g}(v,\,w)

for any
v\in T_{p}M
,
w\in T_{p}M
. Note that
(iv,v)_{g}=0
for any
v\in T_{p}M
.
Equation (
2.16
) immediately implies

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(M,\,g)
be a two-dimensional Riemannian manifold.
For any vector field
X
on
M
, there holds

(2.17)

(iX)^{\flat}=\star X^{\flat}.

In particular, if we take

X=\nabla\psi
where
\psi:M\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function,
and define
\nabla^{\perp}\psi:=i\nabla\psi
, then

(2.18)

(\nabla^{\perp}\psi)^{\flat}=\star\mathrm{d}\psi.

On a
2
-dimensional manifold, the codifferential
\mathrm{d}^{*}

may be written as
\mathrm{d}^{*}=-\star\mathrm{d}\star
.
If
\Psi\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is a
2
-form and
\psi:=\star\Psi
, then

(2.19)

\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi=-\star\mathrm{d}(\star\Psi)=-\star\mathrm{d}\psi.

Suppose now the ambient manifold is
Q:=[0,\,T]\times M
.
Given a local coordinate system
\{t,\,x^{1},\,x^{2}\}
,
we may write the Hodge dual of
1
-forms on
Q
as

(2.20)

\displaystyle\star_{Q}\mathrm{d}x^{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}(-g_{12}\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}-g_{22}\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2})

(2.21)

\displaystyle\star_{Q}\mathrm{d}x^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|g|}}(g_{11}\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}+g_{12}\,\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}).

In particular, let
X
be a vector field on
Q

of the form
X=X^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
, with
X^{k}=X^{k}(t,x)
for
k=1,2
.
By comparing (
2.20
)–(
2.21
)
with (
2.13
)–(
2.14
), we obtain
(
{\bf i}_{t}
denotes the interior multiplication by
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
)

(2.22)

{\bf i}_{t}(\star_{Q}X^{\flat})=-\star X^{b}.

Let
\psi:M\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function. We let
X:=\nabla\psi

and consider a
2
-form
\omega:=\omega_{k}\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{k}

with
\omega_{k}=\omega_{k}(t,x)
.
Thanks to (
2.9
), (
2.22
), (
2.17
) and (
2.7
)
we obtain the following identity:

(2.23)

\begin{split}\omega\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi&=\left(\omega,\star_{Q}X^{\flat}\right)_{g}\\
&=\left({\bf i}_{t}\omega,{\bf i}_{t}(\star_{Q}X^{\flat})\right)_{g}=-\left({\bf i}_{t}\omega,\star X^{\flat}\right)_{g}=-\left({\bf i}_{t}\omega,(\nabla^{\perp}\psi)^{\flat}\right)_{g}\\
&=-\omega\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\nabla^{\perp}\psi\right).\end{split}

2.2.1.
Differential Operators

We recall the definition of a few
differential operators. Throughout this subsection, we
work in the ambient manifold
M
.
The divergence of a vector field
v
is defined
as the Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{v}
of the volume form in the
direction of
v
, that is

(2.24)

(\mathrm{div}\,v)\mathrm{vol}_{g}:=\mathcal{L}_{v}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\mathrm{d}(\mathbf{i}_{v}\mathrm{vol}_{g})

As
\left|v\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=v^{\flat}\wedge\star v^{\flat}
,
we may equivalently write

\mathrm{div}\,v=\star(\mathcal{L}_{v}\mathrm{vol}_{g})=\star\mathrm{d}(\star v^{\flat})=-\mathrm{d}^{*}v^{\flat}

Since
\mathrm{d}^{*}
is the
L^{2}
-adjoint of
\mathrm{d}
, it follows that

-\mathrm{div}\,
is the
L^{2}
-adjoint of the Riemannian gradient
\nabla
.
Moreover, for any smooth, open set
U\subseteq M
and
any smooth function
\varphi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
,
Equation (
2.12
) implies

(2.25)

\int_{U}(\nabla\varphi,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=-\int_{U}\varphi\,(\mathrm{div}\,v)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\int_{\partial U}\varphi\,(\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

where
\nu
is the exterior-pointing unit normal to
\partial U
.

Given a vector field
v
on
M
, the covariant derivative
Dv

may be regarded as (fiber-wise linear) operator
\mathrm{T}M\to\mathrm{T}M
,
which maps a tangent vector
w\in\mathrm{T}_{p}M
into the
tangent vector
D_{w}v\in T_{p}M
. Equivalently, the operator
D

maps sections of
TM
into sections
of
\mathrm{Hom}(TM,\,TM)\simeq TM\otimes T^{\prime}M
.
The bundle
TM\otimes T^{\prime}M
is equipped with a scalar product,
as defined in (
2.3
)-(
2.4
).
The
L^{2}
-adjoint
D^{*}
of
D
maps sections of
TM\otimes T^{\prime}M

into sections of
TM
, i.e. vector fields.
We define the rough Laplacian (also known as connection Laplacian or Bochner Laplacian) as

(2.26)

-\Delta_{g}v:=D^{*}Dv.

By definition, the rough Laplacian satisfies

(2.27)

-\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}v,\,w)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{M}(Dv,\,Dw)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}

for any smooth vector fields
v
,
w
on
M
.
We can characterise more explicitely
D^{*}
, and hence
\Delta_{g}
,
in terms of the covariant derivative
D
.
Let
u
,
v
,
w
be smooth vector fields. Since the covariant derivative
is compatible with the metric, we have

\begin{split}(D_{w}u,\,v)_{g}=\mathrm{d}(u,\,v)_{g}(w)-(u,\,D_{w}v)_{g}=\mathrm{div}\,\!\left((u,\,v)_{g}w\right)-(u,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{div}\,w-(u,\,D_{w}v)_{g}\end{split}

Therefore, for any smooth open set
U\subseteq M
,

(2.28)

\int_{U}(D_{w}u,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\partial U}(u,\,v)_{g}\,(w,\,\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{U}(u,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{div}\,w\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\int_{U}(u,\,D_{w}v)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}

In particular,
D^{*}_{w}=-D_{w}-\mathrm{div}\,w
.
Comparing (
2.27
) with (
2.28
),
we obtain an expression for
\Delta_{g}
in terms of an orthonormal
tangent frame
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
:

(2.29)

-\Delta_{g}u=D^{*}_{\tau_{k}}D_{\tau_{k}}u=-D_{\tau_{k}}D_{\tau_{k}}u-(\mathrm{div}\,\tau_{k})D_{\tau_{k}}u=-D_{\tau_{k}}D_{\tau_{k}}u+D_{D_{\tau_{k}}\tau_{k}}u

(the last equality is obtained by differentiating the orthonormality
conditions
(\tau_{h},\,\tau_{k})_{g}=\delta_{hk}
).

We will consider another notion of Laplacian, that is, the Hodge Laplacian for differential forms.
The Hodge Laplacian of a
k
-form
\omega
is defined as

(2.30)

-\Delta\omega:=\left(\mathrm{d}\mathrm{d}^{*}+\mathrm{d}^{*}\mathrm{d}\right)\omega.

Thanks to (
2.12
),
the Hodge Laplacian satisfies

-\int_{M}(\Delta\omega,\,\zeta)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{M}\left((\mathrm{d}\omega,\,\mathrm{d}\zeta)_{g}+(\mathrm{d}^{*}\omega,\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\zeta)_{g}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}

for any smooth
k
-forms
\omega
,
\zeta
.
In particular, a form
\omega
is harmonic (i.e.,
\Delta\omega=0
)
if and only if
\mathrm{d}\omega=0
and
\mathrm{d}^{*}\omega=0
.
However, the rough Laplacian and the Hodge Laplacian
do
not
coincide: in general,
(\Delta_{g}v)^{\flat}\neq\Delta v^{\flat}
.
(In fact, we have the Weitzenböck
identity
(\Delta_{g}v)^{\flat}-\Delta v^{\flat}=Av^{\flat}
,
where
A
is an operator of order zero depending on
the curvature of
M
.)

2.2.2.
Connection
1
-form

Let
U\subseteq M
be an open set and let
\tau_{1}
,

\tau_{2}
be smooth vector fields on
U
. We say
that
\left\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\right\}
is an orthonormal,
positvely oriented, tangent frame on
U

— or simply a
moving frame
— if

(2.31)

\left(\tau_{j},\tau_{k}\right)_{g}=\delta_{jk},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{vol}_{g}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=1\,\,\,\,\,\\
\text{ on }U.

The connection
1
-form associated with
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}

is defined by

(2.32)

\mathcal{A}(v):=\left(\tau_{1},\,D_{v}\tau_{2}\right)_{g}.

for any smooth vector field
v
on
U
.
Note that the following holds

\mathcal{A}(v)=-\left(\tau_{2},D_{v}\tau_{1}\right)_{g}

Since
\left\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right\}
are orthonormal, we get that

(2.33)

D_{v}\tau_{1}=-\mathcal{A}(v)\tau_{2}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D_{v}\tau_{2}=\mathcal{A}(v)\tau_{1}.

The connection
1
-form satisfies

(2.34)

\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}=\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
\kappa
is the Gauss curvature of
M

(see for instance
[
16
, Proposition 2, p. 92]
).
We let
\mathbb{A}
be the vector field (sometimes called spin connection)

(2.35)

\mathbb{A}:=\mathcal{A}^{\sharp},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ namely }\,\,\,\,\mathbb{A}^{j}:=g^{jk}\mathcal{A}_{k}.

By possibily modifying the frame
\left\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right\}
,
we can always assume (see
[
38
, Lemma 6.1]
) that

(2.36)

\mathrm{div}\,\mathbb{A}=0

2.3.
Special coordinate systems and the Ginzburg-Landau energy in coordinate

2.3.1.
Normal coordinates

We recall that the exponential map

\mathrm{Exp}_{p}\colon T_{p}M\to M

at a point
p\in M
is defined by

\mathrm{Exp}_{p}(v):=\gamma_{p,v}(1)\qquad\textrm{for any }v\in T_{p}M,

where
\gamma_{p,v}\colon\mathbb{R}\to M
is the unique geodesic
such that
\gamma_{p,v}(0)=p
,
\gamma_{p,v}^{\prime}(0)=v
.
As
M
is compact and smooth, there exists a number
\delta_{*}>0

(the injectivity radius of
M
) such that,
for any
\delta\in(0,\,\delta_{*})
,
\mathrm{Exp}_{p}

restricts to a diffeomorphism from
B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq T_{p}M

to its image,
B_{\delta}(p):=\mathrm{Exp}_{p}(B_{\delta}(0))\subseteq M
.

B_{\delta}(p)
is the geodesic ball in
M
of
center
p
and radius
\delta
. We choose an
orthonormal, positively oriented basis
\{\hat{e}_{1},\,\hat{e}_{2}\}

of
T_{p}M
and consider the isomorphism

E\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to T_{p}M
,
E(x^{1},x^{2}):=x^{1}\hat{e}_{1}+x^{2}\hat{e}_{2}
.
Then, we define the geodesic normal coordinates centered at
p
as the map

\Phi:=E^{-1}\circ\text{Exp}_{p}^{-1}\colon B_{\delta}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We have

\Phi(B_{\delta}(p))=B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The metric tensor
g
, written in geodesic normal coordinates, satisfies

(2.37)

\displaystyle g_{jk}(x)=\delta_{jk}+\mathrm{O}\!\left(\left|x\right|^{2}\right)\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ and }\,\,\,\,\,\,\det g(x)=1+\mathrm{O}\!\left(\left|x\right|^{2}\right),

(2.38)

\displaystyle\left|\nabla\Phi^{-1}(x)\right|=\mathrm{O}\!\left(\left|x\right|\right)

for any
x\in B_{\delta}(0)
.
Moreover,

(2.39)

\displaystyle g_{ij}(0)=\delta_{ij}\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\partial_{j}\det g(0)=0\quad\forall j,

(2.40)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i,j}^{k}(0)\qquad\textrm{ and }\qquad\partial_{k}g_{ij}(0)=0\quad\forall i,j,k.

In particular, from (
2.39
) we deduce that the coordinates fields
\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}
verify

(2.41)

\mathrm{div}\,\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right)(0)=0\qquad\forall j=1,2.

When working with the cylindrical manifold
Q:=[0,\,T]\times M
,
we will often use the coordinate system given by

(2.42)

\Phi_{Q}:=\left(\Id,\,\Phi\right)\colon[0,T]\times B_{\delta}(p)\to[0,\,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2},\qquad\Phi_{Q}(t,\,x)=(t,\,\Phi(x))

We have (
(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}
denotes the pullback)

(2.43)

(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}(\mathrm{vol}_{g}\wedge\mathrm{d}t)=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|^{2})\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}t.

2.3.2.
The Ginzburg-Landau energy in geodesic coordinates

Given a vector field
u
, locally defined on an
open subset of
u
, we will introduce
a convenient representation of
u
in terms of
geodesic coordinates and express the Ginzburg-Landau
energy of
u
in terms of geodesic coordinates.
For simplicity, we consider time-independent vector fields,
but the same argument applies verbatim to vector fields
that depend on time as well.
Let
B_{\delta}(p)
be a geodesic ball in
M
, with
\delta
small enough,
so that the geodesic coordinates
\Phi\colon B_{\delta}(p)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
are well-defined.
Let
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
be an orthonormal, positively oriented tangent frame
on
B_{\delta}(p)
. We may choose the frame in such a way that the associated
connection
1
-form
\mathcal{A}
(see (
2.32
)) satisfies

(2.44)

\mathcal{A}(p)=0

(see e.g.
[
38
, Lemma 6.1]
). This implies

(2.45)

\left|\mathcal{A}(\Phi^{-1}(x))\right|=O(\left|x\right|)\qquad\textrm{for any }x\in B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Let
u\in H^{1}_{\tan}(B_{\delta}(p))
be a vector field. We write

u=u^{1}\,\tau_{1}+u^{2}\,\tau_{2}

for some scalar functions
u^{1}
,
u^{2}
. We define a map

v=(v^{1},v^{2})\colon B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

by

v^{k}(x):=u^{k}(\Phi^{-1}(x))

for
x\in B_{\delta}(0)
,
k=1,\,2
, so that

(2.46)

u(\Phi(x))=v^{1}(x)\,\tau_{1}(\Phi(x))+v^{2}(x)\,\tau_{2}(\Phi(x))\qquad\textrm{for any }x\in B_{\delta}(0).

We denote with
\left|\cdot\right|
and
(\cdot,\cdot)

the norm and the scalar product in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, respectively.
Moreover, we denote with
\nabla
the usual (Euclidean)
derivative in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
is an orthonormal tangent frame
that satisfies (
2.45
), then

\left|Du(\Phi^{-1}(x))\right|^{2}_{g}=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|^{2})\right)\left|\nabla v(x)\right|^{2}+\mathrm{O}(\left|v(x)\right|^{2})

for any
x\in B_{\delta}(0)
.

Proof.

We compute the covariant derivatives of
u
with
respect to
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
. Thanks to (
2.33
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{\tau_{1}}u=D_{\tau_{1}}\left(u^{1}\tau_{1}+u^{2}\tau_{2}\right)=\left(\mathrm{d}u^{1}(\tau_{1})+u^{2}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{1})\right)\tau_{1}+\left(\mathrm{d}u^{2}(\tau_{1})-u^{1}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{1})\right)\tau_{2}\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{\tau_{2}}u=D_{\tau_{2}}\left(u^{1}\tau_{1}+u^{2}\tau_{2}\right)=\left(\mathrm{d}u^{1}(\tau_{2})+u^{2}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{2})\right)\tau_{1}+\left(\mathrm{d}u^{2}(\tau_{2})-u^{1}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{2})\right)\tau_{2}\end{split}

As the frame
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
is orthonormal, we deduce

\begin{split}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}=\left|D_{\tau_{1}}u\right|^{2}_{g}+\left|D_{\tau_{2}}u\right|^{2}_{g}&=\left|\mathrm{d}u^{1}(\tau_{1})+u^{2}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{1})\right|^{2}+\left|\mathrm{d}u^{2}(\tau_{1})-u^{1}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{1})\right|^{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\left|\mathrm{d}u^{1}(\tau_{2})+u^{2}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{2})\right|^{2}+\left|\mathrm{d}u^{2}(\tau_{2})-u^{1}\mathcal{A}(\tau_{2})\right|^{2}\end{split}

and, by expanding the squares,

(2.47)

\begin{split}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}=\left|\mathrm{d}u^{1}\right|^{2}_{g}+\left|\mathrm{d}u^{2}\right|^{2}_{g}+\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}_{g}\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}+2\sum_{k=1}^{2}\left(u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u^{1}(\tau_{k})-u^{1}\,\mathrm{d}u^{2}(\tau_{k})\right)\mathcal{A}(\tau_{k})\end{split}

The last term in the right-hand side of (
2.47
)
may be estimated as

\left|\sum_{k=1}^{2}\left(u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u^{1}(\tau_{k})-u^{1}\,\mathrm{d}u^{2}(\tau_{k})\right)\mathcal{A}(\tau_{k})\right|\lesssim\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}_{g}\left(\left|\mathrm{d}u^{1}\right|_{g}^{2}+\left|\mathrm{d}u^{2}\right|^{2}_{g}\right)+\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}

Therefore, we obtain

\begin{split}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\left|\mathcal{A}\right|^{2}_{g})\right)\left(\left|\mathrm{d}u^{1}\right|^{2}_{g}+\left|\mathrm{d}u^{2}\right|^{2}_{g}\right)+\mathrm{O}(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g})\end{split}

By writing the right-hand side in terms of
v
, and
applying (
2.37
), (
2.38
)
and (
2.45
), the lemma follows.
∎

We consider the Ginzburg-Landau energy density

(2.48)

e_{\varepsilon}(u):=\frac{1}{2}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1\right)^{2},

and its Euclidean counterpart

(2.49)

\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v):=\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla v\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}-1\right)^{2}.

Lemma
2.2
implies

(2.50)

e_{\varepsilon}(u)\left(\Phi^{-1}(x)\right)=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|^{2})\right)\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v)(x)+\mathrm{O}(\left|v(x)\right|^{2})

for any
x\in B_{\delta}(0)=\Phi(B_{\delta}(p))
.
Therefore, the Ginzburg-Landau energy on the ball
B_{\delta}(p)

may be written as

(2.51)

\int_{B_{\delta}(p)}e_{\varepsilon}(u)\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})\right)\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\left(\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v)(x)+\mathrm{O}(\left|v(x)\right|^{2})\right)\mathrm{d}x

2.4.
Functional spaces

For functions
u:M\to\mathbb{R}
, the Lebesgue and
Sobolev spaces
L^{p}(M)
and
W^{1,p}(M)
are defined as in
[
4
]
.
The dual space of
W^{1,p}(M)
is

W^{-1,p}(M):=\left(W^{1,q}(M)\right)^{\prime},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1.

In particular, for a (measure-valued)
2
-form we set

\left\|\mu\right\|_{W^{-1,p}(M)}:=\sup\left\{\int_{M}f\mu:\quad f\in W^{1,q}(M),\quad\left\|f\right\|_{W^{1,q}(M)}\leq 1\right\}.

When dealing with vector fields, for
p\in[1,+\infty)
we set

(2.52)

\displaystyle L^{p}_{\tang}(M)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v:M\to TM:\,\,\,\,\,\,v(x)\in T_{x}M,\quad\left|v\right|_{g}\in L^{p}(M)\right\},

(2.53)

\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v:M\to TM:\,\,\,\,\,\,v(x)\in T_{x}M,\quad\left|v\right|_{g}+\left|Dv\right|_{g}\in L^{p}(M)\right\},

where the covariant derivative
D
is understood in the distributions sense.
We set
H^{1}_{\tang}(M):=W^{1,2}_{\tang}(M)
and
we recall that smooth vector fields are indeed dense in
W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)
, see
[
19
, Lemma 5.1]
.

The Nash-Moser embedding
[
29
]
gives an isometric embedding

j\colon\left(M,\,g\right)\to\left(\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\,(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}}\right)
. Therefore, we define

(2.54)

H^{1}(a,\,b;\,M):=\left\{v\in H^{1}(a,\,b;\,\mathbb{R}^{\ell}):v(t)\in j(M)\quad\text{ for any }t\in(a,b)\right\}

With some abuse of notation we will identify
M
with
j(M)\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
.

The Riemannian manifold
(M,\,g)
is indeed also a metric space. Given two points
P
and
Q
in
M
, the (Riemannian) distance between
P
and
Q
is

(2.55)

\dist_{g}(P,\,Q):=\inf\left\{\text{L}_{\gamma},\,\,\,\gamma\colon[a,b]\to M\hbox{ is piecewise smooth,}\,\,\gamma(a)=P,\,\,\gamma(b)=Q\right\}\!,

where

(2.56)

\text{L}_{\gamma}:=\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|_{g}\,\hbox{d}t.

is the lenght of the piecewise smooth curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
.

Given a curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to M
, we define its metric derivative (see
[
3
]
) as

(2.57)

\left|\gamma^{\prime}\right|_{g}(t):=\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\dist_{g}\left(\gamma(t+h),\gamma(t)\right)}{\left|h\right|},

provided this limit exists.
For a curve
\gamma\in H^{1}(a,\,b;\,M)
,
the metric derivative exists at almost every point
and belongs to
L^{2}(a,\,b)
, as expressed by
the following

Proposition 2.3

(Metric derivative)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a closed oriented
2
-dimensional Riemannian manifold.
Let
\gamma\colon[a,\,b]\to M
be a measurable curve.
Then
\gamma\in H^{1}(a,b;M)
if and only if there exists

m\in L^{2}(a,b)
such that

(2.58)

\dist_{g}\left(\gamma(t),\gamma(s)\right)\leq\int_{s}^{t}m(r)\,\mathrm{d}r\qquad\textrm{for any }s,t\ \textrm{ with }a<s\leq t<b.

In this case, the limit
(
2.57
) exists for almost any
t\in(a,b)
, the function
t\mapsto\left|\gamma^{\prime}\right|_{g}(t)
belongs to
L^{2}(a,b)
and is such that

\left|\gamma^{\prime}\right|_{g}(t)\leq m(t),\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for a.a. }t\in(a,b)

for each function
m\in L^{2}(a,b)
satisfying (
2.58
).
Moreover,
there holds

(2.59)

\left|\gamma^{\prime}\right|_{g}(t)=\left|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\right|_{g}\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ a.e. in }(a,b).

Proof.

The proof that (
2.58
) implies that the metric derivative exists for almost any
t
and belongs to
L^{2}(a,b)
is in
[
3
, Theorem 1.1.2]
.
It is easy to prove that any
\gamma\in H^{1}(a,b;M)
satisfies (
2.58
)
(take
m(t)=\left|\gamma^{\prime}(t)\right|_{g}
and use (
2.55
)).
We claim that a measurable curve
\gamma\colon[a,\,b]\to M

that satisfies (
2.58
) belongs to
H^{1}(a,b;M)
.
Let
\gamma\colon[a,\,b]\to M
be a measurable curve
that satisfies (
2.58
).
We have
\gamma\in L^{2}(a,\,b;\,M)
because
M
is compact.
Let
h\in(0,\,b-a)
.
Equation (
2.58
) and the Hölder inequality imply

\dist_{g}(\gamma(t),\,\gamma(t+h))\leq h\int_{t}^{t+h}m^{2}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r

for any
t\in(a,\,b-h)
. By Fubini’s theorem, we deduce

\begin{split}\int_{a}^{b+h}\dist_{g}^{2}(\gamma(t),\,\gamma(t+h))\,\mathrm{d}t\leq h\int_{a}^{b-h}\left(\int_{t}^{t+h}m^{2}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathrm{d}t=h^{2}\int_{a}^{b}m^{2}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r\end{split}

Therefore, identifying
M
with
j(M)\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}

we deduce that

\int_{a}^{b+h}\left|\gamma(t)-\gamma(t+h)\right|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{\ell}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq h^{2}\int_{a}^{b}m^{2}(r)\,\mathrm{d}r

for any
h>0
. This implies
\gamma\in H^{1}(a,\,b;\,M)

[
11
]
.

Finally, we show (
2.59
).
Let
\gamma\in H^{1}(a,\,b;\,M)
. Let
s\in(a,\,b)
be
a Lebesgue point for the distributional derivative
\gamma^{\prime}
,
such that the metric derivative
\left|\gamma^{\prime}\right|_{g}(s)<+\infty
.
Let
\sigma>0
be smaller than the injectivity radius of
M
.
Let
I(s)
be a neighborhood of
s
such that for
t\in I(s)

there holds
\gamma(t)\in B_{\sigma}(\gamma(s))
. (Such a neighborhood
exists because
\gamma
is continuous, by Sobolev embeddings.)
We consider geodesic normal coordinates centered in
\gamma(s)

and we let, for
t\in I(s)
,
\bar{\gamma}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

be the representation of
\gamma(t)
in this coordinates (see Subsection
2.3.1
).
In particular,
\bar{\gamma}(s)=(0,\,0)
.
By definition of geodesic normal coordinates, for any
t\in I(s)

there exists a unique geodesic of endpoints
\gamma(s)
and
\gamma(t)
,
which is represented in normal coordinates by a straight line segment of
endpoints
\bar{\gamma}(s)=(0,\,0)
and
\bar{\gamma}(t)
.
Moreover, by Gauss’ lemma, geodesic normal coordinates
are a radial isometry. Therefore, we have

\dist_{g}(\gamma(t),\,\gamma(s))=\left|\bar{\gamma}(t)\right|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}.

Then,

\left|\gamma^{\prime}\right|_{g}(s)=\lim_{t\to s}\frac{\dist_{g}(\gamma(t),\,\gamma(s))}{\left|t-s\right|}=\lim_{t\to s}\frac{\left|\bar{\gamma}(t)\right|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}}{\left|t-s\right|}=\left|\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}(s)\right|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

and, since geodesic normal coordinates centered at
\gamma(s)

map isometrically
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(s)}M
into
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\left|\bar{\gamma}^{\prime}(s)\right|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=\left|\gamma^{\prime}(s)\right|_{g}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

2.5.
Index and vorticity of a vector field

We recall the definitions of index and vorticity of a vector field
(see also
[
19
, Section 1.2]
).
To every isolated zero
p\in M
of a smooth vector field
u
we can associate an integer number, called the index of
u
at
p
, in the following way.
Let
\delta>0
be a small parameter, such that
the ball
B_{\delta}(p)
does not contain any zero of
u

other than
p
. Using normal geodesic coordinates,
we may represent
u
by a map
v\colon B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
as in (
2.46
). We have
\left|v\right|=\left|u\right|_{g}>0
on
\partial V

and hence, the map
v/\left|v\right|\colon\partial_{\delta}(0)\to\mathbb{S}^{1}

is well-defined and smooth. We define the index of
u
at
p

as the topological degree of
v/\left|v\right|
on
\partial B_{\delta}(p)
,

(2.60)

\ind(u,\,\partial B_{\delta}(p)):=\deg\left(\frac{v}{\left|v\right|},\,\partial B_{\delta}(p),\,\mathbb{S}^{1}\right)

We have used geodesic normal coordinates for
convenience only; the index is actually
independent of the choice of the coordinate system,
because the degree is invariant by composition with
(orientation-preserving) diffeomorphisms.
If
U\subseteq M
is an open bounded set,
u
is a smooth vector field
with finitely many zeros in
U
and
u(x)\neq 0
for any
x\in\partial U
,
we define

(2.61)

\text{ind}(u,\partial U):=\sum_{p\in u^{-1}(0)}\ind(u,\,\partial B_{\delta}(p))

where
\delta>0
is such that the balls
B_{\delta}(p)

(with
p\in u^{-1}(0)
) are pairwise disjoint.
The topological degree and the index of a vector field
can defined for maps and vector fields with Sobolev or VMO regularity
(see
[
9
]
,
[
10
]
and
[
12
]
).

There is an equivalent definition of the index,
in terms of the so-called vorticity measure,
which is more convenient for our purposes.
We give the definition directly in terms of vector fields with Sobolev regularity.
For a vector field
u\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
(or, more generally,
u\in W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)\cap L^{q}_{\tang}(M)
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
) we define the
1
-form

(2.62)

j(u)=(Du,iu)_{g}.

(The map
i\colon TM\to TM
is defined in (
2.15
), (
2.16
).)
We define the vorticity measure as the
2
-form

(2.63)

\omega(u)=\mathrm{d}j(u)+\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
\kappa
is the Gauss curvature of
M
.
If
U\subset M
is open with smooth boundary,

U^{\prime}
is a neighbourhood of
\overline{U}

and
u\in W^{1,p}_{\tang}(U^{\prime})\cap L^{q}_{\tang}(U^{\prime})

is such that
\left|u\right|_{g}\geq 1/2
a.e. in a neighbourhood of
\partial U
,
we define the index of
u
on
\partial U
as

(2.64)

\text{ind}(u,\,\partial U):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\partial U}\frac{j(u)}{\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}}+\int_{U}\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)

If, additionally,
\left|u\right|_{g}=1
on
\partial U
,
then by Stokes’ theorem we obtain

(2.65)

\text{ind}(u,\,\partial U)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\partial U}j(u)+\int_{U}\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{U}\omega(u).

If
u
is smooth with finitely many zeroes
in
U
, the index as defined in (
2.64
)
agrees with (
2.61
). Indeed, take an orthonormal,
positively oriented tangent frame
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}

defined in a neighbourhood of
\partial U
.
(Such a frame exists, see e.g.
[
19
, Lemma 6.1]
.)
By lifting results, we may write
u
as

u=\rho\left((\cos\theta)\tau_{1}+(\sin\theta)\tau_{2}\right)\qquad\textrm{on }\partial U,

where
\rho:=\left|u\right|_{g}
and
\theta\colon\partial U\to\mathbb{R}

is a function that is smooth except for,
at most, one jump discontinuity on each connected
component of
\partial U
. We may compute
j(u)

in terms of
\rho
and
\theta
, as

(2.66)

j(u)=\rho^{2}\left(\mathrm{d}\theta-\mathcal{A}\right)

where
\mathcal{A}
is the connection
1
-form of
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
,
defined in (
2.32
). Equation (
2.66
)
shows that
\mathrm{d}\theta
can be extended smoothly to
\partial U
,
even if
\theta
is discontinuous. The index of
u
, as defined
in (
2.64
), can be written as

\begin{split}\text{ind}(u,\,\partial U)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\partial U}(\mathrm{d}\theta-\mathcal{A})+\int_{U}\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\int_{\partial U}\mathrm{d}\theta+\int_{U}(-\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}+\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g})\right)\\
&\hskip-5.69046pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dconnection}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial U}\mathrm{d}\theta\end{split}

We write
\Gamma_{1}
, …,
\Gamma_{p}
for the
connected components of
\partial U
; each
\Gamma_{k}

is given the orientation induced by
U
. Then, we deduce

\text{ind}(u,\,\partial U)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}\deg(e^{i\theta},\,\Gamma_{k},\,\mathbb{S}^{1})

and (
2.61
) follows, by additivity of the degree.

The definitions (
2.62
) and (
2.63
)
apply to Euclidean vector fields as well.
If
u\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is smooth, then

\omega(u)=\mathrm{d}j(u)=2(\det\nabla u)\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}.

and thus, if
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
open, simply connected,
with Lipschitz boundary and
\left|u\right|=1
on
\partial U
, we have

\text{ind}(u,\partial U)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{U}\mathrm{d}j(u)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{U}\text{det}\nabla u\,\mathrm{d}x=\text{deg}(u,\,\partial U,\,\mathbb{S}^{1}).

3.
The renormalized energy and its gradient

First, we set some notation and we
recall some results from
[
19
]
.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\geq 1
.
We denote
n
-uples of points in
M
as
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n})

and
n
-uples of integer numbers as
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\,\ldots,\,d_{n})
.
We restrict our attention to the pairs
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
in the ‘admissible class’

(3.1)

\mathscr{A}^{n}:=\left\{(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in M^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}^{n}\colon a_{j}\neq a_{k}\ \textrm{ for any }j\neq k\ \textrm{ and }\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}=\chi(M)\right\}

For any
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
,
we consider the (unique)
2
-form
\Psi=\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
that satisfies

(3.2)

\begin{cases}-\Delta\Psi=2\pi\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\,\delta_{\mathbf{a}_{j}}-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
\displaystyle\int_{M}\Psi=0\end{cases}

Here
\kappa
is the Gauss curvature of
M
.
The right-hand side of the equation for
\Delta\Psi

has zero average, due to the Gauss-Bonnet theorem
and the assumption that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}=\chi(M)
.
As a consequence, (
3.2
) has a unique solution.
Moreover, elliptic regularity theory implies that

\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in W^{1,p}_{\tan}(M)

for any
p\in(1,\,2)
. Let

\mathfrak{g}:=1-\frac{\chi(M)}{2}

be the genus of
M
, and let
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
be the space
of harmonic
1
-forms on
M
. By Hodge theory,
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

is a real vector space of dimension
2\mathfrak{g}
. We equip
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

with the
L^{2}
norm (but any other norm would do, since
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

is finite-dimensional).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}

and
\xi\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
. We say that a vector field
u_{*}

is a
canonical harmonic field
for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

if
u_{*}\in W^{1,1}_{\tan}(M)
,
\left|u_{*}(x)\right|=1
for a.e.
x\in M
and

(3.3)

j(u_{*})=\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})+\xi

where
\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
is defined by (
3.2
).

Remark 3.1
.

A vector field
u_{*}\in W^{1,1}_{\tan}(M)

is canonical harmonic (for some
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
)
if and only if
\left|u_{*}\right|=1
a.e. in
M
,

\mathrm{d}j(u_{*})=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g},\qquad\mathrm{d}^{*}j(u_{*})=0.

Not any triple
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
admits a canonical harmonic field.
Ignat and Jerrard
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]

characterised the values of
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

that admit a canonical harmonic field; we recall their result.
We say that a set
L\subseteq\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

is an affine lattice if there exists a linear, invertible
map
\alpha\colon\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)\to\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}}
and a vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}}

such that
\alpha(L)=\mathbb{Z}^{2\mathfrak{g}}+b
.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
19
]
)
.

For any
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
, there exists
an affine lattice
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\subseteq\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

such that a canonical harmonic field for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
exists
if and only if

\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

Moreover,

(i)

the canonical harmonic field for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

is essentially unique: any two canonical
harmonic fields
u_{*}
,
u^{*}
for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

satisfy
u^{*}=(\cos\beta)u_{*}+i(\sin\beta)u_{*}
for some
constant
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
;

(ii)

any canonical harmonic field
for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
belongs
to
W^{1,p}_{\tan}(M)
for any
p\in(1,\,2)
and is smooth
in
M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\}
.

Actually, Ignat and Jerrard’s result is stronger: it provides
a characterisation of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

in terms of
\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

(we will recall it later on, in Section
3.2
)
and shows that
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

depends continuously on
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
, in a suitable sense.

Given
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}

and
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
, let
u_{*}

be the (essentially unique) canonical harmonic field for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
.
We define the (intrinsic) renormalized energy of
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
as

(3.4)

W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi):=\lim_{\rho\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{M\setminus\cup_{j=1}^{n}B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left|Du_{*}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}^{2}\left|\log\rho\right|\right)

It can be proved that the limit in (
3.4
) exists and is finite.
What is more, Ignat and Jerrard
[
19
, Proposition 2.4]

gave a characterisation of
W
in terms of the Green function
for the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
M
, thus extending
earlier results by Bethuel, Brezis and Hélein
[
8
]

in the Euclidean setting. Let
\mathrm{Vol}(M):=\int_{M}\mathrm{vol}_{g}
. Let

G\colon\{(x,\,y)\in M\times M\colon x\neq y\}\to\mathbb{R}

be the Green function for the Laplace-Beltrami operator,
i.e. the unique function such that

(3.5)

\begin{cases}-\Delta_{x}(G(x,\,y)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g})=\delta_{y}-\dfrac{\mathrm{vol}_{g}}{\mathrm{Vol}(M)}&\textrm{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(M)\\[7.0pt]
\displaystyle\int_{M}G(x,\,y)\mathrm{vol}_{g}(x)=0\end{cases}

for any
y\in M
. The function
G
may be decomposed as

(3.6)

G(x,\,y)=G_{0}(x,\,y)+H(x,\,y)

where
H\in C^{1}(M\times M)
and

(3.7)

G_{0}(x,\,y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\dist_{g}(x,\,y)\qquad\textrm{if }\dist_{g}(x,\,y)\textrm{ is small enough}

(see e.g.
[
4
, Chapter 4.2]
).
We also define the function
\psi_{0}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
by

(3.8)

\begin{cases}-\Delta\psi_{0}=-\kappa+\dfrac{2\pi\,\chi(M)}{\mathrm{Vol}(M)}\\
\displaystyle\int_{M}\psi_{0}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0\end{cases}

By the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, the right-hand side of this equation
has zero average, so the solution exists and is unique.
By comparing (
3.2
), (
3.5
) and (
3.8
),
we deduce

(3.9)

\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\,G(\cdot,\,a_{j})\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\psi_{0}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

Ignat and Jerrard
[
19
, Proposition 2.4]
proved
the following

Proposition 3.2
.

For any
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
and
any
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
, there holds

\displaystyle W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq n}d_{j}\,d_{k}\,G(a_{j},\,a_{k})+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\pi d_{j}^{2}H(a_{j},\,a_{j})+d_{j}\psi_{0}(a_{j})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|\xi\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\left|\mathrm{d}\psi_{0}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

The goal of this section is to compute the gradient of the
renormalized energy
W
with respect to
\mathbf{a}
. However,
the very definition of the gradient requires some care,
because the set
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
of admissible values
for
\xi
depends on
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
, so the variables

\mathbf{a}
,
\mathbf{d}
and
\xi
are not independent.
Therefore, our first task is to make sure that the
gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W
is well-defined.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
and
any
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
, there exists
an open neighbourhood
U
of
\mathbf{a}
in
M^{n}
and a unique
smooth map
\Xi\colon U\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
such that

\Xi(\mathbf{a})=\xi
and

\Xi(\mathbf{b})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})

for any
\mathbf{b}\in U
.

Lemma
3.3
allows us to interpret the
gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W
with respect to
\mathbf{a}

in a non-ambiguous way: for any
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
and
any
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
, we define

(3.10)

\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi):=\nabla_{\mathbf{b}}\,W(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d},\,\Xi(\mathbf{b}))_{|\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{a}}

where
\Xi
is the map given by Lemma
3.3
.
The gradient
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W
is well-defined now,
because Proposition
3.2
and Lemma
3.3

imply that the map
\mathbf{b}\mapsto W(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d},\,\Xi(\mathbf{b}))

is differentiable (and even smooth).
As it turns out,
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W
can characterised
in terms of canonical harmonic fields.
Given
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
,
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n})
,
we consider geodesic balls
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
of center
a_{j}

and radius
\eta>0
small enough, so that
the closed balls
\bar{B}_{\eta}(a_{j})
are pairwise disjoint.
We denote by
\nu
the exterior unit normal to
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
and
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
.
Let
u_{*}\in W^{1,1}_{\tan}(M)
be the (essentially unique)
canonical harmonic field for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
.
Let
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
be fixed, and
let
e
be a smooth vector field defined in a neighbourhood
U

of
a_{j}
, such that

(3.11)

\mathrm{div}\,e(a_{j})=0\qquad\textrm{in }U.

Then, we have

\begin{split}\lim_{\eta\to 0}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left((D_{e}u_{*},\,D_{\nu}u_{*})_{g}-\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{*}\right|^{2}_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}=\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi),e(a_{j})\right)_{g}\end{split}

In the Euclidean setting, i.e. when
M
is a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
an even stronger statement holds: if
e\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a constant, then

\begin{split}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left((D_{e}u_{*},\,D_{\nu}u_{*})-\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{*}\right|^{2}(\nu,\,e)\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}=\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d}),\,e\right)\end{split}

for any
\eta>0
small enough (see e.g.
[
8
, Theorem VII.4, Theorem VIII.3]

and
[
24
, Theorem 5.1]
). In our setting, we need to take the
limit as
\eta\to 0
to compensate for curvature effects.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Proposition
3.4
.
We distinguish two cases, according to the genus of
M
.

3.1.
The case
\mathfrak{g}=0

First, we suppose that
M
has genus
\mathfrak{g}=0
,
that is,
M
is diffeomorphic to a sphere
(although the Riemannian metric on
M
is arbitrary).
In this case, the problem simplifies
because
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)=0
, so
W
is a function
of
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
only,
W=W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
.
Let us fix
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
.
Let
\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
be the
2
-form defined by (
3.2
).
For any
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
, we define the auxiliary
function
S_{j}\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
as

(3.12)

S_{j}:=\star\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})-2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,\,a_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Phiad-Green}}}{{=}}2\pi\sum_{k\neq j}d_{k}G(\cdot,\,a_{k})+\psi_{0}

The function
S_{j}
is smooth in a neighbourhood of
a_{j}

and hence, it may be interpreted as the ‘regular part’
of
\star\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
near
a_{j}
.

By Equation (
3.6
), we know that
the Green function
G
may be decomposed into
a ‘singular part’, which blows up logarithmically
near the diagonal of
M\times M
, and a ‘regular part’
H\in C^{1}(M\times M)
.
Since
G
is symmetric in
x
and
y

(see e.g.
[
4
, Theorem 4.13]
), we deduce

H(x,\,y)=H(y,\,x)

for any
x\in M
,
y\in M
such that
\dist_{g}(x,\,y)
is small enough.
As a consequence, in case
x=y
, the gradients of
H

with respect to each argument are equal:

(3.13)

\nabla_{x}H(x,\,x)=\nabla_{y}H(x,\,x)

We will write
\nabla H(x,\,x):=\nabla_{x}H(x,\,x)=\nabla_{y}H(x,\,x)
.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
, we have

\begin{split}\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})=2\pi d_{j}\left(\nabla S_{j}(a_{j})+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla H(a_{j},\,a_{j})\right)\end{split}

Proof.

Let
j
be fixed, and let
\mathbf{a}^{*}:=(a_{k})_{k\neq j}
.
By Proposition
3.2
, we may write
the renormalized energy as

W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})=4\pi^{2}\,d_{j}\sum_{k\neq j}d_{k}\,G(a_{j},\,a_{k})+2\pi d_{j}\,\psi_{0}(a_{j})+2\pi^{2}d_{j}^{2}\,H(a_{j},\,a_{j})+C(\mathbf{a}^{*}),

where
C(\mathbf{a}^{*})
is a suitable
function of
\mathbf{a}^{*}
, which does not depend on
a_{j}
.
Using the definition of
S_{j}
, i.e. Equation (
3.12
), we may also write

(3.14)

W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})=2\pi d_{j}\left(S_{j}(a_{j})+\pi d_{j}\,H(a_{j},\,a_{j})\right)+C(\mathbf{a}^{*})

It only remains to differentiate (
3.14
)
with respect to
a_{j}
. For any
x\in M
, we have

\nabla_{x}\left(H(x,\,x)\right)=\nabla_{x}H(x,\,x)+\nabla_{y}H(x,\,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{symmH}}}{{=}}2\nabla H(x,\,x)

and hence, the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Let
e
be a smooth, divergence-free vector field, defined in
a neighbourhood of
a_{j}
. Let

I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j}):=\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(D_{\nu}u_{*},\,D_{e}u_{*})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left|Du_{*}\right|^{2}_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

The definition (
2.62
) of
j(u_{*})

implies, via standard computations, that

\left|Du_{*}\right|_{g}^{2}=\left|j(u_{*})\right|_{g}^{2}
,
j(u_{*})=\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi
and

D_{v}u_{*}=j(u_{*})(v)\,iu_{*}
for any smooth vector field
v
.
Moreover,
j(u_{*})=\mathrm{d}\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
by
Definition
3.1
.
For simplicity, we write
\Psi
instead of
\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
from now on.
Then, we have

(3.15)

\begin{split}I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})=\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left|\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi\right|^{2}_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

Now,

\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi=-\star\mathrm{d}(\star\Psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{S_j}}}{{=}}-\star\mathrm{d}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j}))

and thus

(3.16)

\begin{split}|\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi|^{2}_{g}&=|\mathrm{d}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j}))|^{2}_{g}=|\nabla(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j}))|^{2}_{g}\\
&=|\nabla S_{j}|^{2}_{g}+4\pi^{2}d_{j}^{2}\,|\nabla G(\cdot,a_{j})|^{2}_{g}+4\pi d_{j}\,(\nabla S_{j},\,\nabla G(\cdot,a_{j}))_{g}\end{split}

On the other hand, for any vector field
X
we have

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(X)=-\star\mathrm{d}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j}))\,(X)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:grad_ruotato}}}{{=}}-(i\nabla(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j})),\,X)\\
&\hskip 7.11317pt=(\nabla(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j})),\,iX)\end{split}

because in the tangent plane at each point,
i
is a rotation
and its adjoint is equal to
i^{-1}=-i
. As a consequence,

(3.17)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)&=\nabla_{i\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(x,a_{j}))\,\nabla_{ie}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(x,a_{j}))\\
&=\nabla_{i\nu}S_{j}(\cdot)\,\nabla_{ie}S_{j}(\cdot)+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}S_{j}(\cdot)\,\nabla_{ie}G(\cdot,a_{j})\\
&\qquad+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}G(\cdot,a_{j})\,\nabla_{ie}S_{j}(\cdot)+4\pi^{2}d_{j}^{2}\,\nabla_{i\nu}G(\cdot,a_{j})\,\nabla_{ie}G(\cdot,a_{j})\end{split}

We write the Green function
G
in the form (
3.6
).
For ease of notation, we let
L_{j}:=\log\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})

and
H_{j}:=H(\cdot,\,a_{j})
, so that

2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,\,a_{j})=-d_{j}L_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j}

The equations (
3.16
) and (
3.17
)
imply respectively

(3.18)

\begin{split}|\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi|^{2}_{g}&=\left|\nabla S_{j}\right|^{2}_{g}+d_{j}^{2}\left|\nabla L_{j}\right|^{2}_{g}+4\pi^{2}d_{j}^{2}\left|\nabla H_{j}\right|^{2}_{g}\\
&\qquad-4\pi d_{j}^{2}\,(\nabla L_{j},\,\nabla H_{j})_{g}-2d_{j}\,(\nabla S_{j},\,\nabla L_{j})_{g}+4\pi d_{j}\,(\nabla S_{j},\,\nabla H_{j})_{g}\end{split}

and

(3.19)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)&=\nabla_{i\nu}S_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}S_{j}-d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}S_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}L_{j}+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}S_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}H_{j}\\
&\qquad-d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}L_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}H_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}S_{j}+d_{j}^{2}\,\nabla_{i\nu}L_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}L_{j}\\
&\qquad-2\pi d_{j}^{2}\,\nabla_{i\nu}L_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}H_{j}-2\pi d_{j}^{2}\,\nabla_{i\nu}H_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}L_{j}+4\pi^{2}d_{j}^{2}\,\nabla_{i\nu}H_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}H_{j}\end{split}

The function
H
is of class
C^{1}
in
M\times M

and, by Equation (
3.12
),
S_{j}
is smooth
in a neighbourhood of
a_{j}
. In particular, both
\nabla H_{j}

and
\nabla S_{j}
are locally bounded around
a_{j}
.
Therefore, we can write Equations (
3.18
)
and (
3.18
) as

(3.20)

\left|\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi\right|^{2}_{g}=d_{j}^{2}\left|\nabla L_{j}\right|^{2}_{g}-2d_{j}(\nabla(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j}),\,\nabla L_{j})_{g}+\mathrm{O}(1)

and

(3.21)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)=d_{j}^{2}\,\nabla_{i\nu}L_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}L_{j}&-d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\,\nabla_{ie}L_{j}\\
&-d_{j}\,\nabla_{i\nu}L_{j}\,\nabla_{ie}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

respectively. Gauss’ lemma implies that

(3.22)

\nabla\left(\log\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})\right)=\frac{\nabla\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})}{\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})}=\frac{\nu}{\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})}\qquad\textrm{in }B_{\eta}(a_{j})\setminus\{a_{j}\}

so on the circle
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
we have

\nabla L_{j}=\nu/\eta
,
\nabla_{i\nu}L_{j}=0
and

(3.23)

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{j}^{2}}{\eta^{2}}-\frac{2d_{j}}{\eta}\nabla_{\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})+\mathrm{O}(1)

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)

\displaystyle=-\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}\nabla_{i\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})(\nu,\,ie)+\mathrm{O}(1)

By substituting (
3.23
), (
3.24
)
in (
3.15
), we obtain

(3.25)

\begin{split}I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})=&-\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\nabla_{i\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\,(\nu,\,ie)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&+\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\nabla_{\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\,(\nu,\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{d_{j}^{2}}{2\eta^{2}}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\mathrm{O}(\eta)\end{split}

The properties of
i
imply
(\nu,\,ie)_{g}=-(i\nu,\,e)_{g}
and thus

(3.26)

\begin{split}&-\nabla_{i\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\,(\nu,\,ie)_{g}+\nabla_{\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\,(\nu,\,e)_{g}\\
&\qquad=\big(\nabla_{\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\nu,\,e\big)_{g}+\big(\nabla_{i\nu}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})i\nu,\,e\big)_{g}=(\nabla(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j}),\,e)_{g}.\end{split}

Moreover, by integrating by parts in the last term of (
3.25
) (see (
2.25
)),
we obtain

(3.27)

\begin{split}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}=\int_{B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(\mathrm{div}\,e)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\mathrm{O}(\eta^{3}),\end{split}

because
\left|\mathrm{div}\,e\right|=\mathrm{O}(\eta)
in
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
as
\mathrm{div}\,e(a_{j})=0
(recall (
3.11
)) and
\mathrm{div}\,e
is smooth.
Combining (
3.25
), (
3.27
)
and (
3.26
), we deduce

(3.28)

\begin{split}I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})&=\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\nabla_{e}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}H_{j})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\mathrm{O}(\eta)\end{split}

and, since
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j}))=2\pi\eta+\mathrm{O}(\eta^{2})

(see e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 10]
), we conclude that

(3.29)

\lim_{\eta\to 0}I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})=2\pi d_{j}\left(\nabla S_{j}(a_{j})+2\pi d_{j}\nabla H(a_{j},\,a_{j}),\,e(a_{j})\right)_{g}

The result follows by Lemma
3.5
.
∎

3.2.
The case
\mathfrak{g}>0

When
\mathfrak{g}>0
, the very definition of
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W
requires some care,
as the variables
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
are not independent
— they are related to each other by the constraint

\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
. Our first task is to prove
Lemma
3.3
, which allows us to define
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W

unambigously. First of all, we recall some results
from
[
19
]
which characterise
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
.
We choose closed, simple, geodesic
curves
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{2\mathfrak{g}}
in
M

whose homology classes generate the first homology group
H_{1}(M;\,\mathbb{Z})
.
(Such curves exist; see e.g.
[
19
, Lemma 5.2]
).
Then, the map

(3.30)

\alpha\colon\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)\to\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}},\qquad\alpha_{k}(\xi):=\int_{\gamma_{k}}\xi\quad\textrm{for }k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{g}\}

is linear and invertible, essentially becuase of
Hodge theory and de Rham’s theorem
(for a detailed argument, see
[
19
, Lemma 5.2]
).
Let
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
. Suppose first that for any
j
and
k
,
the curve
\gamma_{k}
does not contain the point
a_{j}
.
For any
k
, we choose an orthogonal tangent frame
\{\tau_{1,k},\,\tau_{2,k}\}

defined in a neighbourhood of
\gamma_{k}
.
Let
\mathcal{A}_{k}
be the connection
1
-form induced
by
\{\tau_{1,k},\,\tau_{2,k}\}
, as in (
2.32
). We define

(3.31)

\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d}):=\int_{\gamma_{k}}\left(\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})+\mathcal{A}_{k}\right)\qquad\textrm{for }k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{g}\},

where
\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
is the
2
-form defined by (
3.2
).
The number
\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
depends on
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, and hence
on the choice of the frame. However, if
\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\prime}
is another
connection arising from a different frame, then

\int_{\gamma_{k}}\mathcal{A}_{k}\equiv\int_{\gamma_{k}}\mathcal{A}_{k}^{\prime}\qquad\mod 2\pi

(see
[
19
, Lemma 6.2]
).
Therefore,
\zeta
is well-defined (and, as it turns out, continuous)
as a map
\zeta\colon\mathscr{A}^{n}\to(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2\mathfrak{g}}
.
If for some
j
and
k
the curve
\gamma_{k}
contains
a_{j}
, then we consider
a small perturbation
\lambda_{k}
of
\gamma_{k}
, which is still
a smooth, closed, simple curve, is homologous to
\gamma_{k}

but avoids all the points
a_{j}
. We define
\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

by integrating over
\lambda_{k}
instead of
\gamma_{k}
.
The set
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
in Theorem
3.1

is defined in terms of
\alpha
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
,
as follows:

(3.32)

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d}):=\left\{\xi\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)\colon\alpha(\xi)+\zeta(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2\mathfrak{g}}\right\}

Now, we proceed towards the proof of Lemma
3.3
.
For any
a\in M
and any
v\in\mathrm{T}_{a}M
, we consider the function

\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v)\colon M\setminus\{a\}\to\mathbb{R}
,

(3.33)

\sigma(x,\,a,\,v):=\left(\nabla_{a}G(x,\,a),\,v\right)\qquad\textrm{for }x\in M\setminus\{a\}

(where
\nabla_{a}G
denotes the gradient of the Green function
with respect to its second argument). The function

\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v)
is harmonic, and hence smooth,
in
M\setminus\{a\}
. Indeed, let
B_{\eta}(a)
be a small
geodesic disk centered at
a
, and
let
\gamma\colon(-\delta,\,\delta)\to M
be a smooth curve
with
\gamma(0)=a
,
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=v
. Since the Green
function
G
is smooth away from the diagonal of
M\times M
,
for any
\eta>0
we have

(3.34)

\frac{G(\cdot,\,\gamma(t))-G(\cdot,\,a)}{t}\to\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v)\qquad\textrm{in }C^{1}(M\setminus B_{\eta}(a))\ \textrm{ as }t\to 0

However, the function
G(\cdot,\,\gamma(t))-G(\cdot,\,a)

is harmonic in
M\setminus\bar{B}_{\eta}(a)
for
t
small enough.
By taking the (distributional) Laplacian in both sides
of (
3.34
), we deduce that
\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v)

is harmonic in
M\setminus\bar{B}_{\eta}(a)
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
, there exists
an open neighbourhood
V
of
\mathbf{a}
in
M^{n}
such that
the map
V\to(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2\mathfrak{g}}
,
\mathbf{b}\mapsto\zeta(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})

has a smooth lifting
V\to\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}}
, which we still
denote
\zeta(\cdot,\,\mathbf{d})

by abuse of notation. Moreover, for any
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
,
any
k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{g}\}

and any
v\in T_{a_{j}}M
, there holds

\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d}),\,v\right)_{g}=-2\pi d_{j}\,\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)

where
\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)
is defined by (
3.33
).

Proof.

Let
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
be fixed. As we observed before,
up to a perturbation we may assume that for each
k
and
j
,
the curve
\gamma_{k}
does not contain
a_{j}
.
We take a small open neighbourhood
V

of
\mathbf{a}
in
M^{n}
such that
V\times\{\mathbf{d}\}\subseteq\mathscr{A}^{n}

— that is, for any
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\,\ldots,\,b_{n})\in V
,
the points
b_{j}
are distinct; this is possible because
the points
a_{j}
themselves are distinct. By taking
V
small enough,
we can also make sure that for any
k
,
j
and any
\mathbf{b}\in V
,

\gamma_{k}
does not contain
b_{j}
.
For each
k
, we
fix
an orthonormal tangent frame
defined in a neighbourhood of
\gamma_{k}
and let
A_{k}

be the corresponding connection
1
-form.
Then, Equation (
3.31
) defines unambigously a map

\mathbf{b}\in V\mapsto\zeta(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}}
.
We claim that this map is smooth. By writing
\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

in terms of the Green function, as in (
3.9
),
and observing that
\mathrm{d}^{*}(f\mathrm{vol}_{g})=-\star\mathrm{d}\star(f\mathrm{vol}_{g})=-\star\mathrm{d}f

for any function
f
, we obtain

(3.35)

\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})=-2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}G(\cdot,\,b_{j})-\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}\psi_{0}+\int_{\gamma_{k}}A_{k}

where
\psi_{0}
is the smooth function defined by (
3.8
).
Since we have assumed that the curves
\gamma_{k}

do not contain any point
b_{j}
for
\mathbf{b}\in V
, the Green
function
G(\cdot,\,b_{j})
is smooth in both its arguments,
and the smoothness of
\zeta_{k}
follows.

Let
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
be fixed.
To compute the derivative of
\nabla_{a_{j}}\zeta_{k}
,
we fix a vector
v\in T_{a_{j}}M
and take a smooth curve

\gamma\colon(-\delta,\,\delta)\to M
such that

\gamma(0)=a_{j}
,
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=v
.
Let
\mathbf{b}(t)=(a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{j-1},\,\gamma(t),\,a_{j+1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n})
.
By smoothness of
G
, we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}_{|t=0}\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{b}(t),\,\mathbf{d})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{zeta-Green}}}{{=}}-2\pi d_{j}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}_{|t=0}\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}G(\cdot,\,\gamma(t))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Green-conv}}}{{=}}-2\pi d_{j}\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)

and the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.

Let
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
. By Lemma
3.6
,
there exists an open neighbourhood
V
of
\mathbf{a}

in
M^{n}
such that the function
\zeta(\cdot,\,\mathbf{d})\colon V\to\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}}

is smooth. Let
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
.
We need to find a neighbourhood
U\subseteq V
of
\mathbf{a}

and a smooth map
\Xi\colon U\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

such that
\Xi(\mathbf{a})=\xi
and

(3.36)

\alpha\Xi(\mathbf{b})+\zeta(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2\mathfrak{g}}\qquad\textrm{for any }\mathbf{b}\in U

Here
\alpha
is the linear map defined by (
3.30
).
(Equation (
3.36
) is equivalent to
\Xi(\mathbf{b})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})
,
due to (
3.32
).) As
(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2\mathfrak{g}}
is discrete,
(
3.36
) is equivalent to require
that
\mathbf{b}\mapsto\alpha\Xi(\mathbf{b})+\zeta(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})

be constant, that is

(3.37)
