\alpha\Xi(\mathbf{b})+\zeta(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})=\alpha\xi+\zeta(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\qquad\textrm{for any }\mathbf{b}\in U

Since
\alpha
is invertible
[
19
, Lemma 5.2]
,
the existence, uniqueness and regularity of a map
\Xi

satisfying (
3.37
) follow by the implicit function theorem.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

For any index
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}

and any
v\in T_{a_{j}}M
, the directional
derivative
\Xi^{\prime}_{j,\mathbf{a},v}:=(\nabla_{a_{j}}\Xi(\mathbf{a}),\,v)_{g}

may be computed by differentiating (
3.37
):

\Xi^{\prime}_{j,\mathbf{a},v}=-\alpha^{-1}\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}\zeta(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d}),\,v\right)_{g}

Recalling the definition of
\alpha
,
i.e. Equation (
3.30
), and Lemma
3.6
,
we deduce that
\Xi^{\prime}_{j,\mathbf{a},v}\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

is the unique harmonic
1
-form that satisfies

(3.38)

\int_{\gamma_{k}}\Xi^{\prime}_{j,\mathbf{a},v}=-\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}\zeta_{k}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d}),\,v\right)_{g}=2\pi d_{j}\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)

for any
k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{g}\}
. This characterisation
will be useful in the proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Before we proceed to the proof of Proposition
3.4
,
we point out a few consequences of Lemma
3.3
.

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}

and
\xi^{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathrm{d})
. Then, the following properties hold.

(i)

For any continuous map
\mathbf{a}\colon[0,\,T]\to M^{n}

such that
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0}
and
(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
for any
t
,
there exists a unique continuous map
\xi\colon[0,\,T]\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

such that

(3.39)

\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d})\quad\textrm{for any }t\in[0,\,T],\qquad\xi(0)=\xi^{0}

(ii)

The (forward-in-time) Cauchy problem

(3.40)

\begin{cases}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{a}(t)=-\dfrac{1}{\pi}\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))\\[11.38092pt]
\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d})\\
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0},\qquad\xi(0)=\xi^{0}\end{cases}

has a
unique
maximal solution
(\mathbf{a},\,\xi)
,
defined in an interval
[0,\,t^{*})
.

Proof.

Let
U\subseteq M^{n}
be a neighbourhood of
\mathbf{a}^{0}

and
\Xi\colon U\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

a smooth map such that

\Xi(\mathbf{b})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})\quad\textrm{for any }\mathbf{b}\in U,\qquad\Xi(\mathbf{a}^{0})=\xi^{0},

as given by Lemma
3.3
.
We prove (i) first. Let
\mathbf{a}\colon[0,\,T]\to M^{n}

be a continuous map such that
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0}

and
(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
for any
t
.
Then, there exists
\tau>0
such that

\xi(t):=\Xi(\mathbf{a}(t))
is well-defined and continuous
for
t\in[0,\,\tau)
. By construction,
\xi
satisfies (
3.39
).
If
\tilde{\xi}\colon[0,\,\tau)\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
is another
continuous map that satisfies (
3.39
),
then (
3.32
) implies

\tilde{\xi}(t)-\xi(t)\in\alpha^{-1}\left(2\pi\mathbb{Z}\right)^{2\mathfrak{g}}

for any
t\in[0,\,\tau)
, where
\alpha\colon\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)\to\mathbb{R}^{2\mathfrak{g}}

is given by (
3.30
). As
\tilde{\xi}-\xi
is continuous
and
(2\pi\mathbb{Z})^{2\mathfrak{g}}
is discrete, we obtain
\tilde{\xi}=\xi
.
Now, (i) follows by a covering argument.

Next, we prove (ii). We define

Z(\mathbf{b}):=W(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d},\,\Xi(\mathbf{b}))
for
\mathbf{b}\in U
.
We consider the (forward-in-time) Cauchy problem

(3.41)

\begin{cases}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{a}(t)=-\dfrac{1}{\pi}\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}Z(\mathbf{a}(t))\\[11.38092pt]
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0},\end{cases}

If
\mathbf{a}
is a solution of (
3.41
),
then
(\mathbf{a},\,\xi:=\Xi(\mathbf{a}))
is a solution of (
3.40
).
Conversely, if
(\mathbf{a},\,\xi)
is a solution of (
3.40
)
then
\mathbf{a}
is a solution of (
3.41
).
The function
Z
is smooth, so local existence and uniqueness
of a solution for (
3.41
) (and hence, (
3.40
))
is given by the classical Cauchy-Lipschitz theory
for ordinary differential equations.
∎

We move on towards the proof of Proposition
3.4
.
As a first step, we need to understand the local behaviour of the
function
\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v)
defined by (
3.33
)
in a neighbourhood of
a
. In turns, this requires some
information on the Green function
G
and its regular part
H
,
defined in (
3.6
).

Lemma 3.8
.

The function
H\in C^{1}(M\times M)
in (
3.6
)
satisfies

(3.42)

\left|\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}H(x,\,y)\right|_{g}\lesssim 1+\left|\log\dist_{g}(x,\,y)\right|

for any
(x,\,y)\in M\times M
such that
x\neq y
.

Proof.

The proof relies on a representation formula for the Green function
on compact manifolds, given in
[
4
, Theorem 4.13]
.
For
(x,\,y)\in M\times M
with
x\neq y
, we define

(3.43)

G_{0}(x,\,y):=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log\dist_{g}(x,\,y)\,f(\dist_{g}(x,\,y)),

where
f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}[0,\,+\infty)
is a cut-off
function, such that
f(t)=1
for
t
sufficiently small
(say,
t
is smaller than half the injectivity radius of
M
)
and
f(t)=0
for
t
larger than the injectivity radius of
M
.
We define

(3.44)

\Gamma_{1}(x,\,y):=-\Delta_{x}G_{0}(x,\,y)+\delta_{y}

(i.e.,
\Gamma_{1}
is the absolutely continuous part of
the distributional Laplacian of
G_{0}
in its first argument).
By working in geodesic coordinates centered at
y
,
we obtain (see
[
4
, § 4.9]
)

(3.45)

\Gamma_{1}(x,\,y)=\frac{1}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,y)}\partial_{\nu}\left(\log\sqrt{\det g(x)}\right)

where
\partial_{\nu}
denotes the derivative in the radial direction.
The metric tensor in geodesic coordinates satisfies

g(x)=\Id+\mathrm{O}(\dist_{g}(x,\,y)^{2})
,
so
\Gamma_{1}
is bounded. We define
inductively the sequence of functions
\Gamma_{k}
as

\Gamma_{k+1}(x,\,y):=\int_{M}\Gamma_{k}(x,\,z)\,\Gamma_{1}(z,\,y)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}(z)\qquad\textrm{for }k\geq 1,\ (x,\,y)\in M\times M,\ x\neq y.

An argument by induction, see
[
4
, Proposition 4.12]
,
shows that
\Gamma_{k}
extends to a function of class
C^{k-1}(M\times M)

for
k\geq 2
. Now,
H
can be written in the form

(3.46)

H(x,\,y)=G(x,\,y)-G_{0}(x,\,y)=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\int_{M}\Gamma_{k}(x,\,z)\,G_{0}(z,\,y)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}(z)+F(x,\,y),

for some function
F
that satisfies

(3.47)

\Delta_{x}F(x,\,y)=\Gamma_{3}(x,\,y)+\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}(M)}

(see Equations (17) and (18) in
[
4
, Theorem 4.13]
).
By differentiating (
3.47
) with respect to
y
, we obtain

\Delta_{x}\nabla_{y}F(x,\,y)=\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{3}(x,\,y)

Since
\Gamma_{3}
is of class
C^{2}
, Schauder’s estimates
implies that
\nabla_{y}F(\cdot,\,y)\in C^{2,\beta}(M)

for any
\beta\in(0,\,1)
and any
y\in M
.
Moreover, by differentiating (
3.43
) and (
3.44
), we see that

(3.48)

\left|\nabla_{x}\Gamma_{1}(x,\,y)\right|_{g}+\left|\nabla_{y}G_{0}(x,\,y)\right|_{g}\lesssim\dist_{g}(x,\,y)^{-1}

while on the other hand
\left|\nabla_{x}\Gamma_{2}(x,\,y)\right|_{g}\leq C

because
\Gamma_{2}
is of class
C^{1}
. Equation (
3.45
) gives

(3.49)

\begin{split}\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}H(x,\,y)=\sum_{k=1}^{2}\int_{M}\nabla_{x}\Gamma_{k}(x,\,z)\,\nabla_{y}G_{0}(z,\,y)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}(z)+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

The estimate (
3.42
) follows from (
3.48
) and (
3.49
) —
see e.g.
[
4
, Proposition 4.12]
for the details.
∎

Given a point
a\in M
, we will denote by
\nu

the unit vector field, locally defined
in a neighbourhood of
a
except at the point
a
itself,
that is orthogonal to the geodesic circles centered at
a

and points outward.

Lemma 3.9
.

For any
a\in M
, we have

D\nu=\frac{1}{\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a)}(i\nu)\otimes(i\nu)^{\flat}+\mathrm{O}(1)

in a neighbourhood of
a
, except at the point
a
itself.

The operator
(i\nu)\otimes(i\nu)^{\flat}\colon TM\to TM

is, at each point
p\in M
, the othogonal projection
onto the line spanned by
i\nu(p)
. Actually, the covariant
derivative of
D\nu
is related to the Hessian of the function

\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a)^{2}
. There are results in the literature
which provide a fine description of the Hessian
of
\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a)^{2}
and hence, of
D\nu

(see e.g.
[
31
, Chapter VI]
).
However, the statement given here is enough for our purposes.
We include a proof, for the sake of convenience.

Proof of Lemma
3.9
.

We work in geodesic normal coordinates centered at
a
.
By abuse of notation, we identify
x
with a point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

and
a
with the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, by Gauss’ lemma,
we can write
\dist_{g}(x,\,a)=\left|x\right|
and

\nu(x)=x/\left|x\right|
. Let
\Gamma^{k}_{ij}
denote
the Christoffel symbols in normal coordinates.
For any smooth vector field
w
, we have

\begin{split}D_{w}\nu&=\left(w^{j}\frac{\partial\nu^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}+\Gamma^{k}_{ij}\nu^{i}w^{j}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}\\
&=w^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\left(\frac{x^{k}}{\left|x\right|}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}+\mathrm{O}(1)=\frac{w^{j}}{\left|x\right|}\left(\delta_{j}^{k}-\nu^{k}\nu^{j}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

As the metric tensor, in normal coordinates,
satisfies
g_{ij}(x)=\delta_{ij}+\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|^{2})
, we deduce

\begin{split}D_{w}\nu&=\frac{1}{\left|x\right|}\left(w-(\nu,\,w)_{g}\nu\right)+\mathrm{O}(1)=\frac{1}{\left|x\right|}(i\nu,\,w)_{g}\,i\nu+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

and the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
a\in M
. Let
v
be a smooth vector field, locally defined
in a neighbourhood of
a
.
Then, the function
\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v(a))

defined by (
3.33
) satisfies

(3.50)

\displaystyle\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v(a))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a)}\left(\nu,\,v\right)_{g}+\mathrm{O}(1)

(3.51)

\displaystyle\star\mathrm{d}\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v(a))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a)^{2}}\left((i\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\nu^{\flat}+(\nu,\,v)_{g}\star\nu^{\flat}\right)+\mathrm{O}(\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a)^{-1})

Proof.

For simplicity, we write
\sigma
instead
of
\sigma(\cdot,\,a,\,v(a))
.
Recalling the definition of
\sigma
, and writing the Green function
in the form (
3.6
), we obtain

(3.52)

\sigma(x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\left(\nabla_{a}\log\dist_{g}(x,\,a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}+\left(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}

The term
(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a))_{g}
is
\mathrm{O}(1)

because
H
is of class
C^{1}
in both variables.
We compute
\nabla_{a}\log\dist_{g}(x,\,a)
.
By Gauss’ lemma,
\nabla_{a}\dist_{g}(x,\,a)=\tilde{\nu}(a)\in T_{a}M
,
where
\tilde{\nu}
is the unit vector field
that is orthogonal to the geodesic circles centered at
x

and points outward. By the chain rule, we obtain

(3.53)

\begin{split}\sigma(x)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)}\left(\tilde{\nu}(a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}+\left(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}\end{split}

If
x
and
a
are close enough, there exists
a unique minimizing geodesic
\gamma\colon[0,\,1]\to M

such that
\gamma(0)=a
,
\gamma(1)=x
.
Let
\tilde{\gamma}\colon[0,\,1]\to M

be the geodesic defined by
\tilde{\gamma}(t):=\gamma(1-t)
.
Let
P_{x,a}\colon T_{x}M\to T_{a}M
denote parallel
transport along
\tilde{\gamma}
(see e.g.
[
39
, Chapter 6, p. 234]
).
Then,

\tilde{\nu}(a)=\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(1)=P_{x,a}\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}(0)=-P_{x,a}\gamma^{\prime}(1)=-P_{x,a}\nu(x)

and hence, (
3.53
) can be written as

(3.54)

\begin{split}\sigma(x)&=\frac{1}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)}\left(P_{x,a}\nu(x),\,v(a)\right)_{g}+\left(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}\end{split}

The parallel transport map
P_{x,a}
is an isometry,
and its inverse
P_{a,x}:=P_{x,a}^{-1}\colon T_{a}M\to T_{x}M

is the parallel tranport along
\gamma
. Then, (
3.54
)
is equivalent to

(3.55)

\begin{split}\sigma(x)&=\frac{1}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)}\left(\nu(x),\,P_{a,x}v(a)\right)_{g}+\left(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}\ \end{split}

Since both
v
and
x\mapsto P_{a,x}v(a)
are smooth,
with
P_{a,a}v(a)=v(a)
, we have

(3.56)

\left|P_{a,x}v(a)-v(x)\right|_{g}=\mathrm{O}(\dist_{g}(x,\,a))

and hence, (
3.50
) follows.

We pass to the proof of (
3.51
).
By differentiating (
3.55
), we obtain

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\left(\frac{\nu(x)}{\dist_{g}(x,\,a)},\,P_{a,x}v(a)\right)_{g}+\mathrm{d}\left(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a)\right)_{g}\end{split}

Here and throughout the rest of the proof,

\mathrm{d}
denotes the exterior differential with respect to
x
.
By Lemma
3.8
,
\mathrm{d}(\nabla_{a}H(x,\,a),\,v(a))_{g}=\mathrm{O}(\log\dist_{g}(x,\,a))
. Moreover, the vector
field
x\mapsto P_{a,x}v(a)
is smooth (in particular,
its derivatives are bounded). Therefore,

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)&=-\frac{(\nu(x),\,P_{a,x}v(a))_{g}}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\left(\dist_{g}(x,\,a)\right)+\frac{(D\nu(x),\,P_{a,x}v(a))}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)}+\mathrm{O}(\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{-1})\\
&=-\frac{(\nu(x),\,P_{a,x}v(a))_{g}}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{2}}\,\nu^{\flat}(x)+\frac{(i\nu(x),\,P_{a,x}v(a))_{g}}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{2}}(i\nu(x))^{\flat}+\mathrm{O}(\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{-1})\end{split}

The second equality follows by Gauss’ lemma
and Lemma
3.9
.
Finally, by applying (
3.56
) and Lemma
2.1
,
we deduce

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma(x)&=-\frac{(\nu(x),\,v(x))_{g}}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{2}}\,\nu^{\flat}(x)+\frac{(i\nu(x),\,v(x))_{g}}{2\pi\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{2}}\star\nu^{\flat}(x)+\mathrm{O}(\dist_{g}(x,\,a)^{-1})\end{split}

and the lemma follows.
∎

Our next result gives a characterisation of
\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)

in terms of
\xi
,
H
and the function
S_{j}
defined in (
3.12
).

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
,
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})
.
For any
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
, we have

\begin{split}\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)=2\pi d_{j}\left(\nabla S_{j}(a_{j})+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla H(a_{j},\,a_{j})+i\xi^{\#}(a_{j})\right)\end{split}

Proof.

We consider the expression for
W
given by Proposition
3.2
.
We computed already, in Lemma
3.5
, the gradient
of the terms that do not depend on
\xi
; now, we
focus our attention on the term that depends on
\xi
.
We take a tangent vector
v\in T_{a_{j}}M
.
We consider the smooth map
\mathbf{b}\mapsto\Xi(\mathbf{b})
,
defined locally in a neighbourhood of
\mathbf{a}
in
M^{n}
,
such that
\Xi(\mathbf{a})=\xi
and
\Xi(\mathbf{b})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{b},\,\mathbf{d})

for any
\mathbf{b}
, as given by Lemma
3.3
.
It suffices to prove that

(3.57)

\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}\int_{M}\left|\Xi(\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g},\,v\right)_{g}=2\pi d_{j}\left(i\xi^{\#}(a_{j}),\,v\right)_{g}

Combining (
3.57
) with Lemma
3.5
,
the result will follow.

Step 1
.

Let
\gamma_{1}
, …,
\gamma_{2\mathfrak{g}}
is a family of
simple, closed, smooth curves whose homology classes generate
the homology group
H_{1}(M;\,\mathbb{Z})
.
As before, we assume (up to a perturbation of the
\gamma_{k}
’s)
that the curves
\gamma_{k}
do not contain any of the points
a_{j}
.
Let
\Xi^{\prime}:=(\nabla_{a_{j}}\Xi(\mathbf{a}),\,v)_{g}
be the derivative
of
\Xi
with respect to the
a_{j}
-variable, in the direction of
v
.
We have seen in Remark
3.2
, Equation (
3.38
),
that
\Xi^{\prime}
is the unique harmonic
1
-form such that

(3.58)

\int_{\gamma_{k}}\Xi^{\prime}=2\pi d_{j}\int_{\gamma_{k}}\star\mathrm{d}\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)

for any
k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{g}\}
,
where
\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)
is given by (
3.33
).
From now on, we write
\sigma
instead of
\sigma(\cdot,\,a_{j},\,v)
.
Since
\sigma
is smooth in
M\setminus\{a_{j}\}
, the
1
-form

(3.59)

\beta:=\frac{\Xi^{\prime}}{2\pi d_{j}}-\star\mathrm{d}\sigma

is smooth in
M\setminus\{a_{j}\}
and satisfies

(3.60)

\int_{\gamma_{k}}\beta=0\qquad\textrm{for any }k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,2\mathfrak{g}\}

due to (
3.58
). By differentiating (
3.59
),
and keeping in mind that a harmonic form is closed, we obtain

\mathrm{d}\beta=-\mathrm{d}\star\mathrm{d}\sigma=\star\mathrm{d}^{*}\mathrm{d}\sigma=\star\Delta\sigma=0\qquad\textrm{in }M\setminus\{a_{j}\},

i.e.
\beta
is closed in
M\setminus\{a_{j}\}
.
Moreover, the homology classes of the curves
\gamma_{1}
,   …,
\gamma_{2\mathfrak{g}}

are also generators of
H_{1}(M\setminus\{a_{j}\};\,\mathbb{Z})
,
because the inclusion
M\setminus\{a_{j}\}\hookrightarrow M

induces a group isomorphism
H_{1}(M\setminus\{a_{j}\};\,\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(M;\,\mathbb{Z})
.
This may be seen by writing the surface
M
as a quotient of a polygon
(more precisely, a
(4\mathfrak{g})
-gon) with suitable identifications
on the boundary; see e.g.
[
26
, Chapter 1, Section 5]

and Figure
1
.

M\setminus\{a_{j}\}
retracts by deformation
onto the
1
-skeleton of
M
and
M
is obtained by attaching
a
2
-cell along a trivial cycle of the
1
-skeleton,
so
H_{1}(M\setminus\{a_{j}\};\,\mathbb{Z})\simeq H_{1}(M;\,\mathbb{Z})
.
1
1

1

An
alternative argument: there is an exact sequence

H_{2}(M;\,\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(M,\,M\setminus\{a_{j}\};\,\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(M\setminus\{a_{j}\};\,\mathbb{Z})\xrightarrow{\chi_{*}}H_{1}(M;\,\mathbb{Z})\to 0

of relative homology groups with integer coefficients
(see e.g.
[
17
, Theorem 2.16]
). Here
\chi_{*}

is the group homomorphism induced by the
inclusion
\chi\colon M\setminus\{a_{j}\}\hookrightarrow M
.
As
M
is closed and orientable, the first arrow is an isomorphism
(see e.g.
[
17
, Theorem 3.26]
); it follows that
\chi_{*}
is an isomorphism, too.

Then, thanks to (
3.60
),
\beta
is not only
closed but also exact in
M\setminus\{a_{j}\}
. As a consequence,
there exists a function
f\in C^{\infty}(M\setminus\{a_{j}\})
such that

(3.61)

\frac{\Xi^{\prime}}{2\pi d_{j}}=\star\mathrm{d}\sigma+\mathrm{d}f\qquad\textrm{in }M\setminus\{a_{j}\}

Step 2
.

Let

I:=\frac{1}{4\pi d_{j}}\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}\int_{M}\left|\Xi(\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g},\,v\right)_{g}

Let
\eta>0
be a small parameter and let
B_{\eta}(a_{j})

be the geodesic disk with radius
\eta
and center
a_{j}
.
We may evaluate
I
as

\begin{split}I=\frac{1}{2\pi d_{j}}\int_{M}(\xi,\,\Xi^{\prime})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\frac{1}{2\pi d_{j}}\lim_{\eta\to 0}\int_{M\setminus B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(\xi,\,\Xi^{\prime})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\hskip-5.69046pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{gradrenSHg5}}}{{=}}\lim_{\eta\to 0}\int_{M\setminus B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(\xi,\,\star\mathrm{d}\sigma+\mathrm{d}f)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\int_{M\setminus B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(\xi,\,-\mathrm{d}^{*}(\star\sigma)+\mathrm{d}f)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\end{split}

We integrate by parts in the right-hand side,
by applying (
2.12
):

(3.62)

I=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(-\sigma\xi-f(\star\xi)\right)

The volume integrals are equal to zero because
\xi

is harmonic, hence
\mathrm{d}\xi=0
,
\mathrm{d}^{*}\xi=0
.
The signs account for orientations effects,
as
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
is given the orientation induced by
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
,
not by
M\setminus B_{\eta}(a_{j})
. Let
\nu
be the outward-pointing
unit normal to
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
. Then,
i\nu
is a unit-norm,
tangent vector field on
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
, consistent with the orientation
induced by
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
. By definition, we have

(3.63)

\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(-\sigma\xi-f(\star\xi)\right)=\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(-\sigma(\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}-f((\star\xi)^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

and, by Lemma
2.1
,
(\star\xi)^{\#}=i\xi^{\#}
.
Therefore, from (
3.62
) and (
3.63
) we obtain

\begin{split}I&=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\left(-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\sigma\,(\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}f(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)\end{split}

or, since
i
is a rotation by
\pi/2
in each tangent plane,

(3.64)

\begin{split}I&=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\left(\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\sigma\,(i\xi^{\#},\,\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}f(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)\end{split}

Figure 1.
A closed surface of genus
\mathfrak{g}>0
can be written
as the quotient of a polygon, modulo a suitable equivalence
on the boundary. In this example,
\mathfrak{g}=2
. Boundary
edges with same label are identified to each other,
in the direction given by the arrows. The boundary of
the polygon carries the cycle

p_{1}+q_{1}-p_{1}-q_{1}+p_{2}+q_{2}-p_{2}-q_{2}=0
.

Step 3
.

In order to proceed further, we need to estimate
\sigma

and
f
on
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
.
We work in normal geodesic coordinates centered at
a_{j}
.
We identify points in
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
with points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

and vector fields with maps
B_{\eta}(a_{j})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

(in particular, we identify
v
with a constant
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
).
An estimate for
\sigma
is given by Lemma
3.10
:

(3.65)

\sigma=\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}(\nu,\,v)_{g}+\mathrm{O}(1)\qquad\textrm{on }\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})

As for
f
, we combine (
3.61
) with (
3.51
)
to obtain

(3.66)

\mathrm{d}f=-\star\mathrm{d}\sigma+\mathrm{O}(1)=\frac{1}{2\pi\eta^{2}}\left((i\nu,\,v)_{g}\nu^{\flat}+(\nu,\,v)_{g}\star\nu^{\flat}\right)+\mathrm{O}(\eta^{-1})\qquad\textrm{on }\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})

Let
x_{\eta}\in\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
be a reference point, say

x_{\eta}=a_{j}+(\eta,\,0)
. For any
x\in\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
,
let
\gamma_{x}
be the circular arc in
\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})

with endpoints
x_{\eta}
and
x
, oriented in the anticlockwise
direction. Then

f(x)=f(x_{\eta})+\int_{\gamma_{x}}\mathrm{d}f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{gradrenSHg9}}}{{=}}f(x_{\eta})+\frac{1}{2\pi\eta^{2}}\int_{\gamma_{x}}(\nu,\,v)_{g}\star\nu^{\flat}+\mathrm{O}(1)

By Gauss’ lemma, when we work in geodesic coordinates
we have
\nu(x)=(x-a_{j})/\left|x-a_{j}\right|
and moreover, the metric
tensor satisfies
g_{ij}(x)=\delta_{ij}+\mathrm{O}(\eta^{2})
.
Therefore, writing
x=a_{j}+\eta(\cos\theta_{x},\,\sin\theta_{x})

for some
\theta_{x}\in(0,\,2\pi)
, we may evaluate the integral as

(3.67)

\begin{split}f(x)&=f(x_{\eta})+\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}\int_{0}^{\theta_{x}}\left(v^{1}\cos\theta+v^{2}\sin\theta\right)\mathrm{d}\theta+\mathrm{O}(1)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}\left(v^{1}\sin\theta_{x}-v^{2}\cos\theta_{x}\right)+C_{\eta}+\mathrm{O}(1)=-\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}(i\nu(x),\,v)_{g}+C_{\eta}+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

for any
x\in\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})
, where
C_{\eta}
depends on
\eta

but not on
x
.

Step 4
.

We combine (
3.64
), (
3.65
)
and (
3.67
):

(3.68)

\begin{split}I=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\bigg(\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(i\xi^{\#},\,\nu)_{g}\,(\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}&+\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,(i\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
+\frac{C_{\eta}}{2\pi\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}&+\mathrm{O}(\eta)\bigg)\end{split}

where
C_{\eta}
is an (unknown) function of
\eta
. However,
Stokes’ theorem implies

\begin{split}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}=\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\star\xi=\int_{B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\mathrm{d}(\star\xi)=0\end{split}

because
\xi
is harmonic.
Therefore, (
3.68
) simplifies to

\begin{split}I&=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(i\xi^{\#},\,(\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\nu+(i\nu,\,v)_{g}\,i\nu\right)_{g}\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}=\lim_{\eta\to 0}\frac{1}{2\pi\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(i\xi^{\#},\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

As
\mathscr{H}^{1}(\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j}))=2\pi\eta+\mathrm{O}(\eta)

(see e.g.
[
39
, Proposition 10]
),
the equality (
3.57
) follows. ∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

Let
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
,
\xi\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

and let
u_{*}
be a canonical harmonic field for
(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)
.
Let
e
be a smooth, divergence-free vector field, defined in a neighbourhood
of
a_{j}
, and

I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j}):=\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(D_{\nu}u_{*},\,D_{e}u_{*})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}|Du_{*}|^{2}_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

We recall that, as a consequence of (
2.62
),
we have
|Du_{*}|^{2}=|j(u_{*})|^{2}
,

D_{v}u_{*}=j(u_{*})(v)\,iu_{*}
for any smooth vector field
v
.
Moreover, the definition of canonical harmonic field
(Definition
3.1
)
gives
j(u_{*})=\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi+\xi
(where
\Psi:=\Psi(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d})

is given by (
3.2
)). Then, we may evaluate
I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})
as

(3.69)

\begin{split}I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})&=\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)-\frac{1}{2}\left|\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi\right|^{2}_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&\qquad+\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(\xi(\nu)\,\xi(e)-\frac{1}{2}\left|\xi\right|^{2}_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&\qquad+\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left(\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(\nu)\,\xi(e)+\xi(\nu)\,\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi(e)-(\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi,\,\xi)_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

We call
J^{1}_{\eta}
,
J^{2}_{\eta}
and
J^{3}_{\eta}

the three integrals at the right-hand side of (
3.69
),
respectively. The limit of
J^{1}_{\eta}
was calculated already in
the the previous section, when we studied the case
\mathfrak{g}=0
; we have

(3.70)

\lim_{\eta\to 0}J^{1}_{\eta}=2\pi d_{j}\left(\nabla S_{j}(a_{j})+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla H(a_{j},\,a_{j}),\,e(a_{j})\right)_{g}

The form
\xi
is smooth in
M
, so

(3.71)

\lim_{\eta\to 0}J^{2}_{\eta}=0

It only remains to estimate
J^{3}_{\eta}
.
Similarly to the case
\mathfrak{g}=0
, we write

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi=-\star\mathrm{d}(\star\Psi)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{S_j}}}{{=}}-\star\mathrm{d}(S_{j}+2\pi d_{j}G(\cdot,a_{j}))\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Green-splitting}}}{{=}}-\star\mathrm{d}S_{j}+d_{j}\star\mathrm{d}\left(\log\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})\right)-2\pi d_{j}\star\mathrm{d}H(\cdot,\,a_{j})\\
&=d_{j}\star\mathrm{d}\left(\log\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})\right)+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

As we have seen before (see Equation (
3.22
)),

\nabla(\log\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j}))=\dist_{g}(\cdot,\,a_{j})^{-1}\nu
.
With the help of (
2.17
) we obtain

(3.72)

\begin{split}\mathrm{d}^{*}\Psi&=\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}(i\nu)^{\flat}+\mathrm{O}(1)\end{split}

We substitute (
3.72
) in the definition of
J^{3}_{\eta}
:

(3.73)

\begin{split}J^{3}_{\eta}&=\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}\left((\xi^{\#},\,\nu)_{g}\,(i\nu,\,e)_{g}-(\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\mathrm{O}(\eta)\end{split}

Since
\{\nu,\,i\nu\}
is an orthonormal frame,
we can write
i\xi^{\#}=(i\xi^{\#},\,\nu)_{g}\nu+(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,i\nu
.
By the properties of
i
, we deduce

\begin{split}(\xi^{\#},\,\nu)_{g}\,(i\nu,\,e)_{g}-(\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}&=(i\xi^{\#},\,i\nu)_{g}\,(i\nu,\,e)_{g}+(i\xi^{\#},\,\nu)_{g}(\nu,\,e)_{g}=(i\xi^{\#},\,e)_{g}\end{split}

and hence

(3.74)

\begin{split}J^{3}_{\eta}&=\frac{d_{j}}{\eta}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(i\xi^{\#},\,e)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\mathrm{O}(\eta)=2\pi d_{j}\,(i\xi^{\#}(a_{j}),\,e(a_{j}))_{g}+\mathrm{O}(\eta)\end{split}

Combining (
3.70
), (
3.71
) and (
3.74
),
we obtain

\begin{split}\lim_{\eta\to 0}I_{\eta}(u_{*})(a_{j})&=2\pi d_{j}\left(\nabla S_{j}(a_{j})+2\pi d_{j}\,\nabla H(a_{j},\,a_{j})+i\xi^{\#}(a_{j}),\,e(a_{j})\right)_{g}\end{split}

and the proposition follows by Lemma
3.11
.
∎

4.
Vortices: the stationary case

4.1.
Compactness and Product Estimates

In this section, we gather some preliminary results
on the Ginzburg-Landau energy for time-independent vector fields
— most importantly, the compactness results of Ignat & Jerrard
[
19
, Proposition 8.1 and Corollary 8.3]

and the product estimates of Sandier & Serfaty
[
36
, Theorem 1]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence such that

F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\leq C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|.

Then, there exists a (non-relabelled) subsequence such that

(4.1)

\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\to\mu=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}\qquad\textrm{in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2).

for
n
distinct points
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and nonzero integers
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}

such that
\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}=\chi(M)
,
\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|d_{j}\right|\leq C
and

(4.2)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|d_{j}\right|.

Moreover,
for any vector fields

X=X^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}
and
Y=Y^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{k}}

with
X^{k}
and
Y^{k}
in
C^{0}_{c}(M)
, there holds

(4.3)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\int_{M}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{M}|D_{Y}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}\geq\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\mu[X,Y]\right|

Proof.

The compactness result is borrowed from Ignat& Jerrard
[
19
, Theorem 2.1, Proposition 8.1 and Corollary 8.3]
.

The proof of the product estimate (
4.3
) is based on the validity of the analogous product estimate in the Euclidean two-dimensional setting and is similar to the proof of the space-time product estimate in Proposition
5.3
, therefore we skip it.
∎

We state a few consequences of the product
estimate (
4.3
), analogous to the results
of
[
36
, Corollary 4]
. The proof is (almost) identical.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of vector fields
such that
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}
in
W^{-1,1}(M)
, where

a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
are distinct points in
M
and
d_{1}
, …,
d_{n}
are non-zero integers.
Moreover, assume that
u_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(M)
and that

(4.4)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\left|\log\varepsilon\right|).

Let
r>0
be small enough, in such a way that the balls
B_{r}(a_{j})

are pairwise disjoint. For any
B_{r}(a_{j})
, let
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}

be a local orthonormal tangent frame on
B_{r}(a_{j})
,
with
\tau_{2}=i\tau_{1}
. Let
X
and
Y

be smooth vector fields on
M
. Then,
\left|d_{j}\right|=1
for any
j
and the following hold:

(4.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{j}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi n,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,j=1,2

(4.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{M}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r}(a_{j})}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=n\pi,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{M}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|X(a_{j})\right|_{g}^{2},

(4.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{M}(D_{X}v_{\varepsilon},D_{Y}v_{\varepsilon})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}(X(a_{j}),Y(a_{j}))_{g}.

Proof.

Let
f,g\in C_{c}(B_{r}(a_{j}))
be such that
\left|f\right|\leq 1
,

\left|g\right|\leq 1
and
f(a_{j})=g(a_{j})=1
.
Let
\mu:=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}
.
By taking
X=f\tau_{1}
,
Y=g\tau_{2}
in the product
estimate (
4.3
), we obtain

(4.9)

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}\mu[f\tau_{1},\,g\tau_{2}]\right|=\pi\left|d_{j}\right|f(a_{j})\,g(a_{j})=\pi\left|d_{j}\right|\end{split}

In particular, since

\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}+|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

and

\left(\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}+|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\pi\left|d_{j}\right|+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)

On the other hand, recalling the assumption (
4.4
),
we conclude that
\left|d_{j}\right|=1
for any
j
and

(4.10)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\cdot\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}\\
&=\pi+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\end{split}

and thus

\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\left(\left(\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\right)^{1/2}-\left(\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right)^{2}=\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1).

As a result, we obtain

\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{1}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}=\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{2}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)

and thus

\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}|D_{\tau_{k}}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}=\pi+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\qquad\textrm{for }k=1,2.

Therefore, (
4.5
)
and (
4.6
) follow.
The estimate (
4.6
),
combined with the assumption (
4.10
), imply

(4.11)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r}(a_{j})}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0.

Now we prove (
4.7
).
Let
X
be a smooth vector field on
M
.
For
\delta\in(0,\,r)
, we have

(4.12)

\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})\setminus B_{\delta}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\int_{B_{\delta}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

As in Subsection
2.3.1
, we work in geodesic normal
coordinates with center at
a_{j}
and represent
u_{\varepsilon}
,
X

as vector fields
v_{\varepsilon}\colon B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\bar{X}\colon B_{\delta}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, as in (
2.46
).
Assume that the orthonormal frame
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}

satisfies (
2.45
). Then, Lemma
2.2
implies

\int_{B_{\delta}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})\right)\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\left(\left|\bar{X}\cdot\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\mathrm{O}(|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2})\right)\mathrm{d}x,

On the other hand,
[
36
, Corollary 4]
gives that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\left|\bar{X}\cdot\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\pi\left|\bar{X}(0)\right|^{2}=\pi\left|X(a_{j})\right|_{g}^{2}.

Therefore, recalling that
u_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(M)
by assumption,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{\delta}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{d}x=\pi|X(a_{j})|^{2}+O(\delta^{2}).

Moreover, (
4.11
) implies

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})\setminus B_{\delta}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})\setminus B_{\delta}(a_{j})}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0

for any
\delta
. Therefore, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi\left|X(a_{j})\right|^{2}_{g}+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})

and, letting
\delta\to 0
,

(4.13)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{B_{r}(a_{j})}\left|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi\left|X(a_{j})\right|^{2}_{g}.

Equation (
4.7
) now follows by (
4.13
)
and (
4.11
). Equation (
4.8
)
follows by applying (
4.7
) to
X-Y
and to
X+Y

and taking the difference.
∎

If the vector fields
u_{\varepsilon}
satisfy an energy upper bound
that is stronger than (
4.4
),
then we can prove even stronger results — in particular,
we can show that the energy of
u_{\varepsilon}
is uniformly bounded
away from a finite set of singularities. These types of results are classical
in the context of Ginzburg-Landau functionals, but we need to
adapt them to our context. Our proof relies crucially on
the analysis in
[
19
]
. We consider
a sequence of vector fields
(u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
H^{1}_{\tang}(M)

that satisfies

(4.14)

\begin{split}\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}\qquad\textrm{in }W^{-1,1}(M)\quad\textrm{as }\varepsilon\to 0,\end{split}

where
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
are disctinct points in
M
and
d_{1}
, …,
d_{n}

are non-zero integers. We also assume that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C_{0}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+C_{0}

for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C_{0}
.
By Lemma
4.2
, we have
\left|d_{j}\right|=1
for any
j
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of vector fields
that satisfies (
4.14
),
(
4.15
), (
4.16
).
Then, for any
r>0
small enough there exists
\varepsilon_{0}(r)>0

such that, for
\varepsilon\in(0,\,\varepsilon_{0}(r))
, we have

(4.17)

\int_{M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}-1)^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n\left|\log r\right|+C,

for some constant
C
that depends only on
M
,
C_{0}
and
n
(not on
\varepsilon
,
r
).

Estimates similar to (
4.17
) are already contained
in Ignat and Jerrard’s work
[
19
]

(see for instance Lemma 8.2 and Proposition 9.1).
However, since the statement of Lemma
4.3

does not appear explicitely in
[
19
]
,
we include a proof, for the sake of completeness.
We will deduce Lemma
4.3
from the so-called
‘ball construction’, which was first introduced in the
Euclidean setting by Sandier
[
33
]
and Jerrard
[
20
]

(see
[
19
, Lemma 8.2]
for the extension
to the Riemannian setting). We will also need an auxiliary result:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of vector fields
that satisfies (
4.14
),
(
4.15
), (
4.16
).
Let
\rho>0
be small enough, so that the closed balls
\bar{B}_{\rho}(a_{j})

are pairwise disjoint. Given an index
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
,
we assume that there exist a constant
C
such that

\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\lesssim\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{\rho}

for any
\varepsilon
small enough. Then, for any
\varepsilon

small enough we have
\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}\geq 1/2

on
\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})
and

\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j}))=d_{j}

We will prove Lemma
4.4
in Appendix
B
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

By a density argument
(as in
[
19
, Lemma 5.1 and Proposition 8.1, Step 1]
),
we may assume without loss of generality that the fields
u_{\varepsilon}

are smooth. Then, we apply
[
19
, Lemma 8.2]
.
For a given
r>0
(sufficiently small) and any
\varepsilon
,
we find a finite collection of pairwise disjoint
balls
B_{1,\varepsilon},\,\ldots,\,B_{N_{\varepsilon},\varepsilon}
,
of centers
b_{k,\varepsilon}
and radii
r_{k,\varepsilon}
,
that satisfy the following properties:

(4.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N_{\varepsilon}}r_{k,\varepsilon}\leq\frac{r}{16}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\left|u_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|_{g}\geq\frac{1}{2}\qquad\textrm{for }x\in M\setminus\bigcup_{k=1}^{N_{\varepsilon}}B_{k,\varepsilon}

(4.20)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{k,\varepsilon}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\left|\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{k,\varepsilon})\right|\left(\pi\log\frac{r}{\varepsilon}-C\right)

for any
k
,
\varepsilon
and some constant
C
that depends only on
M
,
C_{0}
.
We claim that, for any
\varepsilon
small enough and any
k=1,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}
,
there holds

(4.21)

\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{k,\varepsilon})\neq 0\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad b_{k,\varepsilon}\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r/2}(a_{j})

(We recall that the
a_{j}
’s are the points in the support
of the limit of
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})
, i.e.
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}
in
W^{-1,1}(M)
.)
Suppose, towards a contradiction, that (
4.21
)
is false. Then, up to a permutation of the indices
k

and extraction of a subsequence
\varepsilon\to 0
, we may assume that

\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{1,\varepsilon})\neq 0,\qquad b_{1,\varepsilon}\in M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r/2}(a_{j})

By Equation (
4.18
), we obtain

B_{1,\varepsilon}\subseteq M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r/4}(a_{j})

and hence, thanks to (
4.20
),

(4.22)

\begin{split}\int_{M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r/4}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\pi\log\frac{r}{\varepsilon}-C\end{split}

However, the assumption (
4.16
) and
the lower energy bound (
4.6
)
from Proposition
4.2
, combined, give

(4.23)

\begin{split}\int_{M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r/4}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\left|\log\varepsilon\right|)\end{split}

Equation (
4.23
) contradicts (
4.22
), and (
4.21
) follows.

Next, we claim that, for any
\varepsilon
small enough and any
j=1,\,\ldots,\,n
,
there exists
k_{*}=k_{*}(j,\,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}\}
such that

(4.24)

\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{k_{*},\varepsilon})\neq 0,\qquad b_{k_{*},\varepsilon}\in B_{r/2}(a_{j})

Suppose, again towards a contradiction, that (
4.24
)
does not hold. Then, there exists an index
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}

and a countable subsequence
\varepsilon_{m}\to 0

such that, for any
m
and any
k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon_{m}}\}
,

(4.25)

b_{k,\varepsilon_{m}}\in B_{r/2}(a_{j})\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\ind(u_{\varepsilon_{m}},\,\partial B_{k,\varepsilon_{m}})=0

By Fatou’s lemma, we have

\begin{split}&\mathscr{H}^{1}\left(\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcap_{m\geq M}\left\{\rho\in(r/4,\,r/2)\colon\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})\cap\bigcup_{k=1}^{N_{\varepsilon_{m}}}B_{k,\varepsilon_{m}}\neq\emptyset\right\}\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq\liminf_{m\to+\infty}\mathscr{H}^{1}\left\{\rho\in(r/4,\,r/2)\colon\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})\cap\bigcup_{k=1}^{N_{\varepsilon_{m}}}B_{k,\varepsilon_{m}}\neq\emptyset\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{H1b1}}}{{\leq}}\frac{r}{8}\end{split}

Therefore, there exists a radius
\rho\in(r/4,\,r/2)
and a (non-relabelled)
subsequence
\varepsilon_{m}\to 0
such that

(4.26)

\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})\cap\bigcup_{k=1}^{N_{\varepsilon_{m}}}B_{k,\varepsilon_{m}}=\emptyset

By Fubini theorem, we may also assume that

\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon_{m}}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon_{m}}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\lesssim\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon_{m}\right|}{\rho}

From (
4.18
), (
4.25
) and (
4.26
), we deduce that

\left|u_{\varepsilon_{m}}(x)\right|_{g}\geq 1/2
for any
x\in\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})
and that

\ind(u_{\varepsilon_{m}},\,\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j}))=0

However, Lemma
4.4
implies

\ind(u_{\varepsilon_{m}},\,\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j}))=d_{j}\neq 0

for any
m
large enough. This is a contradiction, so (
4.24
) follows.

We can now conclude the proof of the lemma.
Without loss of generality, by taking
r
small enough, we may
suppose that the balls
B_{r}(a_{j})
are pairwise disjoint.
By Equation (
4.24
), for any
\varepsilon

small enough there exist at least
n
values of
k
such that

\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{k,\varepsilon})\neq 0
. By (
4.18
)
and (
4.21
), for these values of
k
we have

B_{k,\varepsilon}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r}(a_{j})
.
Then, (
4.20
) implies

(4.27)

\int_{\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{r}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq n\left(\pi\log\frac{r}{\varepsilon}-C\right)

Combining (
4.27
) with (
4.16
), the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma
4.3
implies a global upper bound
for the sequence
u_{\varepsilon}
in
W^{1,p}(M)
, for
p\in(1,\,2)

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of vector fields
that satisfies (
4.14
),
(
4.15
), (
4.16
).
Then, for any
p\in(1,\,2)
we have

\left\|Du_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{p}(M)}\leq C_{p},

where
C_{p}
depends only on
M
,
p
,
n
and the constant
C_{0}

that appears in (
4.16
).

Corollary
4.5
follows from Lemma
4.3

by an argument due to Struwe
[
42
]
; details may be found,
e.g., in
[
19
, Lemma 12.3]
.

4.2.
Compactness for solutions of an inhomogeneous Ginzburg-Landau equation

The results in Section
4.1
are valid for any sequence
of vector fields that satisfies appropriate bounds. Instead, in this section
we consider vector fields
u_{\varepsilon}
on
M
that solve an
inhomogeneous Ginzburg-Landau equation, that is

(4.28)

-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1\right)u_{\varepsilon}=f_{\varepsilon},

where
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is a given sequence of vector fields on
M
.
For the time being, there is still no dependence on time
in Equation (
4.28
).
However, later on (Section
5
) we will apply these results
to the parabolic Ginzburg-Landau equation (
1.8
),
by working at fixed time
t
and taking
f_{\varepsilon}=-\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}(t)/\left|\log\varepsilon\right|
.

As before, we assume that
u_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

(4.29)

\begin{split}\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}\qquad\textrm{in }W^{-1,1}(M)\quad\textrm{as }\varepsilon\to 0,\end{split}

where
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
are distinct points in
M
and
d_{1}
, …,
d_{n}

are non-zero integers, and that

(4.30)

\displaystyle\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C_{0}

(4.31)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+C_{0}

for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C_{0}
.
Lemma
4.3
and Corollary
4.5

imply that, up to extraction of a subsequence,

u_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0}\qquad\textrm{weakly in }W^{1,p}(M)\textrm{ and in }H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\})

for some limit vector field
u_{0}
, such that
\left|u_{0}\right|_{g}=1
a.e. in
M
.
We are going to prove stronger convergence results.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of solutions of (
4.28
)
that satisfies (
4.29
),
(
4.30
), (
4.31
).
Assume that the sequence
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded in
L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
. Then,

\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}\to 1

locally uniformly in
M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\}
.

The proof of Lemma
4.6
is based on classical arguments from
the Ginzburg-Landau theory, see
[
8
]
, and depends on some auxiliary results.
We recall that
e_{\varepsilon}(u)
denotes the Ginzburg-Landau energy density
of a vector field
u
, as defined in (
2.48
).

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a solution of (
4.28
), with
f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
.
Let
U\subseteq M
be a smooth, open set. Let
X
be a smooth vector field, defined in
a neighbourhood of
\overline{U}
, and let
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}

be a smooth orthonormal tangent frame, defined in a neighbourhood
of
\overline{U}
. Then,

\begin{split}&\int_{U}\left(e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,(\mathrm{div}\,X)-(D_{\tau_{j}}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{\tau_{k}}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(\tau_{j},\,D_{\tau_{k}}X)_{g}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad=\int_{U}\left((D_{\tau_{j}}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{\tau_{k}}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(D_{\tau_{k}}\tau_{j},\,X)_{g}-(D_{X}u_{\varepsilon},\,f_{\varepsilon})_{g}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad-\int_{U}\left((R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})\right)\,(X,\tau_{j})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{\partial U}\left(e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})(X,\,\nu)_{g}-(D_{X}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{\nu}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

where
\nu
is the outer normal to
\partial U
.

Proof.

This lemma is a variant of the so-called stress-energy identity,
see e.g.
[
18
, Theorem 1.3.6]
and

[
34
, Proposition 3.7]
.
To simplify the notation, we write
D_{j}:=D_{\tau_{j}}
.
For a scalar function
\varphi
, we write

\nabla_{j}:=(\nabla\varphi,\,\tau_{j})_{g}=\mathrm{d}\varphi(\tau_{j})
.
Under the assumption
f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
, the second
derivatives of
u_{\varepsilon}
exist in
L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
, by elliptic regularity.
Therefore, we can compute the gradient of the energy
density
e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})
.
First of all, we recall that

D_{j}D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}=D_{k}D_{j}u_{\varepsilon}+R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon}+D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},

where
R(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Riemannian curvature tensor and
[\cdot,\cdot]
are the Lie Brackets, namely
[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]:=D_{\tau_{j}}\tau_{k}-D_{\tau_{k}}\tau_{j}
since the Levi-Civita connection
D
is symmetric by construction (i.e. torsion free).
We have (recall that
\left\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right\}
is an orthonormal frame)

\begin{split}\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\right)&=(D_{j}D_{k}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}=(D_{k}D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})\\
&=\nabla_{k}(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}-(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}+(R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})\\
&\hskip-8.5359pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:rough_frame}}}{{=}}\nabla_{k}(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}-(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\\
&+(\mathrm{div}\,\tau_{k})\,(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}+(R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})\end{split}

The equation (
4.28
) implies

\begin{split}\nabla_{j}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\right)&=\nabla_{k}(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}+(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,f_{\varepsilon})_{g}-\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}-1)\,(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,u_{\varepsilon})\\
&+(\mathrm{div}\,\tau_{k})\,(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}+(R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})\end{split}

and hence,

(4.32)

\begin{split}\nabla_{j}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})&=\nabla_{k}(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}+(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,f_{\varepsilon})_{g}+(\mathrm{div}\,\tau_{k})\,(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\\
&+(R(\tau_{j},\tau_{k})u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})+(D_{[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})\end{split}

We integrate by parts, as in (
2.25
) and (
2.28
):

\begin{split}&\int_{U}\left(e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,(\mathrm{div}\,X)-(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(\tau_{j},\,D_{k}X)_{g}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\quad=\int_{U}\left(-\nabla_{j}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,(X,\,\tau_{j})_{g}+\nabla_{k}(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(\tau_{j},\,X)_{g}+(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(D_{k}\tau_{j},\,X)_{g}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\quad\quad+\int_{U}(\mathrm{div}\,\tau_{k})\,(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(\tau_{j},\,X)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt+\int_{\partial U}\left(e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,(X,\,\nu)_{g}-(D_{j}u_{\varepsilon},\,D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,(\tau_{j},\,X)_{g}\,(\tau_{k},\,\nu)_{g}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

By substituting (
4.32
) in the right-hand side,
the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a solution of (
4.28
),
with
f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
.
Let
B_{\rho}(p)\subseteq M
be a geodesic ball in
M
,
with radius
\rho
smaller than the injectivity radius of
M
.
Then,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B_{\rho}(p)}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}&\lesssim\rho\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{\rho}(p))}\left\|Du_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(B_{\rho}(p))}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\rho^{2}\int_{B_{\rho}(p)}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\rho\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(p)}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

Proof.

We work in geodesic normal coordinates centered at
p

and identify
B_{\rho}(p)
with a Euclidean ball,
B_{\rho}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
We recall that the metric tensor and the Christoffel symbols,
in normal coordinates, satisfy
g_{ij}(x)=\delta_{ij}+\mathrm{O}(|x|^{2})

and
\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(x)=\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|)
, respectively.
We consider the orthonormal tangent frame

\tau_{1}:=\frac{\partial_{1}}{\left|\partial_{1}\right|_{g}},\qquad\tau_{2}:=\frac{\partial_{2}-(\partial_{2},\,\tau_{1})_{g}\,\tau_{1}}{\left|\partial_{2}-(\partial_{2},\,\tau_{1})_{g}\,\tau_{1}\right|_{g}}

(where
\partial_{1}
,
\partial_{2}
are the coordinate fields,

\partial_{j}:=\partial/\partial x^{j}
). This frame satisfies

D_{\tau_{k}}\tau_{j}=\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|)
for any
j
,
k
and thus
[\tau_{j},\tau_{k}]=D_{\tau_{j}}\tau_{k}-D_{\tau_{k}}\tau_{j}=\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|)
.
We apply Lemma
4.7
to the vector field
defined in coordinates as
X(x):=x
. We have

D_{\tau_{k}}X=\left(\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\tau_{k}^{j}+\Gamma^{i}_{jh}X^{h}\tau_{k}^{j}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}=\tau_{k}+\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|^{2}),\qquad\mathrm{div}\,X=2+\mathrm{O}(\left|x\right|^{2})

and the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

For any
\sigma>0
, we define

M_{\sigma}:=M\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{\sigma}(a_{j})

We fix
\sigma>0
. Our task is to prove that
\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}\to 1

uniformly in
M_{\sigma}
. We denote by
C

a generic positive constant, that may depend on
M
,
n
,
C_{0}

but not on
\varepsilon
,
\sigma
.
As a preliminary remark, we observe that the assumptions
(
4.14
), (
4.15
), (
4.16
)
and Lemma
4.3
imply

(4.33)

\int_{M_{\sigma/4}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n\left|\log\sigma\right|+C

We split the proof into several steps.

Step 1

(An extension result)
.

Let
q\in(1,\,+\infty)
. We claim that there exists a
constant
C_{q}
such that, for any
\sigma>0
smaller than
the injectivity radius of
M
, any
x_{0}\in M
and any scalar
function
\rho\in W^{1,q}(B_{\sigma}(x_{0})\setminus B_{\sigma/2}(x_{0}))
,
there exists an extension
\tilde{\rho}\in W^{1,q}(B_{\sigma}(x_{0}))

of
\rho
such that

(4.34)

\left\|\nabla\tilde{\rho}\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{\sigma}(x_{0}))}\leq C_{q}\left\|\nabla\rho\right\|_{L^{q}(B_{\sigma}(x_{0})\setminus B_{\sigma/2}(x_{0}))}

By working in normal geodesic coordinates centered at
x_{0}
,
without loss of generality we may identify
B_{\sigma}(x_{0})

with an Euclidean ball,
B_{\sigma}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
As the inequality (
4.34
) is scale-invariant,
by a scaling argument it suffices to consider the case
\sigma=1
.
If the average
\fint_{B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{1/2}(0)}\rho=0
,
then (
4.34
) follows by standard extension results
and the Poincaré inequality. In general, we first construct an
extension
\tilde{\rho}_{0}
of
\rho_{0}:=\rho-\fint_{B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{1/2}(0)}\rho
, then define
\tilde{\rho}:=\tilde{\rho}_{0}+\fint_{B_{1}(0)\setminus B_{1/2}(0)}\rho
.

Step 2

(Hölder bounds on
\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}
)
.

The equation (
4.28
) and the assumption (
4.15
) imply

\begin{split}\left\|\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(M_{\sigma/4})}&\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left\|\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(M_{\sigma/4})}\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}+\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(M)}\\
&\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left\|\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(M_{\sigma/4})}+C\end{split}

(The norm
\left\|f_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(M)}
is bounded by assumption.)
On the other hand, the energy bound (
4.33
) gives

\left\|\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1\right\|_{L^{2}(M_{\sigma/4})}\leq C\varepsilon\left|\log\sigma\right|^{1/2}

therefore

(4.35)

\begin{split}\left\|\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(M_{\sigma/4})}&\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\left|\log\sigma\right|^{1/2}\end{split}

Let
\alpha\in[1/2,\,1)
and

q:=\frac{2}{1-\alpha}\geq 4

By applying elliptic regularity results
and the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality, we deduce

\begin{split}\left\|Du_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{q}(M_{\sigma/2})}&\leq C\left\|\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(M_{\sigma/4})}^{\alpha}\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}^{1-\alpha}+C\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\\
&\hskip-18.49411pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hp:H1bounds-Linfty},\,\eqref{clear1-1}}}{{\leq}}C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left|\log\sigma\right|^{\alpha/2}\end{split}

Due to (
4.34
), there exists a sequence of scalar
functions
\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\in W^{1,q}(M)
such that

\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}=\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}

out of
\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}B_{\sigma/2}(a_{j})
and

\begin{split}\left\|\nabla\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{q}(M)}&\leq C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left|\log\sigma\right|^{\alpha/2}\end{split}

Then, by applying the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,q}(M)\hookrightarrow C^{\alpha}(M)

to
\tilde{\rho}_{\varepsilon}
, we conclude

(4.36)

\begin{split}[\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}]_{C^{\alpha}(M_{\sigma/2})}&\leq C\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\left|\log\sigma\right|^{\alpha/2},\end{split}

where
[\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}]_{C^{\alpha}(M_{\sigma/2})}
denotes
the
\alpha
-Hölder seminorm of
u_{\varepsilon}
on
M_{\sigma/2}
,
that is

[\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}]_{C^{\alpha}(M_{\sigma/2})}:=\sup_{x,\,y\in M_{\sigma/2},\ x\neq y}\frac{\left|\left|u_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|_{g}-\left|u_{\varepsilon}(y)\right|_{g}\right|}{\dist_{g}(x,\,y)^{\alpha}}

Step 3
.

We show that
\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}\to 1
uniformly in
M_{\sigma}
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose this claim is false.
Then, there exists a number
\delta>0
, a (non-relabelled) subsequence

\varepsilon\to 0
and, for any
\varepsilon
in the subsequence, a pont
x_{\varepsilon}\in M_{\sigma}

such that

(4.37)

\left|\left|u_{\varepsilon}(x_{\varepsilon})\right|_{g}-1\right|\geq\delta

Due to (
4.36
), we deduce that
\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}

remains close to
1-\delta
on a ball, centered at
x_{\varepsilon}
,
of radius comparable to
\varepsilon\left|\log\sigma\right|^{-1/2}
.
More precisely, we have

\left|\left|u_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|_{g}-1\right|\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\qquad\textrm{for any }x\in B_{\lambda\varepsilon\left|\log\sigma\right|^{-1/2}}(x_{\varepsilon}),

where
\lambda>0
is a positive parameter that depends only
on
\delta
and the constant
C
in (
4.36
)
— in particular,
\lambda
is independent of
\varepsilon
and
\sigma
.
As a consequence, we have

(4.38)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B_{\lambda\varepsilon\left|\log\sigma\right|^{-1/2}}(x_{\varepsilon})}\left(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1\right)^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\frac{C_{\delta}}{\left|\log\sigma\right|}

where
C_{\delta}
is a strictly positive constant, depending on
\delta

but not on
\varepsilon
,
\sigma
.

Now, let
\eta>0
be a small parameter, to be chosen later on.
For any
\varepsilon
small enough, there exists a
radius
\rho_{\varepsilon}\in(\varepsilon^{1/2},\,\varepsilon^{1/4})
such that

(4.39)

\int_{B_{\rho_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{\eta}{\rho_{\varepsilon}}

— for otherwise, we would have

\int_{B_{\varepsilon^{1/4}}(x_{\varepsilon})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\int_{\varepsilon^{1/2}}^{\varepsilon^{1/4}}\frac{\eta}{\rho}\,\mathrm{d}\rho=\frac{\eta}{4}\left|\log\varepsilon\right|

which contradicts the energy bound (
4.33
) for
\varepsilon
small enough.
We can estimate the integral of
(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}

on
B_{\rho_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon})
by applying Lemma
4.8
.
We obtain, recalling (
4.39
) and the energy bound (
4.33
),

(4.40)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B_{\rho_{\varepsilon}}(x_{\varepsilon})}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\lesssim\varepsilon^{1/4}\left|\log\sigma\right|^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\left|\log\sigma\right|+\eta

If we choose
\eta
small enough, depending on
\delta
and
\sigma
only,
then the inequality (
4.40
) contradicts (
4.38
)
when
\varepsilon
is small. The lemma follows. ∎

The next lemma further discusses the convergence of
u_{\varepsilon}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, away from the singularities. Our proof follows closely
F. H. Lin
[
24
]
and
[
23
]
.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of solutions of (
4.28
)
that satisfies (
4.29
),
(
4.30
), (
4.31
).
Assume that the sequence
(f_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded in
L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
.
Then, up to extraction of a subsequence, we have

(4.41)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}\to u_{0}\hskip 64.01869pt\textrm{strongly in }H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\})

(4.42)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}\to u_{0}\hskip 64.01869pt\textrm{locally uniformly in }M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\}

(4.43)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}-1)\to 0\qquad\textrm{strongly in }L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\})

Remark 4.1
.

Corollary
4.5
and the strong
convergence (
4.41
) imply,
via the Hölder inequality, that
u_{\varepsilon}\to u_{0}
strongly
in
W^{1,p}(M)
for any
p\in[1,\,2)
.

Proof of Lemma
4.9
.

Let
B\subset\!\subset M\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\}

be a geodesic ball, of radius smaller than the
injectivity radius of
M
. By Lemma
4.3

and Fubini’s theorem, we can make sure that

(4.44)

\begin{split}\int_{B}\left(\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\int_{\partial B}\left(\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq C\end{split}

The constant
C
does depend on the ball
B
(in particular,
on the distance of
B
from the singular points
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
),
but not on
\varepsilon
. Moreover, Lemma
4.6
implies

(4.45)

\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}\to 1\qquad\textrm{uniformly in }\overline{B}.

Step 1
.

We let
\rho_{\varepsilon}:=|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}
and
v_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{u_{\varepsilon}}{|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}}
.
The field
v_{\varepsilon}
is well-defined and belongs to
H^{1}_{\tang}(B)
,
thanks to (
4.45
).
By lifting results (see e.g.
[
38
, Lemma 6.1]
), there exists a scalar
function
\alpha_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(B)
such that

(4.46)

v_{\varepsilon}=\cos(\alpha_{\varepsilon})\hat{e}_{1}+i\sin(\alpha_{\varepsilon})\hat{e}_{1},

where
\left\{\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2}\right\}=\left\{\hat{e}_{1},i\hat{e}_{1}\right\}

is an orthonormal moving frame in
B
such that
\mathrm{div}\,\mathbb{A}=0

(see (
2.36
)).
We rewrite (
1.4
) in terms of
\rho_{\varepsilon}
and
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
.
First of all, note that

\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}=\Delta_{g}(\rho_{\varepsilon}v_{\varepsilon})=\rho_{\varepsilon}\Delta_{g}v_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}\Delta_{g}\rho_{\varepsilon}+2g^{ij}\partial_{i}\rho_{\varepsilon}D_{j}v_{\varepsilon}.

Moreover (see
[
38
, Lemma 6.2 and Lemma 6.3]
)

Dv_{\varepsilon}=(\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{A})iv_{\varepsilon},

and (
\mathbb{A}:=\mathcal{A}^{\sharp}
)

\Delta_{g}v_{\varepsilon}=(\Delta\alpha_{\varepsilon})iv_{\varepsilon}-|\nabla\alpha-\mathbb{A}|^{2}_{g}v_{\varepsilon}.

Therefore,

(4.47)

\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}=\Big(\rho_{\varepsilon}\Delta\alpha_{\varepsilon}+2(\nabla\alpha_{\varepsilon}-\mathbb{A},\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon})_{g}\Big)iv_{\varepsilon}+\Big(\Delta_{g}\rho_{\varepsilon}-|\nabla\alpha_{\varepsilon}-\mathbb{A}|^{2}_{g}\Big)v_{\varepsilon}.

We decompose
f_{\varepsilon}=(f_{\varepsilon},\,v_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,v_{\varepsilon}+(f_{\varepsilon},\,iv_{\varepsilon})_{g}iv_{\varepsilon}=:f_{\varepsilon}^{1}\,v_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,iv_{\varepsilon}
. Since we have assumed that

f_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
, we have

(4.48)

\left\|f^{1}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(B)}+\left\|f^{2}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}(B)}\leq C.

We can thus rewrite (
1.4
) as the following system

(4.49)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\,(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\nabla\alpha_{\varepsilon}-\mathbb{A}))=f^{1}_{\varepsilon},\\
\\
\displaystyle\Delta_{g}\rho_{\varepsilon}+\frac{\rho_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2})-\rho_{\varepsilon}|\nabla\alpha_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}=f^{2}_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Step 2
.

Since
\rho_{\varepsilon}\geq 1/2
and
f^{1}_{\varepsilon}\in L^{2}(B)
, it turns out that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}\in H^{2}(B)
and thus
h_{\varepsilon}:=f^{2}_{\varepsilon}+\rho_{\varepsilon}|\nabla\alpha_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\in L^{2}
.
Then, we multiply the equation for
\rho_{\varepsilon}
with
1-\rho_{\varepsilon}
and we integrate on
B
. We get

\displaystyle\int_{B}|\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}\rho_{\varepsilon}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})^{2}(1+\rho_{\varepsilon})\mathrm{vol}_{g}

(4.50)

\displaystyle=\int_{B}h_{\varepsilon}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\int_{\partial B}\partial_{\nu}\rho_{\varepsilon}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}.

Since
\rho_{\varepsilon}\geq 1/2

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}\rho_{\varepsilon}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})^{2}(1+\rho_{\varepsilon})\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\frac{3}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

On the other hand, since
h_{\varepsilon}
is in
L^{2}
we get

\Big|\int_{B}h_{\varepsilon}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})\mathrm{vol}_{g}\Big|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2\delta}\int_{B}h_{\varepsilon}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}

and (thanks to (
4.44
) and (
4.45
))

\int_{\partial B}\partial_{\nu}\rho_{\varepsilon}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq C\|\rho_{\varepsilon}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B)}.

Choosing
\delta=1/2
, we conclude that

\int_{B}|\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}(1-\rho_{\varepsilon})^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq C\big(\varepsilon^{2}\|h_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\rho_{\varepsilon}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial B)}\big).

Therefore,

(4.51)

\rho_{\varepsilon}\to 1\qquad\textrm{strongly in }H^{1}(B)

Moreover, since

(1-x^{2})^{2}=(1-x)^{2}(1+x)^{2}\leq 4(1-x)^{2},

for
x\in(1/2,1)
, we conclude that

(4.52)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B}(1-|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2})^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0

Step 3
.

The first equation of (
4.47
) can be
equivalently written as

\Delta_{g}\alpha_{\varepsilon}=(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2})\Delta_{g}\alpha_{\varepsilon}+\underbrace{(\nabla(1-\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}),\,\nabla\alpha_{\varepsilon})_{g}-\mathrm{div}\,(\rho_{\varepsilon}^{2}\mathbb{A})+f_{\varepsilon}^{2}}_{=:g_{\varepsilon}}

Both
\rho_{\varepsilon}
and
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
are bounded in
H^{1}(B)
,
due to the energy estimate (
4.44
),
while
f_{\varepsilon}^{1}
is bounded in
L^{2}(B)
by (
4.48
).
Therefore,
g_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{2}(B)
.
Let
B^{\prime}\subset\!\subset B
be a slightly smaller ball.
Thanks to (
4.45
), we can apply
elliptic regularity estimates and conclude that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}

is bounded in
H^{2}(B^{\prime})
. By compact Sobolev embedding,
it follows that
\alpha_{\varepsilon}
converges strongly in
H^{1}(B^{\prime})

(possibly up to extraction of a subsequence).
Therefore, since

D_{\hat{e}_{k}}v_{\varepsilon}=(\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}(\hat{e}_{k})-\mathcal{A}(\hat{e}_{k}))iv_{\varepsilon},

for
k=1,\,2
, we get that
D_{\hat{e}_{k}}v_{\varepsilon}
is strongly convergent in
L^{2}(B^{\prime})
.
As

D_{\hat{e}_{k}}u_{\varepsilon}=\rho_{\varepsilon}D_{\hat{e}_{k}}v_{\varepsilon}+v_{\varepsilon}(\nabla\rho_{\varepsilon},\,\hat{e}_{k})_{g},

thanks to (
4.51
) we conclude that

Du_{\varepsilon}
converges strongly in
L^{2}(B^{\prime})
.
Finally, as a byproduct of the argument above,
we obtain that
u_{\varepsilon}/\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|
is bounded
in
H^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\})
.
Therefore,
u_{\varepsilon}/\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|

converges locally uniformly in
M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\}
,
by Sobolev embeddings. By Lemma
4.6
, we deduce that

u_{\varepsilon}\to u_{0}
locally uniformly in
M\setminus\{a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}\}
.
∎

5.
Vortices: the dynamical case

5.1.
Space-Time Compactness and Product Estimates

In this section we discuss the compactness of sequences of vector fields
u_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,\,T]\times M\to TM
satisfying suitable energy bounds.
These are the typical bounds satisfied by the solutions of the (rescaled) heat flow of the Ginzburg-Landau equation.

First of all, for a smooth vector field
u\colon[0,\,T]\times M\to TM
we define the space-time current
j_{Q}(u)
as

(5.1)

j_{Q}(u):=(D^{Q}u,\,iu)_{g_{\mathrm{cyl}}}=(\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t+j(u),

where
D^{Q}
denotes the Riemannian connection
on the cylinder
Q:=[0,\,T]\times M

and
g_{\mathrm{cyl}}
denotes the product metric in
Q
,

g_{\mathrm{cyl}}:={\mathrm{d}t}^{2}+g
.
We denote with
\mathrm{d}_{Q}
the exterior derivative on
Q

while
\mathrm{d}_{t}
denotes the exterior derivative with respect to time and
\mathrm{d}
denotes the exterior derivative with respect to
the variables of
M
. Recalling from (
2.63
) that

\mathrm{d}j(u)=\omega(u)-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}
, we obtain

(5.2)

\mathrm{d}_{Q}\,j_{Q}(u)=\mathrm{d}\left((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\mathrm{d}_{Q}j(u)=V+\omega(u)-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
V
denotes the two-form on
Q

(5.3)

V:=\mathrm{d}\left((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\mathrm{d}_{t}j(u).

Then, we define the space-time vorticity

(5.4)

J(u):=V+\omega(u).

Now, we fix some
p\in M
and
\delta>0
smaller than the
injectivity radius and consider the geodesic ball
B_{\delta}(p)
.
The geodesic normal coordinates define a
map
\Phi\colon B_{\delta}(p)\to B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Let
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
be a positively oriented,
orthonormal tangent frame
on
B_{\delta}(p)
that satisfies (
2.45
).
With the notation of Subsection
2.3.2
, we let

v=(v_{1},\,v_{2})\colon[0,\,t]\times B_{\delta}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

be the unique vector field such that

(5.5)

\displaystyle u(t,\,\Phi^{-1}(x))=v^{1}(t,\,x)\,\tau_{1}(\Phi^{-1}(x))+v^{2}(t,\,x)\,\tau_{2}(\Phi^{-1}(x))

for any
(t,\,x)\in[0,\,t]\times B_{\delta}(0)
.
As we have seen already in Subsection
2.3.2
,
we may express the Ginzburg-Landau energy of
u
in
B_{\delta}(p)

in terms of the (Euclidean) Ginzburg-Landau energy of
v
,
see Equation (
2.51
).
By applying (
2.37
), (
2.38
)
and (
2.43
), we also obtain

(5.6)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{\delta}(p)}\left|\partial_{t}u\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t=\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})\right)\int_{0}^{T}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\left|\partial_{t}v\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

Now, we write the space-time vorticity in terms of its Euclidean counterpart.
We let
\bar{\jmath}(v)
be the (Euclidean) current of

v
with respect to the space variable
x
, namely

(5.7)

\bar{\jmath}(v):=\sum_{k=1}^{2}(\partial_{k}v,\,iv)\,\mathrm{d}x^{k}.

We define the form

(5.8)

\bar{V}:=\mathrm{d}\left((\partial_{t}v,\,iv)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\mathrm{d}_{t}\bar{\jmath}(v)

and the space-time Euclidean vorticity

(5.9)

\bar{J}(v):=\bar{V}+\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)

(which is consistent with
[
36
]
up to a factor
2
).

Lemma 5.1
.

Let

(5.10)

\mathfrak{R}:=\left(1-\left|v\right|^{2}\right)(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A},

where
\mathcal{A}
is the connection
1
-form induced by
the frame
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
, as in (
2.32
).
Then, we have

(5.11)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}V

\displaystyle=\bar{V}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}

(5.12)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\omega(u)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}

(5.13)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}J(u)

\displaystyle=\bar{J}(v)+\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}

Proof.

As a preliminary step, we will show that

(5.14)

(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}j_{Q}(u)=(\partial_{t}v,iv)\,\mathrm{d}t+\bar{\jmath}(v)-|v|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}.

We take the pull-back via
\Phi_{Q}^{-1}
of both sides of (
5.1
):

(5.15)

(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}j_{Q}(u)=(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\left((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}j(u)

We study separately the terms in the right-hand side of (
5.15
).
By differentiating both sides of (
5.5
)
with respect to
t
, we obtain

(5.16)

(\partial_{t}u)\circ\Phi_{Q}^{-1}=\partial_{t}v^{1}\,(\tau_{1}\circ\Phi^{-1})+\partial_{t}v^{2}\,(\tau_{2}\circ\Phi^{-1})

As a consequence,

(5.17)

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\left((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)&=((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\circ\Phi_{Q}^{-1})\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\left((\partial_{t}u)\circ\Phi_{Q}^{-1},\,i(u\circ\Phi_{Q}^{-1})\right)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:normal_representation_space_time},\eqref{plbk12}}}{{=}}(\partial_{t}v,\,iv)\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

Now, we consider the second term in the right-hand side of (
5.15
).
Let
u^{k}:=v^{k}\circ\Phi_{Q}
for
k=1,2
.
Equation (
5.5
)
may be written as

u(t,\,y)=u^{1}(t,\,y)\,\tau_{1}(y)+u^{2}(t,\,y)\,\tau_{2}(y)

for any
(t,\,y)\in[0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(p)
.
Then, the current of
u
(with respect to the space variables only)
may be written as

(5.18)

\begin{split}j(u)=(Du,\,iu)_{g}&=\left(\mathrm{d}u^{1}\otimes\tau_{1}+u^{1}\,D\tau_{1}+\mathrm{d}u^{2}\otimes\tau_{2}+u^{2}\,D\tau_{2},\,u^{1}\,\tau_{2}-u^{2}\,\tau_{1}\right)_{g}\\
&=u^{1}\mathrm{d}u^{2}-u^{2}\mathrm{d}u^{1}-\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}\mathcal{A}\end{split}

We take the pull-back via
\Phi_{Q}^{-1}
of both sides of (
5.18
).
Since the pull-back commutes with the exterior differential,
and since
(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}u^{k}=u^{k}\circ\Phi_{Q}^{-1}=v^{k}
for
k=1,2
,
we obtain

(5.19)

(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}j(u)=\bar{\jmath}(v)-\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A},

Equation (
5.14
) now follows from (
5.16
),
(
5.17
) and (
5.19
).

Proof of (
5.11
).

We take the pull-back via
\Phi_{Q}^{-1}
of both sides of (
5.3
).
As the pull-back operator is linear and commutes with the exterior derivative,
we obtain

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}V&=(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathrm{d}\left((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{t}j(u)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\left((\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}((\partial_{t}u,\,iu)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t)\right)+\mathrm{d}_{t}\left((\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}j(u)\right)\\
\end{split}

We apply (
5.17
) and (
5.19
) to the right-hand side:

(5.20)

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}V&=\mathrm{d}\left((\partial_{t}v,\,iv)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\mathrm{d}_{t}\bar{\jmath}(v)-\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:flat_V}}}{{=}}\bar{V}-\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\end{split}

As the form
(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}
only depends
on the variables in
M
, not on
t
, we have

(5.21)

-\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)=\mathrm{d}_{t}\left((1-\left|v\right|^{2})(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)=\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}

Equation (
5.11
) follows from (
5.20
) and (
5.21
).

Proof of (
5.12
).

We take the exterior differential
\mathrm{d}_{Q}
of both sides of (
5.14
):

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{Q}j_{Q}(u)=\mathrm{d}\left((\partial_{t}v,iv)\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\mathrm{d}_{t}\bar{\jmath}(v)+\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)-\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)-\mathrm{d}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\end{split}

The right-hand side may be further simplified
by applying (
5.3
) and (
5.20
):

(5.22)

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{Q}j_{Q}(u)&=\bar{V}+\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)-\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)-\mathrm{d}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\\
&=(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}V+\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)-\mathrm{d}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\end{split}

On the other hand, by taking the pull-back via
\Phi_{Q}^{-1}

in both sides of (
5.2
), we obtain

(5.23)

(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{Q}\,j_{Q}(u)=(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}V+(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\omega(u)-(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}(\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g})

Comparing (
5.22
) with (
5.23
) gives

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\omega(u)=\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)-\mathrm{d}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)+(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}(\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g})\end{split}

and hence, recalling that
\mathrm{d}\mathcal{A}=\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}
,

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\omega(u)&=\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)-\mathrm{d}\left(\left|v\right|^{2}\right)\wedge(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}+\left(1-\left|v\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\left((\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)+\mathrm{d}\left(1-\left|v\right|^{2}\right)\wedge(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}+\left(1-\left|v\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\left((\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\right)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v)+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}\end{split}

Proof of (
5.13
).

We take the pull-back in Equation (
5.4
)
and apply (
5.11
), (
5.12
):

\begin{split}(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}J(u)=(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}V+(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\omega(u)=\bar{V}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}+\bar{\jmath}(v)+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}\end{split}

Equation (
5.13
) follows, thanks
to (
5.9
).
∎

In the next Proposition
\mathscr{M}_{2}(M)
will denote the space of
2
-forms whose coefficients are bounded Radon measures on
M
.
\mathscr{M}_{2}(M)
is endowed with the flat norm (i.e., the
W^{-1,1}
-norm).

Proposition 5.2

(Space-Time Compactness)
.

Given
u_{\varepsilon}:[0,\,T]\times M\to TM
such that

(5.24)

\displaystyle\max_{[0,\,T]\times M}|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}\leq C,

(5.25)
