\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(1-|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g})^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq C|\log\varepsilon|,\,\,\,\,\forall t\in[0,T],

(5.26)

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C|\log\varepsilon|,

for some positive constant
C
independent of
\varepsilon
, we let
V_{\varepsilon}
be defined, starting from
u_{\varepsilon}
, as in (
5.3
) and let
\omega(u_{\varepsilon}):=\mathrm{d}j(u_{\varepsilon})+\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}
.
Then, there exist
J\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
,

V\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
and
\mu\in C^{0,1/2}([0,T];\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
such that (up to subsequences)

(5.27)

\displaystyle J(u_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}J\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(Q)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)

(5.28)

\displaystyle\omega(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\mu(t)\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)\textrm{ and }t\in[0,\,T]

(5.29)

\displaystyle V_{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}V\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(Q)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)

and

(5.30)

J=\mu+V.

Moreover, the measure
\mu
has the form

(5.31)

\mu(t)=2\pi\sum_{j=i}^{n(t)}d_{j}(t)\delta_{a_{j}(t)},

where
n:[0,T]\to\mathbb{N}
and
d_{j}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}
,

a_{j}(t)\in M
for all
t\in[0,T]
,
j=1,\ldots,n(t)
.

Proof.

We split the proof into steps.

Step 1

(Localization)
.

Let
\delta>0
be a small parameter.
Let
\mathfrak{B}=\left\{B^{1}_{\delta},\ldots,B^{N}_{\delta}\right\}

be a finite open cover of
M
by geodesic balls of radius
\delta
.
Each ball
B^{k}_{\delta}
is equipped with normal geodesic coordinates

\Phi^{k}\colon B^{k}_{\delta}\to B_{\delta}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The cover
\mathfrak{B}
induces a finite cover of
Q=[0,\,T]\times M
,

[0,\,T]\times\mathfrak{B}:=\left\{[0,\,T]\times B^{1}_{\delta},\,\ldots,\,[0,\,T]\times B^{N}_{\delta}\right\}

and
[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}
is equipped with
coordinates
\Phi^{k}_{Q}=(\Id,\,\Phi^{k})
. For any
k
,
we choose an orthonormal, positively oriented,
tangent frame
\{\tau_{1}^{k},\,\tau_{2}^{k}\}
defined on
B^{j}_{\delta}
.
We represent
{u_{\varepsilon}}_{|[0,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}
by a
map
v_{\varepsilon}^{k}\colon[0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
as in (
5.5
).
The assumptions (
5.25
) and (
5.26
),
combined with (
2.51
)
and (
5.6
), imply

(5.32)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\delta}(0)}\left(|\nabla v_{\varepsilon}^{k}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}(1-|v_{\varepsilon}^{k}|^{2})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}x\leq C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|\qquad\textrm{for all }t\in[0,\,T],

(5.33)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)}|\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}^{k}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|

Let
\bar{\jmath}(v_{\varepsilon}^{k})
,
\bar{V}_{\varepsilon}^{k}
and
\bar{J}(v_{\varepsilon}^{k})

be defined as in (
5.7
), (
5.8
)
and (
5.9
), respectively.
We can now apply the Jerrard-Soner compactness result for Jacobians

[
21
, Theorem 3.1]

and the Sandier-Serfaty product estimates

[
36
, Theorem 3]
). We find a (non-relabelled) subsequence and

\bar{\mu}^{k}\in L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,\mathscr{M}_{2}(B_{\delta}(0))),\quad\bar{V}^{k}\in L^{2}(0,\,T;\,\mathscr{M}_{2}(B_{\delta}(0)))\qquad\textrm{for }k=1,\,\ldots,\,N

such that for any
k=1,\,\ldots,\,N
and
p\in(1,\,2)
,

(5.34)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v_{\varepsilon}^{k})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\bar{\mu}^{k},\quad\bar{V}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\bar{V}^{k}\qquad

\displaystyle\textrm{in }W^{-1,p}([0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0))

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v_{\varepsilon}^{k}(t))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\bar{\mu}^{k}(t)\qquad

\displaystyle\textrm{in }W^{-1,p}(B_{\delta}(0))\quad\textrm{for any }t\in[0,\,T]

Step 2
.

For any
k
, let
\mathcal{A}^{k}
be the connection
1
-form
induced by the choice of the frame
\{\tau_{1}^{k},\,\tau_{2}^{k}\}
.
Let

\mathfrak{R}^{k}_{\varepsilon}:=\left(1-|v_{\varepsilon}^{k}|^{2}\right)(\Phi_{Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}^{k}

The energy estimate (
5.32
) implies that

(5.36)

\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}\|\mathfrak{R}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}(B_{\delta}(0))}\lesssim\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}\|1-|v_{\varepsilon}^{k}|\|_{L^{2}(B_{\delta}(0))}\lesssim\varepsilon\left|\log\varepsilon\right|

Therefore, for any
k
we have

(5.37)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\qquad

\displaystyle\textrm{in }W^{-1,2}([0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)),

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\qquad

\displaystyle\textrm{in }L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,W^{-1,2}(B_{\delta}(0))),

We combine (
5.34
) with (
5.37
),
(
5.38
) and Lemma
5.1

to obtain

J(u_{\varepsilon})_{|[0,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}=\Phi_{Q}^{*}\left(\bar{J}(v_{\varepsilon}^{k})+\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}^{k}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\right)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\Phi_{Q}^{*}\left(\bar{V}^{k}+\bar{\mu}^{k}\right)=:J^{k}

in
W^{-1,p}([0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta})
, for any
p\in(1,\,2)

and
k=1,\,\ldots,\,N
. (A duality argument shows that the
pull-back via a smooth map preserves the convergence in
W^{-1,p}
.)
Now, let
\left\{\eta_{1}^{2},\ldots,\eta_{N}^{2}\right\}

be a partition of unity subordinate to
\mathfrak{B}
.
We have
\hbox{supp}\,(\eta_{k}^{2})\subset B^{k}_{\delta}

for every
k=1,\ldots,N
and hence,

J(u_{\varepsilon})=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\eta_{k}^{2}\,J(u_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\eta_{k}^{2}\,J^{k}=:J

in
W^{-1,p}([0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta})
for any
p\in(1,\,2)
.
This proves (
5.27
). The proof of (
5.28
)
and (
5.29
) is analogous.
Since
J(u_{\varepsilon})=\omega(u_{\varepsilon})+V_{\varepsilon}
(see (
5.4
)),
Equation (
5.30
) follows in the limit.

Step 3
.

By combining Equation (
5.2
)
with (
5.3
), we can write

(5.39)

\mathrm{d}_{Q}j_{Q}(u_{\varepsilon})=V_{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{d}j(u_{\varepsilon})

The smoothness of
u_{\varepsilon}
is smooth, and hence of
\mathrm{d}_{Q}j_{Q}(u_{\varepsilon})
,
implies that
\mathrm{d}_{Q}(\mathrm{d}_{Q}j_{Q}(u_{\varepsilon}))=0
,

\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{d}j(u_{\varepsilon}))=0
and, by differentiating (
5.3
),

\mathrm{d}_{t}V_{\varepsilon}=0
. By taking the exterior differential
\mathrm{d}_{Q}

of both sides of (
5.39
), we obtain

0=\mathrm{d}V_{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathrm{d}j(u_{\varepsilon})=\mathrm{d}V_{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(\omega(u_{\varepsilon})-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)=\mathrm{d}V_{\varepsilon}+\mathrm{d}_{t}\,\omega(u_{\varepsilon})

Therefore, by taking the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
we obtain

(5.40)

\partial_{t}\mu+\mathrm{d}V=0\qquad\hbox{in the sense of distributions. }

Step 4
.

The
L^{2}
-in-time regularity of
V
gives, exactly as in the Euclidean case (with similar proof) the time regularity of
\mu
, namely that

\mu\in C^{0,1/2}([0,T];\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
.
More precisely,
using the transport equation (
5.40
) we obtain that (up to considering smooth approximations of
V
and
\mu
, see
[
36
, Theorem 3]
), for any smooth
\psi:M\to\mathbb{R}
and any
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}<T

(5.41)

\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]\times M}V\wedge\mathrm{d}_{Q}\psi=-\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]\times M}\mathrm{d}V\wedge\psi=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]\times M}\mathrm{d}_{t}(\mu\wedge\psi)=\int_{M}(\mu(t_{2})-\mu(t_{1}))\psi.

Therefore, since
V\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
, for any smooth
\psi\in C^{0,1}(M)
with
\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
in
M
we have

(5.42)

\left|\int_{M}(\mu(t_{2})-\mu(t_{1}))\psi\right|\leq\|V\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))}\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}}.

Thus,

\left\|\mu(t_{2})-\mu(t_{1})\right\|_{\text{flat}}\leq\|V\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))}\sqrt{t_{2}-t_{1}},

namely
\mu\in C^{0,1/2}([0,T],\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
. In particular, (
5.42
) gives that
t\mapsto\int_{M}\mu(t)\psi
is in
C^{0,1/2}(0,T)
for any
\psi\in C^{0,1}(M)
(with
\|\nabla\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
).
As a result, we have (
5.28
). Moreover
thanks to (
5.25
), we apply for any fixed
t\in(0,T)

Proposition
4.1
and we conclude that
the measure
\mu
has the form
(
5.31
). ∎

Proposition 5.3

(Space-Time Product Estimates)
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,\,T]\times M\to TM
be
a sequence that satisfies (
5.24
),
(
5.25
), (
5.26
).
Let
J\in L^{2}(0,\,T;\,\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
be given by
(
5.30
). Then,
for any continuous vector fields
X=X^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}

and
Y=f\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
on
Q
, there holds

(5.43)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\int_{Q}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{Q}f^{2}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\geq\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q}J[X,Y]\right|.

Proof.

The proof again uses a localization argument and the analogous lower bound
in the Euclidean case. The details are as follows.

As in Proposition
5.2
, we consider a finite cover

\mathfrak{B}=\{B_{\delta}^{1},\,\ldots,\,B_{\delta}^{N}\}
by geodesic balls
and a partition of the unity
\{\eta_{1}^{2},\,\ldots,\,\eta_{N}^{2}\}

subordined to
\mathfrak{B}
. We represent
{u_{\varepsilon}}_{|[0,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}

by a map
v_{\varepsilon}^{k}\colon[0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
as in (
5.5
).
By equation (
5.13
) (Lemma
5.1
),
we have

(5.44)

(\Phi_{k,Q}^{-1})^{*}J(u_{\varepsilon})=\bar{J}(v_{\varepsilon}^{k})+\mathrm{d}_{t}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}^{k}+\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}^{k}

where
\Phi_{k,Q}\colon[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}\to[0,\,T]\to B_{\delta}(0)

is the coordinate map and

(5.45)

\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}^{k}:=\left(1-|v_{\varepsilon}^{k}|^{2}\right)(\Phi_{k,Q}^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\qquad\textrm{in }L^{2}([0,\,t]\times B_{\delta}(0))

as in (
5.36
). By (
5.27
) and (
5.34
),
we may extract a subsequence in such a way that
J(u_{\varepsilon})\to J

in
W^{-1,p}(Q)
and
\bar{J}(v_{\varepsilon})\to\bar{J}^{k}

in
W^{-1,p}([0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0))
, for any
k
.
Due to (
5.44
), (
5.45
), we must have

(5.46)

(\Phi_{k,Q}^{-1})^{*}J=\bar{J}^{k}

Then, for any continuous fields
X=X^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}

and
Y=f\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
on
Q
,

(5.47)

\begin{split}\int_{Q}J[X,\,Y]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t&=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}\eta_{k}^{2}\,J[X,\,Y]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\int_{[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}\bar{\eta}_{k}^{2}\,\bar{J}^{k}[\bar{X}_{k},\,\bar{Y}_{k}]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

where
\bar{\eta}_{k}:=\eta_{k}\circ\Phi_{k}^{-1}
and

\bar{X}_{k}
,
\bar{Y}_{k}
are the push-forward of
X
,
Y

via the coordinate map
\Phi_{k,Q}
.

Let
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
be any sequence of positive numbers.
We have

(5.48)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\eta_{k}D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|\eta_{k}f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

For any
k
, we have (see (
2.50
))

(5.49)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\eta_{k}D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|\eta_{k}f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad\qquad=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})\right)\int_{[0,\,T]\times\bar{B}_{\delta}}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\bar{\eta}_{k}\nabla_{\bar{X}_{k}}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{f}_{k}\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})\right)\int_{[0,\,T]\times\bar{B}_{\delta}}\mathrm{O}(|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

where
\bar{f}_{k}:=\eta_{k}\circ\Phi_{k,Q}^{-1}
. The last integral
in the right-hand side of (
5.49
) is
\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})
,
because of the assumption (
5.24
).
Now, the product estimate of Sandier & Serfaty

[
36
, Theorem 3]
, applied on
[0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)
,
implies

(5.50)

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{[0,\,T]\times\bar{B}_{\delta}}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\bar{\eta}_{k}\nabla_{\bar{X}_{k}}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{f}_{k}\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\geq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\left(\int_{[0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)}|\bar{\eta}_{k}\nabla_{\bar{X}_{k}}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{[0,\,T]\times B_{\delta}(0)}|\bar{\eta}_{k}\bar{f}_{k}\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,T]\times\bar{B}_{\delta}}\bar{\eta}^{2}_{k}\,\bar{J}[\bar{X}_{k},\,\bar{Y}_{k}]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

As a result, combining (
5.50
),
(
5.49
) and (
5.48
) we obtain

(5.51)

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|\eta_{k}D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|\eta_{k}f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\qquad\qquad\geq\left(1+\mathrm{O}(\delta^{2})\right)\int_{[0,\,T]\times B^{k}_{\delta}}\frac{1}{2}\eta_{k}^{2}\,J[X,\,Y]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

We sum over
k
, apply (
5.47
) and let
\delta\to 0
.
We obtain

\begin{split}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{Q}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q}J[X,\,Y]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

The same argument, applied with
-X
instead of
X
, shows that

(5.52)

\begin{split}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{Q}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q}J[X,\,Y]\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|\end{split}

Finally, we take

\lambda_{\varepsilon}:=\left(\int_{Q}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{-1/2}\left(\int_{Q}|f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}

if both integrals are strictly positive; otherwise,
if either of the integrals is equal to zero,
we choose
\lambda_{\varepsilon}>0
in such a way that

\int_{Q}\lambda_{\varepsilon}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}|f\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\varepsilon

Then, (
5.43
) follows from (
5.52
).
∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}:[0,\,T]\times M\to TM
be a sequence of vector fields satysfying

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq C|\log\varepsilon|,

\displaystyle\int_{[0,\,T]\times M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C|\log\varepsilon|

and

(5.53)

\frac{1}{2}\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}\int_{M}|Du_{\varepsilon}(t)|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n|\log\varepsilon|(1+o_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1))

Assume that there exist a continuous curve
\mathbf{a}:[0,T]\to M^{n}
and a vector

\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
with
d_{j}=\pm 1
for
j=1,\ldots,n
such that for any
t\in[0,T]

(5.54)

\omega(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ in }\,\,(C^{0,\alpha}_{c}(M))^{\prime},\,\,\,\,\,\forall\alpha\in(0,1).

Let
[t_{1},\,t_{2}]\subset[0,T]
be an interval such that
a_{j}(t)\neq a_{k}(t)
for any
t\in[t_{1},\,t_{2}]
and
j\neq k
. Then,
we have
{\bf a}\in H^{1}(t_{1},\,t_{2};\,M^{n})
and

(5.55)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq\pi\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|a_{k}^{\prime}|^{2}_{g}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\pi\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|{\bf a}^{\prime}|^{2}_{g}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Proof.

The proof follows the one in the Euclidean case in
[
36
, Corollary 7]
.
Let
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T
as in the hypothesis, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and set
\tau:=\frac{t_{2}-t_{1}}{N}
and
t^{k}:=t_{1}+\tau k
for
k=0,1,\ldots,N
.
In the whole interval
[0,T]
there is a finite number of vortices. Moreover
in the interval
[t_{1},t_{2}]

the vortices
a_{j}
(
j=1,\ldots,n
) remain distinct. Therefore,
taking
N
large enough,
for any interval
[t^{k},t^{k+1})
we can find
n
balls
B_{j}
(
j=1,\ldots,n
) such that each of them contains exactly one vortice
a_{j}(t)
for
t\in[t^{k},t^{k+1})
.

Now, for any
k=1,\ldots,N
and for any
j=1,\ldots,d
, we let
\psi\in C^{1}_{c}(B_{j})
with
|\nabla\psi|\leq 1
.
Then, (
5.43
) with
X=\nabla^{\perp}\psi
,
Y=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
, together with (
2.23
) (with
\omega=V
) give

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|^{2}}\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\geq\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}V\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi\right|^{2}\end{split}

Moreover, (
5.41
) gives that

\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}V\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi=2\pi d_{j}\left(\psi(a_{j}(t^{k+1})-a_{j}(t^{k})\right).

Therefore,

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|^{2}}\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\right)\\
&\hskip 113.81102pt\geq\pi^{2}\left|\psi(a_{j}(t^{k+1}))-\psi_{\delta}(a_{j}(t^{k}))\right|^{2}.\end{split}

Now, we apply Lemma
4.2
(specifically (
4.7
) with
X=Y=\nabla^{\perp}\psi_{\delta}
)
with
v_{\varepsilon}=u_{\varepsilon}(t)
(with
t
fixed in
[t^{k},t^{k+1}]
) and we get

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{j}}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}(t)|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi|\nabla^{\perp}\psi(a_{j}(t))|^{2}_{g}=\pi|\nabla\psi(a_{j}(t))|^{2}_{g},

therefore, since
|\nabla\psi|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\tau\pi}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big(\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|^{2}}\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|D_{X}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\pi^{2}\left|\psi(a_{j}(t^{k+1}))-\psi(a_{j}(t^{k}))\right|^{2}.

Thus, if we take the supremum over
\psi\in C^{1}_{c}(M)
with
\left\|\nabla\psi\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
,
we get

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{j}\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\geq\pi\frac{\dist_{g}^{2}(a_{j}(t^{k+1}),a_{j}(t^{k}))}{\tau},

and therefore, summing on
j=1,\ldots,n
,

(5.56)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{\Omega\times[t^{k},t^{k+1}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\geq\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\dist_{g}^{2}(a_{j}(t^{k+1}),a_{j}(t^{k}))}{\tau}.

Given
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
,
we define its piecewise constant interpolant
\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{\tau}
by

\bar{{\bf a}}_{\tau}(t):={\bf a}(t^{k+1})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for }t\in[t^{k},t^{k+1}).

Note that since
{\bf a}
is continuous we have that
\bar{{\bf a}}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{\tau\to 0}a
uniformly in
[t_{1},t_{2}]
.
Moreover, we define

\left|\bar{{\bf a}}_{\tau}^{\prime}\right|_{g}(t):=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\dist_{g}^{2}(a_{j}(t^{k+1}),a_{j}(t^{k}))}{\tau^{2}}\right)^{1/2},\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for }t\in[t^{k},t^{k+1}).

Note that, as observed in
[
3
, Eq. 2.3.4.]
, the notation is justified since when one reduces to a linear framework, e.g.
M=\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, then
\left|\bar{\mathbf{a}}_{\tau}^{\prime}\right|_{g}(t)
is the norm of the derivative of the piecewise interpolation of the values
\mathbf{a}(t^{k})
, for
k=1,\ldots,N
.

We set

L:=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\pi|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{\Omega\times[t_{1},t_{2}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t,

and thus (
5.56
) rewrites as

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|\bar{{\bf a}}_{\tau}^{\prime}|^{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq L.

We assume that
L<+\infty
(otherwise there is nothing to prove). Therefore, there exists
A\in L^{2}(t_{1},t_{2})
and a subsequence of
\tau
(not relabelled) such that

|\bar{{\bf a}}_{\tau}^{\prime}|\xrightarrow{\tau\to 0}A\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ weakly in }L^{2}(t_{1},t_{2}),

and thus

\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|A|^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq L.

Now, we show that
{\bf a}\in H^{1}(t_{1},t_{2};M)
with metric derivative
|a^{\prime}|_{g}(t)
such that

|{\bf a}^{\prime}|_{g}(t)\leq A(t)\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for a.a. }t\in(t_{1},t_{2}).

We fix
t_{1}\leq s<t\leq t_{2}
and we set

\overline{s}_{\tau}:=\max\left\{t^{k}\colon t^{k}\leq s\right\}\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ and }\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\underline{t}_{\tau}:=\min\left\{t^{k}\colon t^{k}\geq t\right\}.

Note that
\overline{s}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{\tau\to 0}s
and
\underline{t}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{\tau\to 0}t
.
Then, the triangle inequality gives

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dist_{g}(a_{j}(\overline{s}_{\tau}),a_{j}(\underline{t}_{\tau}))\leq\int_{\overline{s}_{\tau}}^{\underline{t}_{\tau}}|\bar{\bf a}^{\prime}|_{g}(r)\mathrm{d}r.

Therefore, if we let
\tau\searrow 0
, we get

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\dist_{g}(a_{j}(s),a_{j}(t))\leq\int_{s}^{t}A(r)\mathrm{d}r,

and thus since
A\in L^{2}(t_{1},t_{2})
, thanks to Proposition
2.3
we get that
{\bf a}\in H^{1}(t_{1},t_{2};M)

and

|{\bf a}^{\prime}|_{g}(t)\leq A(t)\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for a.a. }t\in(t_{1},t_{2}).

Therefore, we conclude

\pi\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}|{\bf a}^{\prime}|_{g}^{2}(t)\mathrm{d}t\leq\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{\Omega\times[t_{1},t_{2}]}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t.\qed

Given a
1
-form
\omega\in L^{2}(M,\,\mathrm{T}^{*}M)
, we denote by
\mathbb{P}\omega

the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection of
\omega
onto the space of
harmonic
1
-forms
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
. Equivalently,
\mathbb{P}\omega
is the unique
element of
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
such that

(5.57)

\int_{M}\left(\omega-\mathbb{P}\omega,\,\eta\right)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0\qquad\textrm{ for any }\eta\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

Since the space
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
is finite-dimensional, we can choose
an orthonormal basis
(\eta_{\ell})_{\ell=1}^{2\mathfrak{g}}
of
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

and write
\mathbb{P}\omega
as

(5.58)

\mathbb{P}\omega=\sum_{\ell=1}^{2\mathfrak{g}}\left(\int_{M}(\omega,\,\eta_{\ell})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)\eta_{\ell}\qquad\textrm{for any }\omega\in L^{2}(M,\,\mathrm{T}^{*}M).

The representation (
5.58
) shows that
\mathbb{P}

extends to a linear, bounded operator
L^{1}(M,\,T^{\prime}M)\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}:[0,\,T]\times M\to TM
be a sequence
that satisfies the assumptions (
5.24
),
(
5.26
), (
5.54
) and

(5.59)

\sup_{t\in[0,\,T]}F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+C

for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C
.
Then, there exists a map
\xi\in H^{1}(0,\,T;\,\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))

and a (non-relabelled) subsequence such that

(5.60)

\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\xi(t)\qquad\textrm{for any }t\in[0,\,T].

As
\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
is a finite dimensional space, the
convergence (
5.60
) holds in any norm.

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Due to the assumptions (
5.24
), (
5.54
)
and (
5.59
), we can apply Corollary
4.5

to the sequence
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
, at any fixed
t\in[0,\,T]
. As
a consequence, for any
p\in(1,\,2)
there exists a constant
C_{p}

such that

\left\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,L^{p}(M))}\leq C_{p}

We deduce

\left\|j(u_{\varepsilon})\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,L^{p}(M))}\leq\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}((0,\,T)\times M)}\left\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,L^{p}(M))}\leq C_{p}

and hence, we can extract a subsequence such that

j(u_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}j\qquad\textrm{weakly}^{*}\quad\textrm{in }L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,L^{p}(M))

As the operator
\mathbb{P}\colon L^{1}(M,\,T^{\prime}M)\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)

is linear and bounded, we also have

(5.61)

\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}\mathbb{P}j=:\xi\qquad\textrm{weakly}^{*}\quad\textrm{in }L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))

We claim that the limit
\xi
belongs to
H^{1}(0,\,T;\,\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))

(and hence, is continuous in time).
Let
V_{\varepsilon}
be the
2
-form on
Q:=[0,\,T]\times M

associated with
u_{\varepsilon}
, as in (
5.3
).
Let
\eta\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
, and let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}(0,\,1)

be a (scalar) test function. We have

\begin{split}I_{\varepsilon}&:=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon}),\,\eta)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{harmproj}}}{{=}}\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(j(u_{\varepsilon}),\,\eta)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,\mathrm{d}_{t}\left(j(u_{\varepsilon})\wedge\star\eta\right)=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,(\mathrm{d}_{t}j(u_{\varepsilon}))\wedge\star\eta\end{split}

(we recall that
\star
denotes the Hodge dual operator in
M
).
Let
\alpha_{\varepsilon}:=(\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon},\,iu_{\varepsilon})_{g}
.
By (
5.3
), we know that

V_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{d}(\alpha_{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{d}t)+\mathrm{d}_{t}j(u_{\varepsilon})=\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}\wedge\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}_{t}j(u_{\varepsilon})

Therefore,

\begin{split}I_{\varepsilon}=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,V_{\varepsilon}\wedge\star\eta-\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}\wedge\mathrm{d}t\wedge\star\eta\end{split}

The form
\eta
is harmonic, hence co-closed, so
\mathrm{d}(\star\eta)=0
.
This implies

\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}\wedge\mathrm{d}t\wedge\star\eta=-\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\varepsilon}\wedge\star\eta\wedge\mathrm{d}t=-\mathrm{d}(\alpha_{\varepsilon}\star\eta)\wedge\mathrm{d}t

and hence,

(5.62)

\begin{split}I_{\varepsilon}=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,V_{\varepsilon}\wedge\star\eta+\int_{0}^{T}\varphi(t)\left(\int_{M}\mathrm{d}(\alpha_{\varepsilon}\star\eta)\right)\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,V_{\varepsilon}\wedge\star\eta\end{split}

by Stokes theorem. Proposition
5.2
states
that there exists
V\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
such that
V_{\varepsilon}\to V

in
W^{-1,p}(Q)
, for any
p\in(1,\,2)
. On the other hand,
the convergence (
5.61
) implies

\partial_{t}\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}\partial_{t}\xi

in
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(0,\,T;\,\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
.
Therefore, we can pass to the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
5.62
):

\begin{split}\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}(\xi,\,\eta)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,\,V\wedge\star\eta\end{split}

We deduce

\begin{split}\int_{Q}\varphi(t)\,(\partial_{t}\xi,\,\eta)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\left\|V\right\|_{L^{2}(0,\,T;\,\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))}\left\|\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}(0,\,T)}\left\|\eta\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\end{split}

and, since
\varphi
and
\eta
are arbitrary,

\partial_{t}\xi\in L^{2}(0,\,T;\,\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
.

It only remains to prove (
5.60
); we apply the arguments
from
[
36
, Theorem 3]
. Let
t_{0}\in[0,\,T)
.
Corollary
4.5
implies, as above, that

\left\|j(u_{\varepsilon}(t_{0}))\right\|_{L^{p}(M)}\leq C_{p}
. Then,
we can extract a subsequence (depending on
t_{0}
)
in such a way that
\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon}(t_{0}))\to\zeta\in\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
.
We claim that
\zeta=\xi(t_{0})
. If we prove this claim,
we will deduce that all the subsequences of
\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon}(t_{0}))

must converge to the same limit, and (
5.60
) will follow.
Consider the sequence
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\colon[-1,\,T]\times M\to\mathrm{T}M
,

\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}(t):=\begin{cases}u_{\varepsilon}(t_{0})&\textrm{if }t\leq t_{0}\\
u_{\varepsilon}(t)&\textrm{if }t>t_{0}\end{cases}

The sequence
\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the
assumptions (
5.24
), (
5.26
)
and (
5.59
). Proposition
5.2

implies that
\omega(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}(t))\to\bar{\mu}(t)
in
W^{-1,p}(M)

for any
t\in[-1,\,T]
, where

\bar{\mu}(t):=\begin{cases}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t_{0})}&\textrm{if }t\leq t_{0}\\
2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}&\textrm{if }t>t_{0}\end{cases}

Then, (
5.54
) is satisfied, too.
By the previous arguments, we can extract a subsequence
in such a way that

\mathbb{P}j(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}\bar{\xi}\qquad\textrm{weakly}^{*}\quad\textrm{in }L^{\infty}(0,\,T;\,\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))

Using the definition of weak
∗
convergence, we can check that

\bar{\xi}(t)=\begin{cases}\zeta&\textrm{for a.e. }t<t_{0}\\
\xi(t)&\textrm{for a.e. }t>t_{0}\end{cases}

However, the arguments above imply that both
\xi
and
\bar{\xi}

are continuous functions of
t
; therefore, we must have
\zeta=\xi(t_{0})
,
as claimed. In case
t=T
, we define

\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}\colon[0,\,T+1]\times M\to\mathrm{T}M
,

\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}(t):=\begin{cases}u_{\varepsilon}(t)&\textrm{if }t<T\\
u_{\varepsilon}(T)&\textrm{if }t\geq T\end{cases}

and proceed similarly.
∎

5.2.
Vortex dynamics: Proof of Theorem
1

In this Subsection we finally prove our main result, Theorem
1
.

The proof of Theorem
1
is based on the abstract scheme developed by Sandier & Serfaty
[
35
, Theorem 1.4]
). The core of the Proof is contained in the next Propositions
5.7
,
5.8
and
5.9
.

We consider
well-prepared initial conditions
.
To ease the reading, we recall the definition.
Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
n\geq 1
, we
consider
(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
(see (
3.1
))
such that
d_{j}=\pm 1
for any
j=1,\ldots,n

and
\xi^{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d})
.
We assume that the initial conditions

u^{0}_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
satisfy

(5.63)

\displaystyle\omega(u^{0}_{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}^{0}}\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)

(5.64)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{0})\leq\pi n|\log\varepsilon|+W(\mathbf{a}^{0},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi^{0})+n\gamma+o(1),

(5.65)

\displaystyle\left\|u^{0}_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\leq 1.

By standard parabolic theory, we know that for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a smooth solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of (
1.4
).

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a solution of (
1.4
) with
u_{\varepsilon}^{0}
satisfying
(
5.64
) and (
5.65
).
Then,

(5.66)

\|u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(M\times(0,T))}\leq 1,

(5.67)

F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq\pi n|\log\varepsilon|+C,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for a.a. }t\in[0,T].

and

(5.68)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\leq C|\log\varepsilon|.

for some constant
C>0
independent of
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The fact that
u_{\varepsilon}
verifies (
5.67
) is a consequence of the fact that
any solution of (
1.4
) with the specified regularity satisfies the following energy estimate.

(5.69)

\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t+F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))=F_{\varepsilon}(u_{0})\leq\pi n|\log\varepsilon|+C,

where
C
denote a constant independent of
\varepsilon
.
Therefore (
5.67
) follows.
Set
v_{\varepsilon}(x,t|\log\varepsilon|):=u_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
for
(x,t)\in[0,\,T]\times M
. Then,
v_{\varepsilon}
solves

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\partial_{t}v_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{g}v_{\varepsilon}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}-1)v_{\varepsilon}=0,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ a.e. in }M\times(0,T)\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}(x,0)=u_{0}\,\,\,\,\hbox{ a.e. in }M.\end{cases}

Then, test (in the scalar product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{g}
) with
v_{\varepsilon}
.
We get

\partial_{t}|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}-\Delta_{g}|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+2|\mathrm{D}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}(|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}-1)=0.

Therefore, if we set
w_{\varepsilon}:=|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}-1
, we obtain that

\partial_{t}w_{\varepsilon}-\Delta_{g}w_{\varepsilon}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}w_{\varepsilon}\leq 0,

and thus
w_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq w_{\varepsilon}(0)\leq 0
, namely
|v_{\varepsilon}|_{g}\leq 1
.
By rescaling back with respect to time we obtain (
5.66
).

Finally, we discuss the validity of (
5.68
). In particular, note that that it is sufficient to prove that there exists some
T_{0}>0
such that

\int_{0}^{T_{0}}\int_{M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\leq|\log\varepsilon|,

but this has been already proved in
[
35
, Lemma 3.4]
. The argument uses a contradiction argument combined with the
product estimate
(
5.43
) and can be replicated verbatim.
∎

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a solution of (
1.4
) with
u_{\varepsilon}^{0}
satisfying
(
5.63
), (
5.64
) and (
5.65
).
Then, there exist
T^{*}\in(0,T]
, a curve
{\bf a}:[0,T^{*})\to M^{n}

with
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};M^{n})
integers
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\,\ldots,\,d_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}

such that
(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
for any
t\in[0,T^{*})
and a curve
\xi\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
such that for any
t\in[0,T^{*})

(5.70)

\displaystyle\omega(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2),

(5.71)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\xi(t),\qquad\hbox{ with }\qquad\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d}),

(5.72)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\geq\pi\int_{0}^{t}|{\bf a}^{\prime}|^{2}_{g}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

Moreover, for almost any
t\in[0,T^{*})
there exists a subsequence
\varepsilon_{h}(t)
and a vector field
u^{*}(t)\in W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)

(
p\in[1,2)
) such that
\left|u^{*}(t)\right|_{g}=1
a.e. in
M
and

(5.73)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)\rightharpoonup u^{*}(t)\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2),

(5.74)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}j(u^{*}(t))=-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2\pi\sum_{k=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{k}(t)}

(5.75)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{*}j(u^{*}(t))=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ in }M,

namely
u^{*}(t)
is a canonical harmonic vector field for
(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))
.

Proof.

We divide the proof in several steps.

Step 1

(Compactness)
.

Thanks to Lemma
5.6
and to Proposition
5.2

(see in particular (
5.28
)) we have that the exist

\mu\in C^{0,1/2}([0,T];\mathscr{M}_{2}(M))
such that for any
t\in[0,T]

(5.76)

\omega(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\to\mu(t)\qquad\hbox{ in }W^{-1,p}(M)\quad\textrm{for any }p\in(1,\,2)

Thanks to Proposition
4.1
for any
t\in(0,T)
the measure
\mu(t)
is of the form

(5.77)

\mu(t)=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n(t)}d_{j}(t)\delta_{a_{j}(t)},\,\,\,\hbox{ where }d_{j}\in\mathbb{Z},

where for any
t\in[0,T]

n(t)\in\mathbb{N}
and
a_{j}(t)\in M
for
j=1,\ldots,n(t)
with
a_{j}(t)\neq a_{k}(t)
for
j\neq k
and satisfies

(5.78)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))}{\pi|\log\varepsilon|}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n(t)}|d_{j}(t)|.

Therefore, since

F(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{0})\leq\pi n|\log\varepsilon|+C,

we conclude that for
t\in[0,T)
,

(5.79)

\sum_{j=1}^{n(t)}|d_{j}(t)|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}|d_{j}|.

The fact that the evolution decreases the total charge combined with the continuity of
t\mapsto\int_{M}\mu(t)\zeta
, for
\zeta\in C^{1}_{c}(M)
,
give (see
[
35
, Proposition 3.2]
) that there exists
T^{*}\in(0,T]

2
2

2

Note that since
u_{\varepsilon}
can be defined in the whole
(0,+\infty)
, we can always assume that
T^{*}\leq T
.

and
\mathbf{a}:[0,T^{*})\to M^{n}
with
\mathbf{a}\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};M^{n})
such that
a_{j}(t)\neq a_{k}(t)
for
j\neq k
and for any
t\in[0,T^{*})
. Moreover,
d_{j}(t)=d_{j}(0)=\pm 1
for
t\in[0,T^{*})
and thus
the measure
\mu
has the form

(5.80)

\mu(t)=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}

for any
t\in[0,T^{*})
.
Therefore, we are in the position to apply Proposition
5.4
to obtain (
5.72
).

Finally, we consider the sequence
\xi_{\varepsilon}:=\mathbb{P}(j(u_{\varepsilon}))
.
The sequence
u_{\varepsilon}
verifies all the hypothesis of Proposition
5.5
and therefore there exists
\xi\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
such that

(5.81)

\mathbb{P}(j(u_{\varepsilon}(t)))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\xi(t)\qquad\hbox{ for any }\quad t\in(0,T^{*}).

Step 2

(Convergence to a harmonic vector field)
.

We have

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{M}\left|\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|1-|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2})u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{M}|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t,

and hence, thanks to (
5.68
),

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{M}\left|\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\right)u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{C}{|\log\varepsilon|}.

Therefore

(5.82)

\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\right)u_{\varepsilon}\to 0\,\,\,\,\hbox{ in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(M)),

and, up to subsequence,

(5.83)

\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\right)u_{\varepsilon}\to 0\qquad\hbox{ in }L^{2}_{\tang}(M)\quad\hbox{ and for almost any }t\in(0,T^{*}).

We let
C\subseteq(0,T^{*})
the set of those
t
in which the above convergence holds.
We fix a
t\in C
.
Thanks to Lemma
4.3
and to Corollary
4.5
,
there exists a subsequence
\varepsilon_{h}(t)\xrightarrow{h\to+\infty}0
,
which may depend on the chosen
t
, and a vector field
u^{*}(t)\in H^{1}_{\tang,\text{loc}}(M\setminus\left\{a_{1}(t),\ldots,a_{d}(t)\right\})\cap W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)
such that
\left|u^{*}(t)\right|_{g}=1
and

(5.84)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)\to u^{*}(t)\,\,\,\hbox{ weakly in }H^{1}_{\tang,\text{loc}}(M\setminus\left\{a_{1}(t),\ldots,a_{d}(t)\right\}),

(5.85)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)\to u^{*}(t)\,\,\,\hbox{ weakly in }W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)\,\,\,\,p\in[1,2),

(5.86)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)\to u^{*}(t)\,\,\,\hbox{ strongly in }L^{p}_{\tang}(M)\,\,\,\,p\in[1,+\infty).

Since
Du_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)
converges weakly in
L^{p}_{\tang}(M)
for
p<2
and
u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)
converges strongly in
L^{p}_{\tang}(M)
for any
p<+\infty
we get that

(5.87)

j(u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t))\rightharpoonup j(u^{*}(t))\qquad\hbox{ in }\quad L^{p}(M)\quad\hbox{ for any }p\in[1,2).

Therefore, thanks to (
5.70
), we have that

(5.88)

\mathrm{d}j(u^{*}(t))=-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)},\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ for a.a. }t\in[0,T^{*}).

As a result, we have that

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Stokes}}}{{=}}\int_{M}\mathrm{d}j(u^{*}(t))=2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}-\int_{M}\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Gauss-Bonnet}}}{{=}}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}-2\pi\chi(M),

and thus we have that
(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
.

Now we prove that
\mathrm{d}^{*}j(u^{*}(t))=0
. This will imply that
u^{*}(t)
is indeed a canonical harmonic vector field.
To this end we let
\psi:M\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function.
Then, since

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(1-|u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}|^{2})(u_{\varepsilon_{h}}(t),iu_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)})_{g}\psi=0\qquad\hbox{ in }M

we readily have that

\lim_{h\to+\infty}\int_{M}\left(\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)},iu_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)\right)_{g}\psi\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0

and thus, thanks to Lemma
A.1
below, that

(5.89)

\lim_{h\to+\infty}\int_{M}\psi\,\mathrm{d}^{*}j(u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t))\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0.

Thus, thanks to (
5.87
), we have

0=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\int_{M}\psi\,\mathrm{d}^{*}j(u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t))\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\int_{M}\mathrm{d}\psi\,j(u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t))\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}\psi\,j(u^{*}(t))\mathrm{vol}_{g},

namely

(5.90)

\mathrm{d}^{*}j(u^{*}(t))=0.

Thus we conclude that
u^{*}(t)
is a canonical harmonic vector field.
Since
t
was indeed arbitrary on
C
we have proved that for almost any
t\in(0,T^{*})
we have that
(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}^{n}
with associated a canonical harmonic vector field
u^{*}(t)
.
Note that
u^{*}(t)
is obtained as a limit of
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
along a selected subsequence.
The continuity of
\mathbf{a}:[0,T^{*})\to M^{n}
actually implies that
(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})\in\mathscr{A}
for any
t\in[0,T^{*})
.
The fact that at time
t\in C
the vector field
u^{*}(t)
is a canonical harmonic vector field implies that the harmonic
1
-form
\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})
. Indeed, on the one hand the convergences (
5.81
) and (
5.87
) imply that

\xi(t)=\mathbb{P}j(u^{*}(t)).

On the other hand
we let
\Phi_{t}=\Phi(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})
be the
2
-form such that

(5.91)

\begin{cases}-\Delta\Phi=-\kappa\mathrm{vol}_{g}+2\pi\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}\\
\displaystyle\int_{M}\Phi=0.\end{cases}

Then,
(
5.88
) and (
5.90
)
imply that the
1
-form
j(u^{*}(t))-d^{*}\Phi_{t}\in\text{Harm}^{1}(M)
. Therefore
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]
, and the uniqueness of the projection imply that

\xi(t)=j(u^{*}(t))-\mathrm{d}^{*}\Phi_{t},\qquad\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})\quad\text{ for almost any }t\in[0,T^{*}).

The continuity of
\xi:[0,T^{*})\to\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M)
and of
\mathbf{a}:[0,T^{*})\to M^{n}
and the continuity of the lattice
\mathcal{L}
with respect to
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{d})
(see
[
19
, Lemma 2.3]
) give that

(5.92)

\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d})\qquad\hbox{ for any }\quad t\in[0,T^{*}).

Therefore,
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]
gives that
for any
t\in[0,T]
there exists a canonical harmonic vector field
u^{*}(t)
.
∎

Remark 5.1
.

Note that (
5.87
) can be extended to the whole interval
[0,T^{*})
since
j(u^{*}(t))
is uniquely identified by its differential, its co-differential and the harmonic
1
-form
\xi
, all of which are continuous with respect to time.
On the contrary, the vector field
u^{*}(t)
may not be obtained as a limit of
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
for
t\in[0,T]\setminus C
. Moreover, we have no information regarding the regularity of
u^{*}
with respect to time. Indeed the form
j(u^{*}(t))
identifies
u^{*}(t)
only up to an arbitrary global rotation.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a solution of (
1.4
), with
u_{\varepsilon}^{0}
satisfying
(
5.63
), (
5.64
) and (
5.65
).
Let
\mathbf{a}\colon[0,\,T^{*})\to M^{n}
,
\xi\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
be
as in Proposition
5.7
. Then, for a.e.
t\in(0,\,T^{*})
, we have

\begin{split}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{2}\int_{M}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left|\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))\right|_{g}^{2}\end{split}

Proof.

Let
t\in(0,\,T^{*})
be fixed, in such a way that

\frac{1}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}(t)\to 0
in
L^{2}(M)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
First of all, we extract a subsequence
\varepsilon_{h}(t)\to 0

(possibly depending on
t
) in such a way that

u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}(t)
converges to a canonical harmonic field
u^{*}(t)
and

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{2}\int_{M}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad\qquad=\lim_{h\to+\infty}\frac{\left|\log\varepsilon_{h}(t)\right|}{2}\int_{M}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{h}(t)^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}|^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon_{h}(t)}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\end{split}

We will then be able to extract further subsequences later on.
We work at fixed
t
and drop the dependence on
t
from the notation;
we write
\varepsilon
instead of
\varepsilon_{h}(t)
.
Let
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
be fixed, and let
\eta>0

be a small parameter. We choose an orthonormal basis

\{\hat{e}_{1},\,\hat{e}_{2}\}
of
T_{a_{j}}M
.
We fix geodesic normal coordinates
\{x^{1},\,x^{2}\}
centered in
a_{j}
in such a way that the fields

e_{k}:=\partial/\partial x^{k}
satisfy

e_{k}(a_{j})=\hat{e}_{k},\qquad\mathrm{div}\,e_{k}(a_{j})=0,\qquad\Gamma_{i,k}^{m}(a_{j})=0\quad\forall i,m,k,

where
\Gamma_{ik}^{m}
are the Cristhoffel symbols of the metric
g
. We let
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
be the geodesic ball centered at
a_{j}
(to simplify the notation, we write
B_{\eta}

instead of
B_{\eta}(a_{j})
) and let
\nu
be the outward-pointing unit normal to
\partial B_{\eta}
.

Let
k\in\{1,\,2\}
be fixed.
We multiply the equation (
1.4
) by
D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}
(
D_{k}
, as usual, stands for
D_{e_{k}}
)
and integrate over
B_{\eta}
. We obtain

(5.93)

\begin{split}\int_{B_{\eta}}\left(\frac{\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}}{|\log\varepsilon|},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}&+\int_{B_{\eta}}\left(-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B_{\eta}}\left((|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}-1)u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}\right)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0.\end{split}

We integrate by parts in the second and in
the third term in the left hand side.
The details of the computations are given in
Lemma
A.2
, Lemma
A.3

(see Appendix
A
below). We obtain

(5.94)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{\eta}}(\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}(\nu,e_{k})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&\qquad-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(D_{k}u_{\varepsilon},D_{\nu}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\int_{B_{\eta}}(Du_{\varepsilon},R(\cdot,e_{k})u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{div}\,(e_{k})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\partial_{k}g^{ij})(D_{i}u_{\varepsilon},D_{j}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\end{split}

where
R(\cdot,\,e_{k})
is the Riemann curvature tensor of
M
.
The left hand side is controlled by

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{\eta}}(\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon},D_{k}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq\left(\int_{B_{\eta}}|\log\varepsilon|\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{B_{\eta}}\frac{|D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}{|\log\varepsilon|}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)^{1/2},\end{split}

and thus,

(5.95)

\begin{split}&\left(\int_{B_{\eta}}|\log\varepsilon|\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)\left(\int_{B_{\eta}}\frac{|D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}}{|\log\varepsilon|}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right)\\
&\geq\left|\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}|Du_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}(\nu,e_{k})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(D_{k}u_{\varepsilon},D_{\nu}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\right.\\
&\left.+\int_{B_{\eta}}(Du_{\varepsilon},R(\cdot,e_{k})u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{div}\,(e_{k})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\partial_{k}g^{ij})(D_{i}u_{\varepsilon},D_{j}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right|^{2}.\end{split}

Thanks to (
4.7
) (see Lemma
4.2
) we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{\eta}}|D_{k}u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|}\int_{B_{\eta}(a_{j})}(Du_{\varepsilon},e_{k})_{g}^{2}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\pi|e_{k}(a_{j})|^{2}_{g}=\pi,

since
\left\{e_{1},\,e_{2}\right\}
are orthonormal in
a_{j}(t)
.
Corollary
4.5
implies that, up to extraction of a subsequence,

Du_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly in
L^{p}(M)
for any
p\in[1,2)

and
u_{\varepsilon}
converges strongly in
L^{q}(M)
for any
q\in[1,+\infty)

(except, possibly, for a negligible set of times
t
). As a consequence, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{B_{\eta}}(Du_{\varepsilon},R(\cdot,e_{k})u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{B_{\eta}}(Du^{*},R(\cdot,e_{k})u^{*})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

On the other hand, Lemma
4.9
implies that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}=0

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}|Du_{\varepsilon}|^{2}_{g}(\nu,e_{k})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(D_{k}u_{\varepsilon},D_{\nu}u_{\varepsilon})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}|Du^{*}|_{g}^{2}(\nu,e_{k})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(D_{k}u^{*},D_{\nu}u^{*})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

for almost every
\eta
.
Finally, we concentrate on the last two terms in the right hand side of (
5.95
).
First of all, we observe that both terms are controlled by quantities of the form

\int_{B_{\eta}}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})f\,\mathrm{vol}_{g},

where
e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}):=\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\left(\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}^{2}-1\right)^{2}
is the density of the Ginzburg Landau energy and
f:B_{\eta}(p)\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function such that
f(p)=0
(in the first term
f=\mathrm{div}\,(e_{k})
and in the second
f=\partial_{k}g^{ij}
, see (
2.40
) e (
2.41
)).
We show that

(5.96)

\lim_{\eta\to 0}\,\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\int_{B_{\eta}}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})f\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=0.

To this end, we dyadically decompose
B_{\eta}
to obtain that

\int_{B_{\eta}}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})f\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\int_{B_{2^{-j}\eta}\setminus B_{2^{-j-1}\eta}}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})f\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

On the one hand the smoothness of
f
and the fact that
f(a_{j})=0
imply
\left|f\right|\leq\eta 2^{-j}
on each anulus
B_{2^{-j}\eta}\setminus B_{2^{-j-1}\eta}
.
On the other hand, Lemma
4.3
imply that, for some constant
c
independent of
\eta
and of
\varepsilon
,

\int_{B_{2^{-j}\eta}\setminus B_{2^{-j-1}\eta}}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq c\left(\left|\log\left(\eta 2^{-j-1}\right)\right|+1\right).

Therefore,

\left|\int_{B_{\eta}}e_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})f\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\right|\leq c\eta\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}\left(\left|\log\left(\eta 2^{-j-1}\right)+1\right|\right).

Since the series in the right hand side converges, (
5.96
) follows.

Therefore, taking the
\liminf
in (
5.95
) produces

(5.97)

\begin{split}&\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|\log\varepsilon|}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}|Du^{*}|^{2}_{g}(\nu,e_{k})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}}(D_{e_{k}}u^{*},D_{\nu}u^{*})_{g}\right.\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\left.\int_{B_{\eta}}(Du^{*},R(\cdot,e_{k})u^{*})_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+o_{\eta\to 0}(1)\right|^{2}\end{split}

Recall that the canonical harmonic vector field
u^{*}\in W^{1,p}_{\tang}(M)\cap L^{\infty}_{\tang}(M)
for
p\in[1,2)
and the curvature tensor is smooth. Therefore, as
\eta\to 0
, we have that

\int_{B_{\eta}}(Du^{*},R(\cdot,e_{k})u^{*})_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\to 0.

Thanks to Proposition
3.4

we have

\begin{split}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|\log\varepsilon|}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi),\,{\hat{\mathbf{e}}}_{k}\right)_{g}\right|^{2}+\mathrm{o}_{\eta\to 0}(1)\end{split}

As
\{\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\,\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\}
is an arbitrary orthonormal basis
for
\mathrm{T}_{a_{j}}(M)
, we also deduce

\begin{split}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|\log\varepsilon|}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\left(\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi),\,{\hat{\mathbf{e}}}\right)_{g}\right|^{2}+\mathrm{o}_{\eta\to 0}(1)\end{split}

for any unit vector
\hat{\mathbf{e}}\in\mathrm{T}_{a_{j}}(M)
. By taking the
supremum over
\hat{\mathbf{e}}
, we obtain

\begin{split}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{|\log\varepsilon|}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}}\left|-\Delta_{g}u_{\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|u_{\varepsilon}|_{g}^{2}-1)u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\left|\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)\right|_{g}^{2}+\mathrm{o}_{\eta\to 0}(1)\end{split}

By taking the sum over
j
, the proposition follows.
∎

We can finally prove our main result, Theorem
1
,
which is an immediate consequence of the following proposition.
We recall that the constant
\gamma>0
is the core energy,
implicitely defined in Equation (
1.7
)
(see
[
19
, Section 2.3]
for more details).

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}
be a solution of (
1.4
) with
u_{\varepsilon}^{0}
satisfying
(
5.63
), (
5.64
) and (
5.65
).
Let
\mathbf{a}\colon[0,\,T^{*})\to M^{n}
,
\xi\in H^{1}(0,T^{*};\mathrm{Harm}^{1}(M))
be
as in Proposition
5.7
. Then, the following statements hold.

(i)

For any
t\in(0,T^{*})
,
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
is a recovery sequence for
W
, that is

\displaystyle\omega(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}(t)}\qquad\textrm{in }W^{-1,p}(M)\textrm{ for any }pin[1,\,2)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+W(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d},\xi(t))+n\gamma+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)

(ii)

For any
t\in(0,T^{*})
,

\mathbb{P}j(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\xi(t)\qquad\text{ and }\quad\xi(t)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d})

(iii)

The vortex curve
\mathbf{a}
satisfies for any
t\in(0,T^{*})

\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\right|_{g}^{2}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(s),\mathbf{d},\xi(s))\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{d}s+W(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathbf{d},\xi(t))=W(\mathbf{a}^{0},\mathbf{d},\xi_{0})

Equivalently,
\mathbf{a}
is a solution of the gradient flow of the renormalized energy

(5.98)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{a}(t)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(t),\mathrm{d}(t),\xi(t))\,\,\,\,\textrm{for any }t\in(0,\,T^{*}),\\
\\
\mathbf{a}(0)=\mathbf{a}^{0}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The proof is based on the abstract scheme developed by Sandier & Serfaty (see
[
35
, Theorem 1.4]
).

Equation (
1.4
) is the
L^{2}
-gradient flow of the Ginzburg-Landau energy
F_{\varepsilon}
.
More precisely, for any
\varepsilon>0
and for any
v\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
we let

\displaystyle E_{\varepsilon}(v):=\begin{cases}F_{\varepsilon}(v)-\pi n|\log\varepsilon|-n\gamma,\,\,\,\,\,\,&\hbox{ if }Dv\in L^{2}_{\tang}(M),\,\,\,\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(1-|v|^{2})^{2}\in L^{1}(M)\\
\\
+\infty\,\,\,\,\,&\hbox{otherwise in }L^{2}_{\tang}(M).\end{cases}

Recall that
E_{\varepsilon}

\Gamma
-converges to
W
and therefore, for any
t\in[0,T)^{*}
,

(5.99)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\geq W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d}(t),\,\xi(t)).

We set
X_{\varepsilon}:=L^{2}_{\tang}(M)
. We endow
X_{\varepsilon}
with the norm
\left\|v\right\|_{X_{\varepsilon}}:=\frac{1}{|\log\varepsilon|^{1/2}}\left\|v\right\|_{L^{2}(M)}
.
It is easy to check that the subdifferential of
E_{\varepsilon}
(with respect to the scalar product in
X_{\varepsilon}
) is singlevalued and is given by

\partial_{X_{\varepsilon}}E_{\varepsilon}(v)=\left|\log\varepsilon\right|\left(-\Delta v+\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}(|v|_{g}^{2}-1)v\right).

Therefore the Ginzburg-Landau evolution (
1.4
) rewrites as the gradient flow

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}(t)+\partial_{X_{\varepsilon}}E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=0\\
\\
u_{\varepsilon}(0)=u_{\varepsilon}^{0}.\end{cases}

Equivalently,
u_{\varepsilon}
is a
curve of maximal slope
for the energy
E_{\varepsilon}
with respect to the slope
\|\partial_{X_{\varepsilon}}E_{\varepsilon}\|_{X_{\varepsilon}}
and thus it satisfies

(5.100)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{X_{\varepsilon}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial_{X_{\varepsilon}}E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(s))\right\|^{2}_{X_{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}s+E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))=E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{0})

for any
t\in[0,\,T^{*})
.
Thanks to Proposition
5.7
(see in particular (
5.72
))
we have

(5.101)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}(s)\right\|^{2}_{X_{\varepsilon}}\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\right|^{2}_{g}(s)\mathrm{d}s.

On the other hand, Proposition
5.8
and Fatou’s Lemma imply

(5.102)

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\partial_{E_{\varepsilon}}u_{\varepsilon}(s)\right\|_{X_{\varepsilon}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(s),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(s))\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

Therefore, for any
t\in[0,T^{*})
we obtain

(5.103)

\begin{split}E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{0})-E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))&\geq\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\right|^{2}_{g}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(s),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(s))\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\\
&\geq\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}a_{j}}{\mathrm{d}t},-\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)\right)_{g}\mathrm{d}s+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\\
&=W(\mathbf{a}(0),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)-W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1).\end{split}

On the other hand, since the initial conditions are well prepared according to
(
5.63
), (
5.64
) and we have the
\Gamma
-convergence of
E_{\varepsilon}
along
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
(see (
5.99
)),
we have

E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{0})-E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))\leq W(\mathbf{a}(0),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)-W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1).

Therefore, the inequalities in (
5.103
) become equalities.
In particular, we obtain

\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\right|^{2}_{g}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(s),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(s))\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))=W(\mathbf{a}(0),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi),

that is (
iii
).
Moreover, we also obtain that

E_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}(t))=W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))+\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(1)\qquad\textrm{for any }t\in[0,T^{*}),

namely that
u_{\varepsilon}
remains well prepared along the evolution, or, in other words, that
u_{\varepsilon}(t)
is a recovery sequence for the renormalized energy
W
for any
t\in(0,T^{*})
.

Statement (ii) is already contained in
Proposition
5.7
.
Finally, the fact that (
iii
) is indeed equivalent to the differential formulation of the gradient flow (
5.98
) is standard. More precisely,
since the chain rule implies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}a_{j}}{\mathrm{d}t},-\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)\right)_{g}\mathrm{d}s=W(\mathbf{a}(0),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)-W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t)),

we obtain that for any
t\in(0,T^{*})
there holds

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\right|^{2}_{g}(s)\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{t}\left|\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}W(\mathbf{a}(s),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(s))\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}a_{j}}{\mathrm{d}t},-\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a},\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi)\right)_{g}\mathrm{d}s,

which means

\int_{0}^{t}\left|\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{a}(s)+\frac{1}{\pi}\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a}(s),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(s))\right|_{g}^{2}\mathrm{d}s=0,

and thus

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{a}(t)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\nabla_{a_{j}}W(\mathbf{a}(t),\,\mathbf{d},\,\xi(t))\qquad\text{ for any }\quad t\in(0,T^{*}).\qed

Conflict of interest

The authors declare that they have no conflict of interest.

Appendix A
Integration by parts

We collect some integration by parts results needed in our argument.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
(M,\,g)
be a compact two dimensional Riemannian manifold.
Let
v
be a smooth vector field. Then, for any smooth

\psi\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
there holds

(A.1)

\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}v,\,iv)_{g}\,\psi\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=-\int_{M}\psi\,\mathrm{d}^{*}j(v).

Proof.

We integrate by parts, and apply (
2.27
)

(A.2)

\begin{split}-\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}v,\,iv)_{g}\,\psi\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\int_{M}(Dv,\,D(\psi\,iv))_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&=\int_{M}(Dv,\,iv\otimes\mathrm{d}\psi)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\int_{M}(Dv,\,D(iv))_{g}\,\psi\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\end{split}

Here,
iv\otimes\mathrm{d}\psi
is the (fibre-wise linear) operator

TM\to TM
that maps a vector field
w
to the vector field
iv\,\mathrm{d}\psi(w)
.
We claim that

(A.3)

(Dv,\,D(iv))_{g}=0\qquad\textrm{at any point of }M.

Indeed, let
\left\{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right\}
be a
moving frame on an open set
U\subseteq M
.
There holds that
\tau_{2}=i\tau_{1}
and thus

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=v^{1}\tau_{1}+iv^{2}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle iv

\displaystyle=-v^{2}\tau_{1}+v^{1}\tau_{2}.

Therefore (see Section
2.2.2
in this paper and
[
19
, Section 5.2]
) for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle D_{\tau_{j}}(iv)

\displaystyle=-D_{\tau_{j}}(v^{2}\tau_{1})+D_{\tau_{j}}(v^{1}\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=-(\mathrm{d}v^{2}(\tau_{j})-\mathcal{A}(\tau_{j})v^{2})\tau_{1}+(\mathrm{d}v^{1}(\tau_{j})-\mathcal{A}(\tau_{j})v^{1})\tau_{2}

\displaystyle=iD_{\tau_{j}}v\,\,\,\,\,\,\hbox{ in }U,

from which (
A.3
) follows.

Now, we consider
(Dv,\,iv\otimes\mathrm{d}\psi)_{g}
.
In terms of the orthonormal frame
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}
,
we have

(Dv,\,iv\otimes\mathrm{d}\psi)_{g}=(D_{\tau_{1}}v,\,iv)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\psi(\tau_{1})+(D_{\tau_{2}}v,\,iv)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\psi(\tau_{2})

Recalling the definition of
j(v)
, Equation
2.62
,
we can write

(Dv,\,iv\otimes\mathrm{d}\psi)_{g}=j(v)(\tau_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\psi(\tau_{1})+j(v)(\tau_{2})\,\mathrm{d}\psi(\tau_{2})=(j(v),\,\mathrm{d}\psi)_{g}

Therefore,

-\int_{M}(\Delta_{g}v,\,iv)_{g}\,\psi\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{M}(j(v),\,\mathrm{d}\psi)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:in_parts_form}}}{{=}}\int_{M}\mathrm{d}^{*}j(v)\,\psi\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\qed

Lemma A.2
.

Let
p\in M
and let
u
,
v
be smooth vector
fields defined in a neighbourhood of
p
. Then, for any
\varepsilon>0

and any
\eta>0
small enough, there holds

(A.4)

\begin{split}&\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}((|u|^{2}_{g}-1)u,\,D_{v}u)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad\qquad=-\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(|u|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{div}\,(v)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(p)}(|u|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}\,(\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1},\end{split}

where
\nu
is the outer normal to
\partial B_{\eta}
.

Proof.

Thanks to the compatibility of the covariant
derivative with the metric, we have

(A.5)

(\nabla\left|u\right|^{2}_{g},\,v)_{g}=2(u,\,D_{v}u)_{g}

Therefore,

((\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)u,\,D_{v}u)_{g}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\left|u\right|^{2}_{g},\,(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)v)_{g}=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1),\,(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)v)_{g}

We integrate by parts, by applying (
2.25
). We obtain

\begin{split}&\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}((\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)u,\,D_{v}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}\,\mathrm{div}\,(v)\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)\,(\nabla\left|u\right|^{2}_{g},\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(p)}(\left|u\right|^{2}_{g}-1)^{2}\,(\nu,\,v)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

Taking (
A.5
) into account, we obtain (
A.4
).
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
u
be a smooth vector field. Let
\{x^{1},\,x^{2}\}

be a local coordinate system, defined in a neighbourhood
of a point
p\in M
.
We let
e_{k}:=\partial/\partial x^{k}
.
Then, for
k=1,\,2
, we have

(A.6)

\begin{split}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(-\Delta u,\,D_{k}u)_{g}\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(p)}|Du|^{2}_{g}\,(\nu,\,e_{k})_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(p)}(D_{\nu}u,\,\mathrm{D}_{k}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\\
&+\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(Du,\,R(\cdot,\,e_{k})u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{div}\,(e_{k})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\partial_{k}g^{ij})(D_{i}u,D_{j}u)_{d}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g},\end{split}

where
R
is the Riemann curvature tensor.

Proof.

Recall that
\Delta u=-D^{*}Du
and thus

\begin{split}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(-\Delta u,\,D_{k}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(D^{*}Du,\,D_{k}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&=\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(Du,\,DD_{k}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(p)}(D_{\nu}u,\,D_{k}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}.\end{split}

Now we work on the first term in the right hand side. Recalling (
2.2
) and the symmetry
of the metric we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{k}|Du|^{2}_{g}=g^{ij}(D_{i}u,D_{k}D_{j}u)_{g}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{k}g^{ij}(D_{i}u,D_{j}u)_{g}

Then, the very definition of curvature tensor gives

D_{j}D_{k}u=D_{k}D_{j}u+R(e_{j},\,e_{k})u,

and thus

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{k}|Du|^{2}_{g}=g^{ij}(D_{i}u,D_{j}D_{k}u)_{g}+\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{k}g^{ij})(D_{i}u,D_{j}u)_{g}-g^{ij}(D_{i}u,R(e_{j},e_{k})u)_{g}.

Therefore

\begin{split}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(Du,\,DD_{e_{k}}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}\partial_{k}|Du|^{2}_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\partial_{k}g^{ij})(D_{i}u,D_{j}u)_{d}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&+\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}g^{ij}(D_{i}u,\,R(e_{j},\,e_{k})u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}.\end{split}

Then we
integrate by parts in the first integral in the right hand side
(see (
2.25
)).
We get

\begin{split}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(Du,\,DD_{e_{k}}u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial B_{\eta}(p)}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}(\nu,e_{k})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}\left|Du\right|^{2}_{g}\mathrm{div}\,(e_{k})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}(\partial_{k}g^{ij})(D_{i}u,D_{j}u)_{d}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}+\int_{B_{\eta}(p)}g^{ij}(D_{i}u,\,R(e_{j},\,e_{k})u)_{g}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}\end{split}

and the lemma follows.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Lemma
4.4

The goal of this secton is to prove Lemma
4.4
.
For convenience, we reproduce the statement of the lemma here.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}_{\tang}(M)
be a sequence of vector fields
that satisfies

(B.1)

\begin{split}&\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\to 2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}\qquad\textrm{in }W^{-1,1}(M)\quad\textrm{as }\varepsilon\to 0\end{split}

where
a_{1}
, …,
a_{n}
are distinct points in
M
and
d_{1}
,…
d_{n}

are non-zero integers, and

(B.2)

\displaystyle\left\|u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C_{0}

(B.3)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}\leq\pi n\left|\log\varepsilon\right|+C_{0}

for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C_{0}
.
Let
\rho>0
be small enough, so that the closed balls
\bar{B}_{\rho}(a_{j})

are pairwise disjoint. Given an index
j\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,n\}
,
we assume that there exist a constant
C
such that

(B.4)

\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\lesssim\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{\rho}

for any
\varepsilon
small enough. Then, for any
\varepsilon

small enough we have
\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|_{g}\geq 1/2

on
\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j})
and

\ind(u_{\varepsilon},\,\partial B_{\rho}(a_{j}))=d_{j}

If we knew that the sequence of measures
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})

has uniformly bounded mass, then we could extract a subsequence
such that
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})\rightharpoonup^{*}2\pi\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\delta_{a_{j}}

weakly
∗
in the sense of measures, as well as in
W^{-1,1}(M)
.
As a consequence, we would deduce
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})(B_{\rho}(a_{j}))\to 2\pi d_{j}

for a.e. radius
\rho
, and the lemma would follow
by the properties of
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})
(see Section
2.5
).
Unfortunately, though, the assumptions (
B.1
),
(
B.2
),
(
B.3
) do
not
imply that the
measures
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})
have uniformly bounded mass.
This makes the proof of Lemma
B.1
more technical.
The strategy we adopt is quite classical in the theory of Ginzburg-Landau
functionals (see, for instance,
[
1
]
): first,
we construct a suitable partition of the domain (a ‘grid’); then,
we use this partition to construct a sequence of measures
that approximate
\omega(u_{\varepsilon})
in
W^{-1,1}
and have uniformly bounded mass.
This enable us to pass to the limit in the sense of measures.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

As a preliminary remark, we observe that the
assumptions (
B.1
), (
B.2
),
(
B.3
) and the
estimate (
4.6
) in
Lemma
4.2
imply

(B.5)

\int_{B_{2\rho}(a_{j})\setminus B_{\rho}(a_{j})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left|Du_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|u_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}_{g})^{2}\right)\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\left|\log\varepsilon\right|)

We split the proof in several steps.

Step 1

(Reduction to the Euclidean setting)
.

Let
\{\tau_{1},\,\tau_{2}\}

be an orthonormal tangent frame on
B_{2\rho}(a_{j})
, whose associated
connection form
\mathcal{A}
satisfies (
2.45
).
By working in geodesic normal coordinates, we can represent

u_{\varepsilon}
by means of a Euclidean vector
field
v_{\varepsilon}\colon B_{2\rho}(0)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, as in (
2.46
).
Equation (
2.50
) implies

(B.6)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2\rho}(0)}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hp:H1bounds-energyapp}}}{{\leq}}C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|

(B.7)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2\rho}(0)\setminus B_{\rho}(0)}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ubH1-0}}}{{=}}\mathrm{o}_{\varepsilon\to 0}(\left|\log\varepsilon\right|)

(B.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{\rho}(0)}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:upper_bound_bdapp}}}{{\leq}}\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{\rho}

where

\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}):=\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla v_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon^{2}}(1-\left|v_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2})^{2}

is the Euclidean Ginzburg-Landau energy density.
Let
\Phi\colon B_{2\rho}(a_{j})\to B_{2\rho}(0)
be the coordinate map.
The Euclidean vorticity of
v_{\varepsilon}
, i.e. the
2
-form
\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v_{\varepsilon})

(where
\bar{\jmath}
is defined by (
5.7
)), satisfies

\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v_{\varepsilon})=(\Phi^{-1})^{*}\omega(u_{\varepsilon})-\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}\qquad\textrm{where }\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}:=\left(1-\left|v_{\varepsilon}\right|^{2}\right)(\Phi^{-1})^{*}\mathcal{A},

because of Lemma
5.1
. The energy
estimate (
B.6
) implies
\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}\to 0

strongly in
L^{2}(B_{2\rho}(0))
as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
hence
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{R}_{\varepsilon}\to 0
strongly in
W^{-1,2}(B_{2\rho}(0))
and

(B.9)

\mathrm{d}\bar{\jmath}(v_{\varepsilon})\to 2\pi d_{j}\delta_{0}\qquad\textrm{in }W^{-1,1}(B_{2\rho}(0))\quad\textrm{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

Step 2

(Construction of a suitable cover)
.

From now on, we denote by
C
a generic positive constant,
that may change from line to line; it may depend on
\rho

(which is fixed), but does not depend on
\varepsilon
. Let

h(\varepsilon):=\left|\log\varepsilon\right|^{-2}

We construct a finite collection
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
of Lipschitz, closed sets
that satisfies the following properties:

(i)

\bar{B}_{2\rho}(0)=\bigcup_{K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}}K

and the interiors of
K
are pairwise disjoint;

(ii)

for any
K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, there exists
an invertible, Lipschitz map
T_{K}\colon K\to[-h(\varepsilon),\,h(\varepsilon)]^{2}
,
with Lipschitz inverse, such that

\left\|\nabla T_{K}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}+\left\|\nabla(T^{-1}_{K})\right\|_{L^{\infty}([-h(\varepsilon),\,h(\varepsilon)]^{2})}\leq C

for some constant
C
that does not depend on
\varepsilon
,
K
;

(iii)

for
R_{\varepsilon}:=\cup_{K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}}\partial K
,
there holds
\mathscr{H}^{1}(R_{\varepsilon})\leq Ch(\varepsilon)^{-1}
and

(B.10)

\int_{R_{\varepsilon}}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{h(\varepsilon)}

for some
\varepsilon
-independent constant
C
;

(iv)

for any
K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, either

\mathrm{interior}(K)\subseteq B_{2\rho}(0)\setminus\bar{B}_{\rho}(0)\qquad\textrm{or}\qquad\mathrm{interior}(K)\subseteq B_{\rho}(0).

First, we define a suitable cover of
B_{\rho}(0)
.
Let
T\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
T(x):=\max(\left|x^{1}\right|,\,\left|x^{2}\right|)x/\left|x\right|
.
The map
T
is Lipschitz, invertible, has Lipschitz inverse, and maps
the square
[-\rho,\,\rho]^{2}
to the disk
\bar{B}_{\rho}(0)
.
Let
N_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
be the integer part of
2\rho/h(\varepsilon)
.
By Fubini theorem, we can select suitable points

a_{\varepsilon}^{0}=-\rho<a_{\varepsilon}^{1}<\ldots<a_{\varepsilon}^{N_{\varepsilon}}=\rho

such that, for any
k\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}-1\}
,

(B.11)

\displaystyle\left|a_{\varepsilon}^{k}-\left(-\rho+\frac{2\rho k}{N_{\varepsilon}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{\rho}{2N_{\varepsilon}}

(B.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon}^{k}\}\times[-\rho,\,\rho]}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\circ T)\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{N_{\varepsilon}}{\rho}\int_{[-\rho,\,\rho]^{2}}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\circ T)\,\mathrm{d}x

We observe that
2\rho/N_{\varepsilon}\sim h(\varepsilon)
as
\varepsilon\to 0

and hence, (
B.12
) implies

(B.13)

\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon}^{k}\}\times[-\rho,\,\rho]}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\circ T)\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{C}{h(\varepsilon)}\int_{B_{\rho}(0)}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ubH11-1}}}{{\leq}}\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{h(\varepsilon)}

In fact, (
B.13
) remains valid for
k=0
and
k=N_{\varepsilon}
,
thanks to (
B.8
). In a similar way, we find points

b_{\varepsilon}^{0}=-\rho<b_{\varepsilon}^{1}<\ldots<b_{\varepsilon}^{N_{\varepsilon}}=\rho

such that, for any
h\in\{1,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}-1\}
,

(B.14)

\displaystyle\left|b_{\varepsilon}^{h}-\left(-\rho+\frac{2\rho h}{N_{\varepsilon}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{\rho}{2N_{\varepsilon}}

(B.15)

\displaystyle\int_{[-\rho,\,\rho]\times\{b_{\varepsilon}^{h}\}}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\circ T)\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{N_{\varepsilon}}{\rho}\int_{[-\rho,\,\rho]^{2}}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon}\circ T)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{h(\varepsilon)}

We consider the collection of sets
K_{k,h}:=T([a^{k}_{\varepsilon},\,a^{k+1}_{\varepsilon}]\times[b^{h}_{\varepsilon},\,b^{h+1}_{\varepsilon}])
, for
k\in\{0,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}-1\}

and
h\in\{0,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}-1\}
. These sets cover
B_{\rho}(0)
,
have pairwise disjoint interiors, satisfy the condition (ii) above
(thanks to (
B.11
), (
B.14
)) and

\int_{\bigcup_{k,h}\partial T_{k,h}}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\leq\frac{C\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}{h(\varepsilon)}

We construct a cover of
B_{2\rho}(0)\setminus\bar{B}_{\rho}(0)

by considering suitable points

\rho_{\varepsilon}^{0}=\rho<\rho_{\varepsilon}^{1}<\ldots<\rho^{N_{\varepsilon}}=2\rho,\qquad\theta_{\varepsilon}^{0}=0<\theta_{\varepsilon}^{1}<\ldots<\rho^{N_{\varepsilon}}=2\pi

and defining, for any
k\in\{0,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}-1\}
,

h\in\{0,\,\ldots,\,N_{\varepsilon}-1\}
,

\tilde{K}_{k,h}:=\left\{(\rho\cos\theta,\,\rho\sin\theta)\colon(\rho,\,\theta)\in[\rho^{k}_{\varepsilon},\,\rho^{k+1}_{\varepsilon}]\times[\theta^{h}_{\varepsilon},\,\theta^{h+1}_{\varepsilon}]\right\}

For a suitable choice of
\rho_{\varepsilon}^{k}
and
\theta_{\varepsilon}^{h}

(analogous to (
B.11
), (
B.12
)), we can make sure that
the collection
\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}:=\{K_{k,h},\,\tilde{K}_{k,h}\}_{k,h}

satisfies all the properties (i)–(v) above.

Step 3

(Construction of a suitable measure)
.

An argument based on Sobolev embeddings (e.g.,

[
19
, Lemma A.1]
)
shows that

(B.16)

\sup_{x\in R_{\varepsilon}}\left|1-\left|v_{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\left(\int_{R_{\varepsilon}}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\right)^{1/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ubH12-1}}}{{\leq}}\frac{C\varepsilon^{1/2}}{h(\varepsilon)^{1/2}}\left|\log\varepsilon\right|^{1/2}\to 0

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. In particular, for any
K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, the
projection
v_{\varepsilon}/\left|v_{\varepsilon}\right|\colon\partial K\to\mathbb{S}^{1}

is well-defined and continuous. We denote by
\deg(v_{\varepsilon},\,\partial K)

the topological degree of
v_{\varepsilon}/\left|v_{\varepsilon}\right|
on
\partial K
.
We define the measure

(B.17)

\mu_{\varepsilon}:=2\pi\sum_{K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}}\deg(v_{\varepsilon},\,\partial K)\,\delta_{b_{K}},

where
b_{K}
is an arbitrary point in the interior of
K
.
Let
\delta>0
be a small parameter. Classical results by
Sandier
[
33
]
and Jerrard
[
20
]

(see also
[
1
, Lemma 3.10]

for a statement that is particular suited to our needs) imply

(B.18)

\begin{split}&\left|\deg(v_{\varepsilon},\,\partial K)\right|\left(\log\frac{h(\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}-C_{\delta}\left(1+\log\left|\deg(v_{\varepsilon},\,\partial K)\right|\right)\right)\\
&\qquad\qquad\leq C\int_{K}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}x+C\delta h(\varepsilon)\int_{\partial K}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

for any
K\in\mathscr{G}_{\varepsilon}
, where
C_{\delta}
is a constant
that depends only on
\delta
and
\rho
. By dividing both
sides of (
B.18
) by
\left|\log\varepsilon\right|
, we deduce

(B.19)

\begin{split}&\left|\deg(v_{\varepsilon},\,\partial K)\right|\leq\frac{C}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{K}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{C\delta h(\varepsilon)}{\left|\log\varepsilon\right|}\int_{\partial K}\bar{e}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\varepsilon})\,\mathrm{d}\mathscr{H}^{1}\end{split}

By applying (
B.6
), (
B.7
) and (
B.10
), we deduce
